Learning Hierarchical Structure of Clusterable Graphs
Michael Kapralov, Akash Kumar, Silvio Lattanzi, Aida Mousavifar

Abstract
We consider the problem of learning hierarchical cluster structure of graphs in the seeded model, where besides the input graph the algorithm is provided with a small number of ‘seeds’, i.e. correctly clustered data points. In particular, we ask whether one can approximate the Dasgupta cost of a graph, a popular measure of hierarchical clusterability , in sublinear time and using a small number of seeds. Our main result is an O ⁡ ( log ⁡ k ) O(\sqrt{\log k}) approximation to Dasgupta cost of G G in ≈ poly ​ ( k ) ⋅ n 1 / 2 + O ⁡ ( ϵ ) \approx\text{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)} time using ≈ poly ​ ( k ) ⋅ n O ⁡ ( ϵ ) \approx\text{poly}(k)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)} seeds, effectively giving a sublinear time simulation of the algorithm of Charikar and Chatziafratis[SODA’17] on clusterable graphs. To the best of our knowledge, ours is the first result on approximating the hierarchical clustering properties of such graphs in sublinear time.



1
Introduction

Clustering graph data is a central problem in unsupervised learning. It has applications
in a wide variety of scientific disciplines from data mining to social science, statistics and more. The overall objective in these problems is to partition
the vertex set of the graph into disjoint “well connected” subgraphs which are sparsely connected to each
other. In
hierarchical clustering
the objective is to construct a hierarchy of partitions that explain the cluster structure of the graph at different scales. The quality of such a hierarchy is often evaluated using Dasgupta’s cost
[
Das16
]
.

In this paper we consider the problem of learning hierarchical cluster structure of graphs in the semi-supervised setting. In particular we adopt the widely used
seeded
model(see, e.g.,
[
BBM02
]
and numerous follow up works, e.g.,
[
DBE99
,
KBDM09
,
SK02
,
AB15
]
). In the seeded model of
[
BBM02
]
the task is to construct a good clustering of the input instance starting from a small number of ‘seeds’, i.e. correctly clustered data points. In the context of hierarchical graph clustering we ask

Is it possible to learn hierarchical clustering properties of the input graph using a small number of accesses to the input graph and a few seeds?

In this paper, we focus on the popular version of the problem where one assumes the existence of a planted solution, namely that the input graph
G=(V,E)
admits a partitioning into a disjoint union of
k
induced expanders
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
with outer conductance bounded by
\epsilon\ll 1
. We refer to such instances as
k
-clusterable
graphs. Such graphs have been the focus of significant attention in the property testing literature
[
CS07
,
KS08
,
NS10
]
, starting from the seminal work of
[
GR11
]
. We capture
hierarchical clustering
properties of the input graph by a partition of vertices of a graph into nested clusters, naturally represented by a rooted tree whose leaves correspond to the vertices and whose internal nodes
represent clusters of their descendant leaves, and measure the cost of such a tree using the well-established Dasgupta’s cost
[
Das16
]
. Thus, our problem is

Is it possible to approximate Dasgupta’s cost of a
(k,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph using few queries to the input graph and a few correctly clustered seed vertices?

We consider two models for the distribution of the seed vertices. In the first one, the
random sample
model, the algorithm gets access to correct cluster labels for a few vertices in the graph sampled uniformly a random. In the second one, the algorithm can ask for correct labels for any vertex of its choice. Our main result is Theorem
1
below:

Theorem 1
.

[Informal version of Theorem
3
]
There exists an algorithm that for every
(k,\Omega(1),\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
G=(V,E)
estimates the Dasgupta’s cost of
G
up to
O(\sqrt{\log k})
factor in the random sample model in
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
time and
\approx n^{O(\epsilon)}
seeds. Furthermore, in this model at least
\Omega(\sqrt{n/k})
time is needed.

The
oracle model
is stronger:

Theorem 2
.

[Informal version of Theorem
5
]
There exists an algorithm that for every
(k,\Omega(1),\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
G=(V,E)
estimates the Dasgupta’s cost of
G
up to
O(\sqrt{\log k})
factor in the oracle model
\approx k^{O(1)}
time and
\approx k^{O(1)}
seeds (i.e., oracle queries to the graph
G
).

We note that the currently best known approximation to Dasgupta’s cost on
n
-vertex graphs is
O(\sqrt{\log n})
, achieved by the recursive sparsest cut algorithm of
[
CC17
]
. Our approximation is
O(\sqrt{\log k})
, matching what the Charikar and Chatziafratis algorithm achieves on
k
-node graphs. In fact, our main technical contribution is an efficient way of simulating this algorithm in sublinear time on
k
-clusterable graphs.

Related work on
(k,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs.
Such graphs have been extensively studied in the property testing framework as well as local computation models. Its testing version, where one essentially wants to determine
k
, the number of clusters in
G
, in sublinear time, generalizes the well-studied problem of testing graph
expansion, where one wants to distinguish between an expander (i.e. a good single cluster) and a graph with a sparse cut (i.e. at least two clusters).
[
GR11
]
showed that expansion testing requires
\Omega(n^{1/2})
queries, then
[
CS07
,
KS08
,
NS10
]
developed algorithms to distinguish an expander from a graph that is far from a graph with conductance
\epsilon
in time
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
, which the recent work of
[
CKK+18
]
showed to be tight.
The setting of
k>2
has seen a lot of attention recently
[
CPS15
,
CKK+18
,
Pen20
,
GKL+21
]
, with close to information theoretically optimal clustering oracles, i.e. small space data structures that provide quick access to an approximate clustering, obtained in
[
GKL+21
]
. More recently,
[
MS21
]
studied hierarchical clustering of
k
-clusterable graphs and developed a nearly
linear time algorithm that approximates the Dasgupta’s cost of the graph up to constant factor. The above approach, unfortunately, does not work unless the cluster structure of the graph is very pronounced (i.e.,
\epsilon\ll 1/\text{poly}(k)
) and does not achieve the sublinear runtime. Our work estimates Dasgupta’s cost of
k
-clusterable graphs up to
O(\sqrt{\log k})
factor on hard instances in which
\epsilon\ll 1
(i.e., when the Dasgupta’s cost is dominated by the outgoing edges), and our algorithm runs in
sublinear time
.

Why existing clustering oracles do not suffice.
Of course, a good labelling of a random sample of vertices can be obtained by invoking a clustering oracle (e.g., the one of
[
GKL+21
]
). However, we note that while existing clustering oracles are guaranteed to misclassify at most
\approx\epsilon
fraction of nodes per cluster, such misclassification rates can in general dramatically affect Dasgupta cost. Moreover, existing sublinear clustering oracles work either under the assumption that the cluster structure of the graph is very pronounced (i.e.,
\epsilon\ll 1/\text{poly}(k,\log n)
e.g., in
[
CPS15
]
) or require time that is
exponential
in the number of clusters
k
(e.g.,
[
GKL+21
]
), and it is by no means clear how to improve this (the reason for the exponential dependence is the need to find candidate cluster centers, for which a search over a net in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is used).
Our submission gives a simple estimator with a
polynomial
dependence on
k
. An exciting direction left open by our work is to design clustering oracles that not only achieve low misclassification rate overall, but also respect hierarchical clustering structure of the input graph.

Related work on hierarchical clustering.

We briefly review developments in the area of algorithms for hierarchical clustering
since the introduction of Dasgupta’s objective function.
Dasgupta designed an algorithm based on recursive sparsest-cut that provides
O(\log^{3/2}n)

approximation for his objective function. This was improved by Charikar and Chatizafratis
who showed that the he recursive sparsest-cut algorithm already returns a tree with approximation
guarantee
O(\sqrt{\log n})

[
CC17
]
. Furthermore, they showed that it’s
impossible to approximate Dasgupta’s cost within constant factor in general graphs under
the Small-Set Expansion hypothesis.
More recently,
[
CAKMTM18
]
studied this problem in a regime in which the input graph is sampled from
a Hierarchical Stochastic Block Model
[
CAKMTM18
]
. They construct a tree in nearly
the linear time that approximates Dasgupta’s cost of the graph up to a constant factor.
[
CAKMTM18
]
use a type of hierarchical stochastic block model, which generates close to regular expanders with high probability, and their analysis crucially relies on having dense clusters and large degrees. Our model allows for arbitrary expanders as opposed to dense random graphs and is more expressive in this sense. To simplify the presentation we assume that the input graph has vertex degree bounded by
d
.

Related work in semi-supervised active clustering.

We note that our model is closely related to the semi-supervised active clustering framework (SSAC) introduced in
[
AKB16
]
. In this model we are given a set
X
of
n
points and an oracle answering to same-cluster queries of the form “are these two points in the same cluster?”. Thanks to its elegance and applications to crowdsourcing applications, the model received a lot of attention and has been extensively studied both in theory
[
ABJ18
,
ABJK18
,
BCBLP20
,
BCLP21
,
HMMP19
,
MP17
,
MS17a
,
MS17b
,
SS19
,
VRG19
]
and in practice
[
FGSS18
,
GNK+15
,
VGM15
,
VGMP17
]
— see also
[
EZK18
]
for other types of queries. It is interesting to note that the strong oracle model that we study in this paper can simulate the same-cluster oracle and vice-versa using roughly the same number of queries. From this perspective, our work is the first result approximating properties of hierarchical clustering in sublinear time.

1.1
Formal Problem Statement and Main Results

In this paper we will consider
d
-regular graphs.
1
1

1

Note that if the input graph is bounded degree, it can be turned into a
d
-regular graph by adding an appropriate number of self-loops; this of course changes the notion of conductances somewhat.
We parameterize the cluster structure of the graph using the notions of inner and outer conductance, defined below:

Definition 1

(
Inner and outer conductance
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph. For a set
C\subseteq V
and a set
S\subseteq C
, let
E(S,C\setminus S)
be the set of edges with one endpoint in
S
and the other in
C\setminus S
. The conductance of
S
within
C
, is
\phi^{G}_{C}(S)=\frac{|E(S,C\setminus S)|}{d\cdot|S|}
. The outer conductance of
C
is defined to be

\phi^{G}_{\text{out}}(C)=\phi_{V}^{G}(C)=\frac{|E(C,V\setminus C)|}{d\cdot|C|}\text{.}
The inner conductance of
C\subseteq V
is defined to be

\phi_{\text{in}}^{G}(C)=\min_{S\subseteq C\text{,}0<|S|\leq\frac{|C|}{2}}\phi^{G}_{C}(S)

if
|C|>1
and one otherwise.

We define
k
-
clusterable
graphs as a class of instances that can be partitioned into
k
expanders with small outer conductance:

Definition 2
.

(
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
)
Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph. A
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering of
G
is a partition of vertices
V
into disjoint subsets
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
such that for all
i\in[k]
,
\phi_{\text{in}}^{G}(C_{i})\geq\varphi
,
\phi_{\text{out}}^{G}(C_{i})\leq\epsilon
(Definition
1
).
Graph
G
is called
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable if there exists a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering for
G
.

Estimating Dasgupta’s Cost of Clusterable Graphs:

Hierarchical clustering is the task of partitioning vertices of a graph into nested clusters.
The nested partitions can be represented by a rooted tree whose leaves correspond to the vertices
of graph, and whose internal nodes represents the clusters of its descendant leaves. Dasgupta
introduced a natural optimization framework for formulating hierarchical clustering tasks as an
optimization problem
[
Das16
]
. We recall this framework now. Let
T
be any rooted tree whose leaves are vertices of the graph. For any node
x
of
T
, let
T[x]
be the subtree rooted at
x
, and let
\textsc{leaves}(T[x])\subseteq V
denote the leaves of this subtree. For leaves
x,y\in V
, let
\textsc{LCA}(x,y)
denote the lowest common ancestor of
x
and
y
in
T
. In other words,
T[\textsc{LCA}(x,y)]
is the smallest subtree whose leaves contain both
x
and
y
.

Definition 3
.

(
Dasgupta’s cost
[
Das16
]
)

Dasgupta’s cost of the tree
T
for the graph
G=(V,E)
is defined to be

\text{COST}_{G}(T)=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in E}|\textsc{leaves}(T[\textsc{LCA}(x,y)])|\text{.}

Note that the cost of an edge depends on the size of the lowest common ancestor between the
endpoints of that edge. Therefore, intuitively if a cluster is well-connected, we need to cut
its edges lower in the tree to optimize the cost of the solution. Since the clusters
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
are
\varphi
-expanders, they contribute a fixed, up to a
O(1/\varphi)
factor, amount to Dasgupta’s cost of the entire graph
G
. As a consequence, our problem is the most interesting when Dasgupta’s cost is dominated by the edges going between the clusters, and the task of approximating Dasgupta’s cost amounts to learning the structure of these edges.
2
2

2

One can easily show that the Dasgupta cost of a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph is at least
\Omega(\frac{\varphi\cdot dn^{2}}{k})
, simply because of the contribution of the
k
induced
\varphi
-expanders. On the other hand, the total number of edges running between the clusters is bounded by
\epsilon dn
, and therefore their total contribution to Dasgupta’s cost is
O(\epsilon\cdot dn^{2})
. Therefore, the problem of estimating Dasgupta’s cost becomes non-trivial only when
\epsilon\gg\frac{1}{k}
, i.e., when Dasgupta’s cost of the graph is dominated by the outgoing
edges between different clusters.
Thus, a natural approach is to contract the clusters
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
into supernodes, and use Dasgupta’s cost of the contracted graph as a proxy for Dasgupta’s cost of
G
. The problem is of course that it is not clear how to get access to this contracted graph in sublinear time, and our main contribution is a way of doing so. We start with

Definition 4
.

(
Contracted graph
)

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
denote a partition of
V
into disjoint subsets. We say that the weighted graph
H=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},W,w\right)
is a contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
if for every
i,j\in[k]
we have
W(i,j)=|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
, and for every
i\in[k]
we have
w(i)=|C_{i}|
.
We denote the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
by
H=G/\mathcal{C}
.

Our first algorithmic result is Theorem
3
(formal version of Theorem
1
).
It postulates a sublinear time algorithm for estimating Dasgupta’s cost of
k
-clusterable graphs.
This (sublinear time) algorithm is facilitated by assuming we have access to
G
in the
random sample model

defined below.

Definition 5

(
The
RandomSampleModel
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. In the
RandomSampleModel
, we assume that there exists an oracle that gives us a (multi)set
S
of i.i.d random vertices (seeds) together with their cluster label, i.e., for every vertex
x\in S
, a label
i\in[k]
such that
C_{i}\ni x
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
are smaller than a
positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
.
Then in the
RandomSampleModel
, procedure
\textsc{InitializeCostOracle}(G)
(Algorithm
5
) computes in time
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
using
n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
number of seed queries, a sublinear space data structure
\mathcal{D}
such that with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
the following
property is satisfied:

\textsc{EstimatedCost}(G,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm

3
) with probability at least
1-2\cdot n^{-100}
estimates the optimum Dasgupta cost of
G
within a
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{5}}\right)
factor in time

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.

An outline of the main ideas that go into the proof is given in Section
2
, and the algorithm together with its analysis are presented in Section
4
. Our approach here amounts to first obtaining access to the quadratic form of the Laplacian of the contracted graph
H
, and then using the hierarchical clustering algorithm of
[
CC17
]

on the corresponding approximation to the contracted graph. Thus, we essentially show how to simulate the algorithm of
[
CC17
]
in sublinear time on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs.

We also show that in
RandomSampleModel
(Definition
5
), essentially
\Omega(\sqrt{n/k})

queries are unavoidable if one seeks good approximations to Dasgupta Cost on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable
graphs. This is the content of the following theorem (which is proved in Section
5
).

Theorem 4
.

Fix
d\geq 8
. Then there exists
\varphi=\varphi(d),k=k(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
and

\frac{1}{100\log k}\leq\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
such that for any algorithm that on input a

(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
G
outputs a
o\left(\frac{k}{\log k}\right)

approximation to
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
with probability at least
2/3
necessarily
requires at least
\Omega(\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{k}})

queries in the
RandomSampleModel
.

We show how one can bypass these lower bounds with a stronger oracle. In particular,
Theorem
5
(formal version of Theorem
2
)
asserts a sublinear time algorithm approximates Dasgupta Cost in the oracle model which we now define.

Definition 6
.

(
The OracleModel
)

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering

C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. In the
OracleModel
, we assume that there exists an oracle which for any input
vertex
x\in V
returns an index
i\in[k]
such that
C_{i}\ni x
.

Theorem 5
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}

be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that
admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
. Let

\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
.
Condition on

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
(Algorithm
13
) succeeding (which happens
with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
). Then, in
OracleModel
, with probability at least
1-1/n
, the

\textsc{EstimatedCostOM}(G,\mathcal{D})
procedure (Algorithm
12
) estimates the
optimum Dasgupta cost of
G
within a
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi}\right)
factor in
time
\left(\frac{k\log n}{\eta\cdot\epsilon}\right)^{O(1)}
using at most
\left(\frac{k\log n}{\eta\cdot\epsilon}\right)^{O(1)}
number of
oracle queries.

2
Technical Overview

In this section we outline the main ideas that go in the proof of Theorem
3
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph, and let

\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k})
be a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering of
G
. For ease of presentation in this overview, we
assume for every
i\in[k]
,
|C_{i}|=\Theta(n/k)
. We denote Laplacian of
G
by
\mathcal{L}_{G}
and
the normalized Laplacian of
G
by
L_{G}
. We first consider the
task of estimating the Dasgupta cost of
G
to within a factor
O(\sqrt{\log k})
of the optimal
(in
RandomSampleModel
) in time

\approx O\left(n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\right)
, i.e. essentially simulating the algorithm of
[
CC17
]
in sublinear time.

First note that it is enough to get a tree
\widetilde{T}
with small Dasgupta cost with the
k

clusters as its leaves. This is because at an intuitive level, expanders behave just
like complete graphs, and therefore, up to
\text{poly}(1/\varphi)
factors, the Dasgupta cost of the tree obtained
by extending such a tree
\widetilde{T}
arbitrarily continues to be a
good approximation for Dasgupta cost of the optimal tree for
G
. We now outline how we find
the tree
\widetilde{T}
above. To this effect, we define the contraction
H
of
G
with respect to
the underlying clustering
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k})
denoted as
H=G/\mathcal{C}
.
We write
H=([k],\binom{[k]}{2},W)
to emphasize that the vertex set of the contracted graph
H
corresponds
to the clusters of
G
and for
i,j\in[k]
, the pair
(i,j)
is an edge of
H
with weight

W(i,j)=|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
.

If we were explicitly given the adjacency/Laplacian matrix
of the contracted graph
H
, then finding a good Dasgupta tree for
H
can be easily done by using
the algorithm of
[
CC17
]
which gives a
O(\sqrt{\log k})

approximation to the optimal tree for
H
(and an
O(\sqrt{\log k}/\varphi^{5})
approximation
to the optimal tree for
G
as shown in Theorem
3
). The problem is that we
do not have an explicit access to Laplacian of the contracted graph (which we denote as
\mathcal{L}_{H}
).
However, as shown in Lemma
10
, to get a good approximation to Dasgupta Cost of
H
,
it suffices to provide explicit access to a Laplacian
\widetilde{L}
(which corresponds to a graph
\widetilde{H}
)
where cuts in
\widetilde{H}
approximate sizes of corresponding cuts in
H
in the following sense:

\exists c_{1},c_{2}>1
and
\alpha>0
such that for all
S\subseteq[k]
,

\frac{1}{c_{1}}\cdot|E(S,V\setminus S)|-\alpha\leq|\widetilde{E}(S,V\setminus S)|\leq c_{2}\cdot|E(S,V\setminus S)|+\alpha.

Motivated by this observation, we simulate this access approximately
by constructing a matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
which spectrally approximates
\mathcal{L}_{H}
in the sense that

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}-\xi dn\preceq\mathcal{L}_{H}\preceq a\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}+\xi dn,

for some not too large
a>1
and small
\xi>0
(in time
\approx O\left(n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\right)\cdot\text{poly}(1/\xi)
), as shown in Theorem
10
.
So, our immediate goal is to spectrally approximate
\mathcal{L}_{H}
. We describe this next.

Spectrally approximating
\mathcal{L}_{H}
:
To find a spectral approximation
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}

to
\mathcal{L}_{H}
it would be convenient to have a procedure which estimates quadratic forms
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
.
This is done via a
\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(z)
procedure which on input vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

runs in time
\approx O\left(n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\right)\cdot\text{poly}(1/\xi)
and returns for some sufficiently
small
\xi>0
, an estimate

\alpha_{z}\in\left(z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z-\xi n,a\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi n\right)
.
We now setup some notation and describe the
QuadraticOracle
procedure.

Since we are given access to
G
and not
H
, it is convenient to express the quadratic form
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
in terms of the graph
G
, and then approximate it using random walks in
G
. To that effect, for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, define
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the natural extension of
z
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:
z_{\text{ext}}(x)=z_{i}
if
C_{i}\ni x
, so that that
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z=z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}.

Thus, it suffices to design a good estimate for
z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}
, which we do next.
3
3

3

One important aspect is that we cannot construct
z_{\text{ext}}
in
sublinear time; we will use sampling instead, and bound the error incurred by this.

For simplicity of notation, we estimate
w^{T}L_{G}w
(where
L_{G}
is the normalized Laplacian) for vectors
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
which are not necessarily constant over the clusters. Letting
L_{G}=U\Lambda U^{T}
denote the eigendecomposition of the Laplacian
(and let
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
denote the eigendecomposition of the lazy random walk matrix), our estimator is simply

w^{T}(I-U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})w,

where
U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}
is the best rank-
k
approximation to
M
in spectral norm, obtained by truncating the eigendecomposition
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
appropriately. We note here that while our estimator above is simple, there exists an arguably simpler and a more natural one, namely the rank-
k
approximation to the Laplacian of
G
itself. However, one can verify that the latter does not provide a multiplicative approximation to the quadratic form of the Laplacian – see Remark
2
for more details.

The formal guarantees of the estimator above are given by Lemma
2
. Applied to the vector
z_{\text{ext}}
defined above, Lemma
2
gives

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}\leq d\|z_{\text{ext}}\|_{2}^{2}-dz_{\text{ext}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z_{\text{ext}}\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}.

Thus, in order to obtain an
O(1/\varphi^{2})
approximation to
z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}
, it suffices to approximate

z_{\text{ext}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z_{\text{ext}}=\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}z_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in C_{i}\\
y\in C_{j}\end{subarray}}\left\langle f_{x}^{k},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}^{k}\right\rangle.

(
\dagger
)

where for
x\in V
we write
f_{x}^{k}=U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{x}
denotes the
k
-dimensional spectral embedding of
x
(as per Definition
7
). A natural approach here is to approximate this sum by sampling, assuming that one is able to compute
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle
for vertices
x,y\in V
quickly (we refer to a procedure for computing such weighted dot products as the
weighted dot product oracle
). We design a weighted dot product oracle, and analyze the variance of the sampling process using tail bounds on the spectral embedding derived in
[
GKL+21
]
. The resulting algorithm is presented in Section
4
as
QuadraticOracle
(Algorithm
1
). Its analysis is presented in Section
4
, with the main guarantee provided by Theorem
9
.
Our estimation of
\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}z_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in C_{i}\\
y\in C_{j}\end{subarray}}\left\langle f_{x}^{k},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}^{k}\right\rangle

in (

†

‣
2
) (via sampling) depends on the specifics of our model (
RandomSampleModel
). In particular,
in the
RandomSampleModel
, we get an initial
collection of vertices labelled with
their cluster ids which is used to look up
z_{i}
for
x\in C_{i}
. This requires estimating
|C_{i}|
which is
achieved by taking an initial sample and empirically determining the fractional size of each cluster by using
the labels on these samples.

A very useful property of
QuadraticOracle
is its output is in fact simply a quadratic form of some symmetric matrix
K
, as demonstrated in Lemma
6
. Formally, there exists a symmetric matrix
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that

\textsc{QuadraticOracle}=z^{T}Kz\text{~~for all~}z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

(*)

This fact is very useful in what follows, as it leads to clean way of using the output of
QuadraticOracle
in a semidefinite program to approximate
K
with a bona-fide graph Laplacian matrix. The error introduced by this rounding can be bounded using the following theorem (informal version of Theorem
9
).

Theorem 6
.

[Informal version of Theorem
9
]
The algorithm
QuadraticOracle
when given a
(k,\Omega(1),\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
as input, uses data structure
\mathcal{D}
obtained from a
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
time preprocessing
routine, runs in time
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
and offers the following guarantee:
For any fixed
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|z\|_{2}=1
, whp:

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z\leq\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi dn\text{.}

Using Semidefinite Programming to round
K
:
As noted in (
*
‣
2
) above, the
QuadraticOracle
procedure
computes quadratic forms of some (symmetric) matrix
K
. Theorem

9
shows that the matrix
K
satisfies

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}\preceq K\preceq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+\xi dn\cdot I_{k}
which holds for some sufficiently
small
\xi>0
. However, the matrix
K
is not necessarily a Laplacian matrix of a graph. We use semidefinite programming
to find a Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
which is spectrally close to
K
. The details of this part of the analysis are presented in Section
4.3.3
. Formally, we show the following

Theorem 7
.

[Informal version of Theorem
10
]
The algorithm
ApproxContractedGraph
when given a
(k,\Omega(1),\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
as input, uses data structure
\mathcal{D}
obtained from a
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
time preprocessing
routine, runs in time
\approx n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}
, finds a graph
\widetilde{H}
with Laplacian
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}

such that whp:

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{6}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-\xi dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+\xi dn\cdot I_{k}.

Obtaining the weighted dot product oracle:
Our construction of the weighted dot product oracle proceeds along the lines of

[
GKL+21
]
, except for one important aspect related to ensuring that
QuadraticOracle
in fact computes a quadratic form of the input vector, i.e. ensuring (
*
‣
2
). We explain this aspect now. The work of
[
GKL+21
]
uses short random walks in the input graph to obtain dot product access to the spectral embedding. Fix
t\geq\log n/\varphi^{2}
, the length of the walks.
Given
x\in V
, we denote the end point distribution of a
t
-step random walk from vertex
x
by
m_{x}
.
One of the key lemmas in that paper shows the following: given a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
G
,
for
any pair

x,y\in V
, with high (constant) probability we can estimate
\left\langle m_{x},m_{y}\right\rangle
to
within an additive error of
\frac{\xi}{n}
in time
\approx O\left(n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\right)\cdot\text{poly}(1/\xi)
. We use the Gram matrix of random walk distributions started from sampled vertices and apply the appropriate linear transformation to estimate

\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle
(see Algorithm
14
,
15
in Section
8
). The proof is presented in Theorem
8
. Recall Theorem
10
seeks to spectrally approximate

\mathcal{L}_{H}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
with probability at least
1-1/n
and this amounts to approximating all quadratic
forms on
\mathcal{L}_{H}
with quadratic forms on
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
. We achieve this via an epsilon-net style argument where
the net has size
\exp(k)
. Thus, we want our estimates to be accurate with probability at
least
1-n^{-100k}
. This facilitates union-bounding over the epsilon-net of size
\exp(k)
.
We get such high probability concentration bounds by adapting the Kim-Vu bounds
for our setting. The weighted dot product oracle together with the main formal guarantees that
it satisfies are presented in Appendix
A.1
, and the analysis is given in
Appendix
A.1
as well. The aforementioned Kim-Vu style bounds are presented in
Appendix
B
(see Theorem
12
).

Query Lower Bounds for Approximating Dasgupta Cost:
Next, we overview the
main ideas that go in proving Theorem
4
.
The argument is inspired by the expansion testing lower bounds presented in
[
GR02
]

and adapting it for our purposes. We construct a distribution
D_{1}
, graphs sampled
from which are
d
-regular,
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable (with high probability) and conditioned
on this event, these graphs have Dasgupta cost at most
O(\frac{\epsilon dn^{2}\cdot\log k}{k})
.
We define another distribution
D_{2}
over high cost instances. In particular, graphs sampled from

D_{2}
are
d
-regular,
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable (with high probability) and conditioned
on this event, their Dasgupta cost is at least
\Omega(\epsilon dn^{2})
.

Theorem
4
asserts that, under
RandomSampleModel
, for
Q\leq\frac{1}{100d}\sqrt{n/k}
, any
Q
-query
(determenistic) algorithm
A
which samples
s
initial vertices labeled with the clusters they
belong to and is allowed only an additional
Q-s
future queries, cannot reliably distinguish between
graphs sampled from
D_{1}
or
D_{2}
. In both of these distributions, each of the
k
-clusters is
obtained by (making
\Theta(\frac{\epsilon dn}{k})
edge modifications to) a
d
-regular random graph
on
n/k
vertices. Take a graph
G\sim D
where
D
is either
D_{1}
or
D_{2}
(each with probability
1/2
). We show
that the transcript obtained by
A
on such a graph
G
is a just a union of acyclic components
where each component can contain at most one of the initial
s
vertices whose cluster identities
was revealed to
A
. Thus in particular, the distribution
P_{1}^{A}
induced on the transcripts obtained by
A

on graphs
G\sim D_{1}
and the distribution
P_{2}^{A}
obtained on graphs
G\sim D_{2}
, are statistically
close. Since this holds for any
(Q,s)
-deterministic algorithm
A
, by Yao’s minimax lemma it follows that
in either of these models, there is no
(Q,s)
-randomized algorithm that approximates
Dasgupta cost to within an
o(k/\log k)
factor in time
O(\sqrt{n/k})
with probability at least
2/3
.

A faster approximation to Dasgupta Cost in the Oracle Model:
Finally, we overview the
ideas that go in proving Theorem
5
. This is done in Section
6
.
As shown in Theorem
4
, obtaining even an
O\left(\frac{k}{\log k}\right)

approximation to Dasgupta Cost in
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs takes

\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{k}\right)
queries in the
RandomSampleModel
.
Theorem
5
bypasses this lower bound with the stronger model in which
oracle queries are allowed (Definition
6
). The main idea behind this
improvement is that with oracle queries, one can obtain a good approximation to the contracted
graph at a very low cost (with only
k^{O(1)}
queries). To this effect, consider the following naive algorithm.
Sample
S\subseteq V
with
|S|=k^{O(1)}
and consider together with
S
, the set
N(S)
of
its neighbors. Using the oracle, obtain the cluster ids for every
v\in S\cup N(S)
.
For every pair of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
which have enough edges (at least
\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon dn}{k}\right)
)
between them, one can estimate
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
within very good precision by finding out the
cluster ids of endpoints of all the edges leaving the set
S
. This in turn gives a good approximation
to the contracted graph
H=G/\mathcal{C}
which is later used to approximate the optimum Dasgupta Cost of
G
.

3
Preliminaries

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
we use
[i]
to denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,i\}
. For a vertex
x\in V
, we say that
\mathds{1}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the indicator of
x
, that is, the vector which is
1
at index
x
and
0
elsewhere. For a (multi)set
S\subseteq V
, let
\mathds{1}_{S}
we say that
\mathds{1}_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the indicator of set
S
, i.e.,
\mathds{1}_{S}=\sum_{x\in S}\mathds{1}_{x}
. For a multiset
I_{S}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
of vertices from
V
we abuse notation and also denote by
S
the
n\times s
matrix whose
i^{\text{\tiny{th}}}
column is
\mathds{1}_{x_{i}}
.

Our algorithm and analysis use spectral techniques, and therefore, we setup the following notation. For a symmetric matrix
A
, we write
\nu_{i}(A)
(resp.
\nu_{\max}(A),\nu_{\min}(A))
to denote the
i^{\text{th}}
largest (resp. maximum, minimum) eigenvalue of
A
.

We also denote with
A_{G}
the adjacency matrix of
G
and let
\mathcal{L}_{G}=d\cdot I-A_{G}
denote
the Laplacian of
G
. Denote with
L_{G}
the
normalized Laplacian

of
G
where
L_{G}=I-\frac{A_{G}}{d}
. We denote the eigenvalues of
L_{G}
by

0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n}\leq 2
and we write
\Lambda
to refer to the
diagonal matrix of these eigenvalues in non-decreasing order. We also denote by

(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
L_{G}
and with
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

the matrix whose columns are the orthonormal eigenvectors of
L_{G}
arranged in non-decreasing order
of eigenvalues. Therefore the eigendecomposition of
L_{G}
is
L_{G}=U\Lambda U^{T}
. For any

1\leq k\leq n
we write
U_{[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
for the matrix whose columns are the first
k

columns of
U
. Now, we introduce a central definition to this work, which is the notion of a spectral embedding.

Definition 7
.

(
k
-dimensional spectral embedding
)
For every vertex
x
we let
f_{x}=U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{x}
be the
k
-dimensional spectral embedding of vertex
x
.

The spectral embeddings of vertices in a graph provide rich geometric information which has been
shown to be useful in graph clustering
[
LGT14
,
CPS15
,
CKK+18
,
GKL+21
]
.

In this paper, we are interested in the class of graphs which admit a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering.
We would often need to refer to weighted graphs of the form
H=([k],\binom{[k]}{2},W,w)

where
W
is a weight function on the edges of
H
and
w
is a weight function on the vertices
of
H
. We denote the Laplacian of
H
as
\mathcal{L}_{H}=D_{H}-W
where
D_{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is a diagonal matrix
where for every
i\in[k]
,
D_{H}(i,i)=\sum_{i,j\in[k]}W(i,j)
.
Our algorithms often require
to estimate the inner product between spectral embeddings of vertices
x
and
y
denoted
f_{x}
and
f_{y}
. For pairs of vertices
x,y\in V
we use the notation

\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle:=({f_{x}})^{T}(f_{y})

to denote the dot product in the embedded domain. The following remark asserts that the inner products between
f_{x}
and

f_{y}
are well defined even though the choice for these vectors may not be basis free. First, we need the following
standard result on eigenvalues of
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs
[
LGT14
,
CKK+18
]
.

Lemma 1

(
[
GKL+21
]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering. Then we have
\lambda_{k}\leq 2\epsilon
and
\lambda_{k+1}\geq\frac{\varphi^{2}}{2}
.

Now, we are ready to state the remark from before.

Remark 1
.

Take a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph
G
where
\epsilon/\varphi^{2}
smaller than a constant. Thus,
the space spanned by the bottom
k
eigenvectors of the normalized Laplacian
of
G
is uniquely defined, i.e. the choice of
U_{[k]}
is unique up to multiplication
by an orthonormal matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
on the right. Indeed,
by Lemma
1
it holds that
\lambda_{k}\leq 2\epsilon
and
\lambda_{k+1}\geq\varphi^{2}/2
.
Thus, since we assume that
\epsilon/\varphi^{2}
is smaller than an
absolute constant, we have
2\epsilon<\varphi^{2}/2
and thus, the subspace spanned by the
bottom
k
eigenvectors of the
Laplacian, i.e. the space of
U_{[k]}
, is uniquely defined, as required. We note
that while the choice of
f_{x}
for
x\in V
is not unique, but the dot product
between the spectral embedding of
x\in V
and
y\in V
is well defined, since for every orthonormal

R\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
one has

\langle Rf_{x},Rf_{y}\rangle=(Rf_{x})^{T}(Rf_{y})=\left(f_{x}\right)^{T}(R^{T}R)\left(f_{y}\right)=\left(f_{x}\right)^{T}\left(f_{y}\right)\text{.}

We denote the transition matrix of the
random walk associated with

G
by
M=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(I+\frac{A_{G}}{d}\right)
. From any vertex
v
, this random walk takes every edge incident on
v
with probability
\frac{1}{2d}
, and stays on
v
with the remaining probability which is at least
\frac{1}{2}
. Note that
M=I-\frac{L_{G}}{2}
. Observe that for all
i
,
u_{i}
is also an eigenvector of
M
, with eigenvalue
1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}
.
We denote with
\Sigma
the diagonal matrix of the eigenvalues of
M
in descending order. Therefore the eigendecomposition of
M
is
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
. We write
\Sigma_{[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
for the matrix whose columns are the first
k
rows and columns of
\Sigma
. Furthermore, for any
t
,
M^{t}
is a transition matrix of random walks of length
t
. For any vertex
x
, we denote the probability distribution of a
t
-step random walk started from
x
by
m_{x}=M^{t}\mathds{1}_{x}
. For a multiset
I_{S}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
of vertices from
V
, let matrix
M^{t}S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}
is a matrix whose column are probability distribution of
t
-step random walks started from vertices in
I_{S}
. Therefore, the
i
-th column of
M^{t}S
is
m_{x_{i}}
.

We recall standard (partial) ordering on
n
-by-
n
symmetric matrices.

Definition 8
.

Given two symmetric matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we say that matrix
A

precedes
B
in the Loewner (or the PSD) order if
B-A
is a positive semidefinite
matrix. This is denoted as
A\preceq B
or as
B\succeq A
.

4
Estimating Dasgupta Cost of Clusterable Graphs

In this section we present an algorithm to estimate the Dasgupta’s cost of
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs in sublinear time assuming oracle access to the underlying clustering, i.e., in the
RandomSampleModel
(see Definition
5
). The following definition is central to our algorithm and analysis:

See
4

In fact, our algorithm for estimating the Dasgupta’s cost of
G
will first construct a low cost tree for the contracted graph
H
and use its cost as a proxy for the Dasgupta cost of
G
. The algorithm is reproduced in Section
4.3
below (see Algorithm
3
), and satisfies the following guaratees:

See
3

We start by designing a sublinear time estimator for the quadratic form of
L_{G}
, where
L_{G}
is the normalized Laplacian of the graph
G
. Later we will use this estimator to obtain an approximation of the contracted graph. Recall that
M
is the random walk matrix of
G
and let
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
be the eigendecomposition of
M
. Let
U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}
be a rank-
k
approximation to
M
obtained by truncating the terms corresponding to small eigenvalues. Since,
G
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable, the space spanned by the bottom
k
eigenvectors of the
M
is uniquely defined (see Remark
1
).
Thus, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
z^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z
is well defined. Lemma
2
below shows that the quadratic form of
I-U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}
approximates the quadratic form of
L_{G}
multiplicatively.

Lemma 2
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive sufficiently small
constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
L_{G}
and
M
denote the normalized Laplacian and lazy random walk matrix of
G
. Let
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
denote the eigendecomposition of
M
. Then for any vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
||z||_{2}=1
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}L_{G}z\leq\left(1-z^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z\right)\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}L_{G}z

Proof.

Recall that
U_{[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is a matrix whose columns are the first
k

columns of
U
, and
\Sigma_{[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is a matrix whose columns are the first
k
rows and columns of
\Sigma
.
Let
U_{[-k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(n-k)}
be matrix whose columns are the last
n-k

columns of
U
, and
\Sigma_{[-k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-k)\times(n-k)}
be a matrix whose columns are the last
n-k
rows and columns of
\Sigma
. Thus the eigendecomposition of
M
is
M=U\Sigma U^{T}=U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}+U_{[-k]}\Sigma_{[-k]}U_{[-k]}^{T}
.
Note that
M=I-\frac{L_{G}}{2}
, thus we have

z^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z+z^{T}(U_{-[k]}\Sigma_{-[k]}U_{-[k]}^{T})z=z^{T}Mz=1-\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{2}\text{,}

(1)

which by rearranging gives

\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{2}\leq 1-z^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z=\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{2}+z^{T}(U_{-[k]}\Sigma_{-[k]}U_{-[k]}^{T})z

(2)

The first inequality gives
1-z^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z\geq\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{2}
as desired.

To establish the second inequality above, we will show

z^{T}(U_{-[k]}\Sigma_{-[k]}U_{-[k]}^{T})z\leq\frac{2}{\varphi^{2}}z^{T}L_{G}z
.
Let
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}u_{i}
be the eigendecomposition of vector
z
. Note that

z^{T}L_{G}z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\geq\lambda_{k+1}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}

By Lemma
1
we have
\lambda_{k+1}\geq\frac{\varphi^{2}}{2}
. This gives

\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{\lambda_{k+1}}\leq\frac{2}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}L_{G}z

(3)

Finally, putting (
2
) and (
3
) together we get

\displaystyle 1-z^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z

\displaystyle=\frac{z^{T}L_{G}z}{2}+z^{T}(U_{-[k]}\Sigma_{-[k]}U_{-[k]}^{T})z\leq z^{T}L_{G}z\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{\varphi^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}L_{G}z\text{.}

∎

Let
G
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
), let
\mathcal{L}_{H}
be the Laplacian of graph
H
. We apply Lemma
2
to estimate the quadratic form of
\mathcal{L}_{H}
on a vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. To that effect, for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we define
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the natural extension of
z
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
: we let
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the vector such that for every
x\in V
,
z_{\text{ext}}(x)=z_{i}
, where
C_{i}
is the cluster that
x
belongs to.

Definition 9

(
Extension
z_{\text{ext}}
of a vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
)
.

For a vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
let
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the vector such that for every
x\in S
,
z_{\text{ext}}(x)=z_{i}
, where
C_{i}
is the cluster that
x
belongs to.

Note that
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z=z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}=d\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}.
(see Observation
1
). Thus, to estimate
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
it suffices to design a good estimate for
z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}
, for which we use
z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(I-U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}
, as per Lemma
2
:

z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(I-U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}=||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}-z_{\text{ext}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z_{\text{ext}}=\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|z_{i}^{2}-z_{\text{ext}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z_{\text{ext}}.

Since the first term on the RHS can be easily approximated in the random sample model (Definition
5
), we concentrate on obtaining a good estimate for the second term. We have

z_{\text{ext}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}z_{\text{ext}}=\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}z_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in C_{i}\\
y\in C_{j}\end{subarray}}\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle,

(4)

and therefore in order to estimate
z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}
, it suffices to use few random samples to estimate the sum above, as long as one is able to compute high accuracy estimates for
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle,x,y\in V,
with high probability. We refer to such a primitive as a
weighted dot product oracle
, since it computes a weighted dot product between the
k
-dimensional spectral embeddings
f_{x}
and
f_{y}
for
x,y\in V
(as per Algorithm
14
). Assuming such an estimator, which we denote by
WeightedDotProductOracle
, our algorithm for estimating the quadratic form of the Laplacian of the contracted graph
H
on a vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, i.e.
z^{T}L_{H}z
is
QuadraticOracle
(Algorithm
1
below).

Algorithm 1

QuadraticOracle
(
G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D}
)

For
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
||z||_{2}=1
, we estimate
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
, where
H
is the contracted graph.

1:

s\leftarrow\frac{10^{9}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{6}\cdot n^{80\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\eta^{3}\cdot\xi^{2}}

2:

S\leftarrow
(multi)set of
s
i.i.d random vertices together with their cluster label

3:

S_{i}\leftarrow S\cap C_{i}
, for all
i\in[k]

4:

\xi^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\xi}{8}

5:

v_{1}\leftarrow\frac{n}{s}\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}z_{i}^{2}

6:

v_{2}\leftarrow\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}\cdot z_{j}\cdot\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{D})

# (See Algorithm
14
)

7:

return
d\cdot\left(v_{1}-v_{2}+\frac{\xi\cdot n}{2}\right)

#(See Theorem
9
for approximation guarantees)

Remark 2
.

A yet another natural choice for obtaining the estimator
\widetilde{L}
uses the rank
k
SVD of

L_{G}
which is
U_{[k]}\Lambda_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}
. However, this does not provide a multiplicative
approximation to the quadratic form of the Laplacian. To see this, consider the following instance.
Let
G
consist of
k
disjoint expanders each with inner conductance
\varphi
. In this case,

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\cdots=\lambda_{k}=0
, and the rank
k
approximation above results in the estimator
being
0
.

A handy property of the
QuadraticOracle
procedure above is that its output is just the quadratic
form with some symmetric
K
(as shown in Lemma
6
). Thus, we can use a semidefinite
program to find a bonafide Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
that is spectrally close to
K
. This is achieved by the
ApproxContractedGraph
procedure below (the guarantees that it satisfies are given by Theorem
10
):

Algorithm 2

\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})

1:

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
denote a PSD matrix obtained from the following convex program below:

(C1).

Minimize
0
(Feasibility program, no objective)

(C2).

For every
i\in[k]
,
\sum_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j}=0
.

(C3).

For every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,i}\geq 0
.

(C4).

For every
i\neq j\in[k]
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j}\leq 0
.

(C5).

For
i\neq j
,

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{3}K-\frac{\xi}{2}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq 2K+\frac{\xi}{2}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}.

Recall,
K
is the matrix such that
z^{T}Kz=\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
where
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{8}
.

(C6).

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
symmetric and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\succeq 0
.

2:

\widetilde{H}\leftarrow\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)

#
\widetilde{H}
is the weighted graph with Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}

3:

# Note that
\widetilde{W}_{e}=-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
for every
e\in{[k]\choose 2}

4:

return
\widetilde{H}

Once Theorem
10
has been established, our algorithm for approximating Dasgupta’s cost of
G
simply uses the algorithm of
[
CC17
]
on the approximation
\widetilde{H}
to the contracted graph
H
. Since the cluster sizes in
G
are different and Dasgupta’s cost of a graph is a function of the size of the lowest common ancestor of endpoints of edges, to relate the cost of
G
and the contracted graph
H
, we recall the definition of weighted Dasgupta’s cost.

Definition 10
.

[
Weighted Dasgupta’s cost
]

Let
G=(V,E,W,w)
denote a vertex and edge weighted graph. For a tree
T
with
|V|
leaves (corresponding to vertices
of
G
), we define the weighted Dasgupta’s cost of
T
on
G
as

\text{WCOST}_{T}(G)=\sum_{(x,y)\in E}W(x,y)\cdot\sum_{z\in\textsc{leaves}(T[\textsc{LCA}(x,y)])}w(z).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
, and let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
. We denote
the optimal Dasgupta tree for
Q
as
T^{*}_{Q}
. With this setup,
we will now show the main result (Theorem
3
)
which is finally presented in Section
4.3
.
We consider the graph
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
returned by the
ApproxContractedGraph
procedure. Let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
be a vertex weighted version of
\widetilde{H}
such that for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
, where
\widehat{c_{i}}
is an accurate estimation of
|C_{i}|
obtained in Lemma
27
).

Our estimation primitive
EstimatedCost
(Algorithm
3
below) uses a simple vertex-weighted extension of a result of
[
CC17
]

to find a
tree
\widetilde{T}
on
\widetilde{Q}
. Specifically, we need the following definitions.

Definition 11

(
Vertex-weighted sparsest cut problem
)
.

Let
H=(V,E,W,w)
be a vertex and edge weighted graph. For every set
S\in V
, we define the sparsity of cut
(S,V\setminus S)
on graph
H
as

\text{Sparsity}_{H}(S)=\frac{W(S,V\setminus S)}{w(S)\cdot w(V\setminus S)}\text{,}

where
w(S)=\sum_{x\in S}w(x)
. The vertex-weighted sparsest cut of graph
G
is the cut with the minimum sparsity, i.e.,
\arg\min_{S\subseteq V}\text{Sparsity}_{H}(S)
.

Definition 12

(
Vertex-weighted recursive sparsest cut algorithm
(
WRSC
))
.

Let
\alpha>1
and
H=(V,E,W,w)
be a vertex and edge weighted graph. Let
(S,V\setminus S)
be the vertex-weighted sparsest cut of
H
. The vertex-weighted recursive sparsest cut algorithm on graph
H
is a recursive algorithm that first finds a cut
(T,V\setminus T)
such that
\text{Sparsity}_{H}(T)\leq\alpha\cdot\text{Sparsity}_{H}(S)
, and then recurs on the subgraph
H[T]
and subgraph
H[V\setminus T]
.

Note that on a vertex and edge weighted graph
H=(V,E,W,w)
, there exists an algorithm that finds a
O(\sqrt{\log|V|})
-approximation to the vertex-weighted sparsest cut and runs in time
\text{poly}\left(|V|,\frac{1}{\eta}\right)
, where
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in V}w(i)}{\max_{i\in V}w(i)}
. The following vertex-weighted extension of Corollary
2
is immediate:

Corollary 1
.

Let
H=(V,E,W,w)
be a vertex and edge weighted graph. Then algorithm
WRSC
achieves an
O(\sqrt{\log|V|})
approximation for the weighted Dasgupta cost of
H
.

Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
. We denote the cost of
\widetilde{T}
as
\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})
, we present an estimator (
EST
)
for the Dasgupta cost of an optimal tree in
G
. In particular, we show

\textsc{EST}:=\frac{12}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{12\xi dn^{2}k^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{5}}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G}).

The proof proceeds in two steps: in the first step, we prove Lemma
8

which upper bounds
EST
in terms of
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
,
where
T^{*}_{Q}
is the optimum Dasgupta tree for
Q
.
Next, we use Lemma
12
to relate
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
with
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
. We finally present the
EstimatedCost
procedure.

Algorithm 3

\textsc{EstimatedCost}(G,\mathcal{D})

1:

\xi\leftarrow\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}

2:

\widetilde{H}\leftarrow\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})

# (Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
)

3:

\widetilde{Q}:=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}

# (
\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm\delta)|C_{i}|
computed by Algorithm
4

\&
stored in
\mathcal{D}
)

4:

Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the vertex-weighted recursive sparsest cut algorithm
# See Definition
12

5:

\textsc{EST}\leftarrow\frac{12}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{12\xi dn^{2}k^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}

6:

return
EST

The details are presented in Section
4.3
. The rest of this section is organized as follows. We first present the analysis of
QuadraticOracle
in Section
4.1
. We then show how to turn this approximation into a bonafide graph Laplacian in Section
4.2
. Finally, the proof of Theorem
3
is given in Section
4.3
.

4.1
Correctness of Quadratic Oracle (Proof of Theorem
9
)

The main result of this section is the correctness of
QuadraticOracle
in the random sample (Theorem
9
). Since
QuadraticOracle
relies on our
WeightedDotProductOracle
, we will need correctness guarantees for the latter in our analysis. These are presented in Theorem
8
below, and proved in Appendix
A.1
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small
constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
M
denote the random walk matrix of
G
, and let
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
denote the eigendecomposition of
M
.
Then
\textsc{InitializeWeightedDotProductOracle}(G,\xi)
(Algorithm
14
) computes in time

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}

a data structure
\mathcal{D}
such that
with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
the following holds:

With probability at least
1-3\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}
for all
x,y\in V
we have

|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle|\leq\frac{\xi}{n}\text{,}

where
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}=\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi,\mathcal{D})
.
Moreover, the running time of the procedure
WeightedDotProductOracle

(Algorithm
15
) is
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.

Now we present Lemma
3
, which proves that for any pair of large enough subsets
A,B\subseteq V

we can estimate the means of the weighted inner product between the spectral embedding of vertices in
A
and
B

which equals i.e.,
\frac{1}{|A|\cdot|B|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in A\\
y\in B\end{subarray}}\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\rangle
, by taking enough random samples from
S_{A}\subseteq A
and
S_{B}\subseteq B
and estimating the
(weighted) inner product empirically.
This in turn requires the following lemma which is proved later (in Appendix
A.2
).

Lemma 3
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi,\xi\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive sufficiently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph
that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
A,B\subseteq V
. Let
S_{A}\subseteq A
and
S_{B}\subseteq B
denote (multi)sets of vertices sampled independently and uniformly at random from
A
and
B
respectively, where
|S_{A}|,|S_{B}|\geq\frac{1600\cdot k^{3}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n}{\xi^{2}\cdot\eta}
. Let
M
denote the lazy random walk matrix of
G
, and
M=U\Sigma U^{T}
be the eigendecomposition of
M
. Then, with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

\left|\mathds{1}_{A}^{T}\cdot(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})\ \mathds{1}_{B}\ -\frac{|A|\cdot|B|}{|S_{A}|\cdot|S_{B}|}\cdot\mathds{1}_{S_{A}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})\mathds{1}_{S_{B}}\right|\leq\xi\cdot n

(5)

To prove the correctness of quadratic oracle we need the following lemma that bounds the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm on any unit vector in the eigenspace spanned by the bottom
k
eigenvectors of
L_{G}
, i.e.
U_{[k]}
proved by
[
GKL+21
]
.

Lemma 4
.

[
GKL+21
]

Let
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\leq\frac{\varphi^{2}}{100}
, and let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
u
be a normalized eigenvector of
L
with
||u||_{2}=1
and with eigenvalue at most
2\epsilon
. Then we have

||u||_{\infty}\leq n^{20\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{160}{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}}\text{.}

Finally, we require the following observation that relates the quadratic form of
L_{G}
to the quadratic form of
\mathcal{L}_{H}
where
H
is the contraction of
G
.

Observation 1
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
). Let
\mathcal{L}_{G}
and
\mathcal{L}_{H}
denote the Laplacian of
G
and
H
respectively. Let
L_{G}
denote the normalized Laplacian of
G
. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be extension of a vector
z
(Definition
9
). Then we have

z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z=z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}=d\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}\text{.}

To prove Theorem
9
we need to show that we recive enough samples from
every cluster in the
RandomSampleModel
. Lemma
5
proves this
fact whose relies on a simple Chernoff bound application and we defer its proof to
Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
S\subseteq V
denote a set of random vertices returned by the
RandomSampleModel
(Definition
5
). For every
i\in[k]
let
S_{i}=S\cap C_{i}
. If
|S|\geq\frac{400\cdot\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}
, then with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for every
i\in[k]
we have
|S_{i}|\in(1\pm\delta)\cdot|S|\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}
.

Algorithm 4

InitializeOracle(
G,\xi
)

1:

\delta\leftarrow\frac{\xi\cdot\eta}{512\cdot k^{2}\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}

2:

\xi^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\xi}{8\cdot k^{2}}

3:

\widehat{c_{i}}\leftarrow\textsc{EstimateClusterSize}(G,i,\delta)
, for every
i\in[k]

# See Algorithm
16

4:

\mathcal{D}_{w}\leftarrow\textsc{InitializeWeightedDotProductOracle($G,\xi^{\prime}$)}

# See Algorithm
14

5:

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{w},\{\widehat{c_{i}}\}_{i\in[k]}

6:

return
\mathcal{D}

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
9
.

Theorem 9
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\frac{1}{n^{4}}<\xi<1
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Suppose that we can access
\mathcal{C}
using the
RandomSampleModel
(Definition
5
). Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
), and let
\mathcal{L}_{H}
be the Laplacian of
H
. Then the procedure
\textsc{InitializeOracle}(G,\xi)
(Algorithm
4
) computes in time
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
a sublinear space data structure
\mathcal{D}
such that with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
the following
property is satisfied:

For any fixed
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|z\|_{2}=1
,
with probability at least
1-n^{-95\cdot k}
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z\leq\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi dn\text{.}

Morover,
QuadraticOracle
(Algorithm
1
) runs in time

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.

Proof.

By (Definition
5
) recall that in
RandomSampleModel
, we access
\mathcal{C}
by receiving a random (multi)set
S
(see line (
2
) of Algorithm
1
) together with the cluster label of every vertex in
S
. Thus, for every vertex
x\in S
, the oracle returns a label
i\in[k]
such that
C_{i}\ni x
. Let
S_{i}=S\cap C_{i}
. Let
\xi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\xi}{16\cdot k^{2}}
and
\delta=\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot\eta}{320\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}=\frac{\xi\cdot\eta}{512\cdot k^{2}\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}
. As per line (
1
) of Algorithm
1
we have
|S|=s=\frac{10^{9}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{6}\cdot n^{80\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\eta^{3}\cdot\xi^{2}}\geq\frac{400\cdot\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}
, thus by Lemma
5
with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for all
i\in[k]
we have

|S_{i}|\in\left(1\pm\delta\right)\cdot s\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}

(6)

Note that as per line (
7
),
QuadraticOracle
returns the value
d\cdot\left(v_{1}-v_{2}+\frac{\xi n}{2}\right)
, where as per line (
5
)
v_{1}
is defined as

v_{1}=\frac{n}{s}\cdot\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}z_{i}^{2}\text{,}

(7)

and as per line (
6
)
v_{2}
is defined as

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}

(8)

Let

v^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle=\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}z_{j}\left({\mathds{1}_{S_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{S_{j}}}\right)

(9)

By Theorem
8
with probability

1-3\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}
for all
x,y\in V
we have

\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}
. Thus, with probability at least
1-3\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}
, we get

\displaystyle\left|v_{2}-v^{\prime}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle-\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\right|

By (
4.1
) and (
9
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}

By Theorem
8

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}\cdot s^{2}

As
|z_{i}|,|z_{j}|\leq 1

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi\cdot n}{8}

As
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{8}
as per line (
4
)

(10)

By (
6
), for all
i\in[k]
we have
|S_{i}|\geq(1-\delta)\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\cdot s\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\eta}{k}\cdot s\geq\frac{1600\cdot k^{3}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n}{\xi^{\prime\prime 2}\cdot\eta}\text{,}

where, the first inequality holds as
|C_{i}|\geq\eta\cdot\max_{j\in[k]}|C_{j}|\geq\frac{\eta\cdot n}{k}
and the last inequality holds since
s\geq\frac{10^{9}\cdot k^{7}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n}{\xi^{2}\cdot\eta}\geq\frac{1600\cdot k^{3}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n}{\xi^{\prime\prime 2}\cdot\eta}
(as per line (
1
)) and by choice of
\xi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\xi}{16\cdot k^{2}}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3
for every
i,j\in[k]
with probability at least
1-n^{-100k}
we have

\left|{\mathds{1}_{C_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{C_{j}}}-\frac{|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|}{|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|}\cdot\left({\mathds{1}_{S_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{S_{j}}}\right)\right|\leq\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot n

(11)

Also, note that by (
6
) with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|}{|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|}-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\right|\cdot\left|{\mathds{1}_{S_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{S_{j}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|}{|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|}-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\right|\cdot\left|\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|}{|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|}-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\right|\cdot|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|\cdot\max_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle=\left||C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|-\frac{n^{2}\cdot|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|}{s^{2}}\right|\cdot\max_{x,y\in V}||f_{x}||_{2}\cdot||\Sigma_{[k]}||_{2}\cdot||f_{y}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left||C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|-(1-\delta)^{2}\cdot|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|\right|\cdot\max_{x\in V}||f_{x}||_{2}^{2}

As
||\Sigma_{[k]}||_{2}=\max_{i\in[k]}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\right)\leq 1
, and by (
6
)

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\delta\cdot|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}||u_{i}||^{2}_{\infty}

As
||f_{x}||_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\delta\cdot|C_{i}|\cdot|C_{j}|\cdot k\cdot\frac{160\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\min_{t\in[k]}|C_{t}|}

By Lemma
4

\displaystyle\leq\frac{320\cdot\delta\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot n}{\eta}

As
\frac{\min_{t\in[k]}|C_{t}|}{\max_{t\in[k]}|C_{t}|}=\eta

\displaystyle\leq\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot n

As
\delta=\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot\eta}{320\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}

(12)

Therfore, by (
11
), (
4.1
) and triangle inequality, with probability at least
1-k^{2}\cdot n^{-100k}
, for all
i,j\in[k]
we have

\left|{\mathds{1}_{C_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{C_{j}}}-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\left({\mathds{1}_{S_{i}}^{T}}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}{\mathds{1}_{S_{j}}}\right)\right|\leq 2\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot n

(13)

Let
z_{\text{ext}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector such that for any
x\in V
we have
z_{\text{ext}}[x]=z_{i}
if
x\in C_{i}
.
Note that

z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}=\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}\cdot\left(\mathds{1}_{|C_{i}|}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})\mathds{1}_{|C_{j}|}\right)\cdot z_{j}

(14)

Thus with probability at least
1-k^{2}\cdot n^{-100k}
, for all
i,j\in[k]
we have

\displaystyle\left|z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}-v^{\prime}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i,j\in[k]}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\left(\left(\mathds{1}_{|C_{i}|}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{|C_{j}|}\right)-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\left(\mathds{1}_{S_{i}}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{S_{j}}\right)\right)\right|

By (
9
) and (
14
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j\in[k]}|z_{i}\cdot z_{j}\cdot 2\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot n|

By (
13
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot k^{2}\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot n

As
|z_{i}|,|z_{j}|<1

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi\cdot n}{8}

As
\xi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\xi}{16\cdot k^{2}}

(15)

Putting (
4.1
), (
4.1
) and by triangle inequality we have

\left|z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}-v_{2}\right|\leq\frac{\xi\cdot n}{4}

(16)

Recall that
L_{G}
is the normalized Laplacian of
G
. By Lemma
2
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}}{||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}}\leq\left(1-\frac{z_{\text{ext}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})z_{\text{ext}}}{||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\frac{z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}}{||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}}

Multiplying throughout by
\|z_{\text{ext}}\|_{2}^{2}
, we get

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}\leq\left(||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}-z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\left(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\right)z_{\text{ext}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}

(17)

By (
16
), (
17
), and triangle inequality we have

\frac{d}{2}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}-\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{4}\leq\left(d\cdot||z_{\text{ext}}||_{2}^{2}-d\cdot v_{2}\right)\leq\frac{3\cdot d}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}+\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{4}

(18)

Recall that in line (
7
),
QuadraticOracle
returns
d\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})+\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{2}
. Note that

\displaystyle\left|d\cdot\|z_{\text{ext}}\|_{2}^{2}-d\cdot v_{1}\right|

\displaystyle=d\cdot\left|\|z_{\text{ext}}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{n}{s}\cdot\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}z_{i}^{2}\right|

By (
7
)

\displaystyle=d\cdot\left|\sum_{i\in[k]}z_{i}^{2}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}\left(\frac{|C_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}-\frac{n}{s}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\sum_{i\in[k]}z_{i}^{2}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}\frac{2\cdot\delta\cdot n}{s}

By (
6
),
|S_{i}|\in(1\pm\delta)\cdot s\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\delta\cdot d\cdot n

As
|z_{i}|\leq 1
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|S_{i}|=s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{4}

As
\delta\leq\frac{\xi}{8}

(19)

By (
18
), (
4.1
) and triangle inequality, we have

d(v_{1}-v_{2})\leq\frac{3\cdot d}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}+\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{2}

and

d(v_{1}-v_{2})\geq\frac{d}{2}\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}-\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{2}

Recalling
\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\mathcal{D})=d(v_{1}-v_{2})+\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{2}
and by Observation
1
which says

z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z=z_{\text{ext}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{G}z_{\text{ext}}=d\cdot z_{\text{ext}}^{T}L_{G}z_{\text{ext}}
, we get

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z\leq\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi\cdot d\cdot n

holds with probabilty at least
1-(4+k^{2})\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}\geq 1-n^{-95k}
.

Running time:

Note that
QuadraticOracle
procedure
gets
s=\frac{10^{9}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{6}\cdot n^{80\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\eta^{3}\cdot\xi^{2}}
samples and call
WeightedDotProductOracle
for every pair of samples. For every
i,j\in[k]
line
6
takes time
at most
s^{2}
times the running time of
WeightedDotProductOracle

which is at most
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
. Therefore,
QuadraticOracle
runs in time
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.
∎

4.2
Proof of Theorem
10

Theorem 10
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\frac{1}{n^{3}}<\xi<1
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
), and let
\mathcal{L}_{H}
be the Laplacian of
H
. Then the procedure
\textsc{InitializeOracle}(G,\xi)
(Algorithm
4
) computes in time
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
a sublinear space data structure
\mathcal{D}
such that with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
the following
property is satisfied:

\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm
2
), runs in time

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}

and finds a graph
\widetilde{H}
with Laplacian
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
such that with probability
at least
1-n^{-100}
, we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{6}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-\xi dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+\xi dn\cdot I_{k}.

We now outline the proof.
Recall from Theorem
9
in Section
4.1
that for any fixed

z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-95k}
,

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z\leq\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi dn

Using an epsilon-net argument, Lemma
7
extends a similar guarantee to all vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}

(at the cost of some additive errors in the bound). Moreover, we also show that the evaluations

\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm
1
)
are consistent with some symmetric matrix
K
. That is, we show that for all

z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})=z^{T}Kz

for some symmetric
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. Theorem
10
then follows
by using a SDP to find a matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
spectrally close to
K
where
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies the constraints
for being a graph Laplacian. First we present the following lemma that bound the Frobenius norm of
K
, whose proof can be found in Appendix
A.4
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\frac{1}{n^{4}}<\xi<1
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. There exists a symmetric matrix
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we have

\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})=z^{T}Kz\text{,}

Moreover, with probability at least
1-n^{-99\cdot k}
we have
\|K\|_{F}\leq 2\cdot n^{5}
.

Next, in Lemma
7
we show that with high probability,
QuadraticOracle

(Algorithm
1
) returns a good approximation to
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
for every unit vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This is achieved via an epsilon-net argument.

Lemma 7
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\frac{1}{n^{4}}<\xi<1
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
), and let
\mathcal{L}_{H}
be the Laplacian of
H
. Then with
probability at least
1-n^{-50\cdot k}
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-2\xi dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq K\preceq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+2\xi dn\cdot I_{k}\text{,}

where,
I_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is the identity matrix and
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is the symmetric matrix constructed by Algorithm
1
such that
\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})=z^{T}Kz
.

Proof.

Note that by Theorem
9
and Lemma
6
there exists a symmetric matrix
K
such that for every unit vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with probability at least
1-n^{-95\cdot k}-n^{-99\cdot k}\geq 1-2\cdot n^{-95\cdot k}
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z\leq z^{T}Kz\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi dn

(20)

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
k
-dimensional
\delta
-net of size
\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{k}
on the Euclidean sphere of radius
1
for some small enough
\delta
. Let us first explain how to construct such
\delta
-net. Pick
y_{1}
of unit norm in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and then for every
t\geq 2
pick
y_{t}
of unit norm such that
||y_{t}-y_{j}||_{2}\geq\delta
for all
j\in[t-1]
until no such
y
can be pick. Note that balls of radius
\frac{\delta}{2}
centered at the
y_{t}
’s are disjoint, and their union belongs to the ball of radius
1+\frac{\delta}{2}
centered at zero. Thus
|\mathcal{N}|\leq\frac{\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}}{\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}}\leq\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{k}\text{.}

Let
\delta=4\cdot n^{-40}
. For
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
||z||_{2}=1
, let
y_{z}
be the closest point to
z
from
\mathcal{N}
. Note that for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
||z-y_{z}||_{2}\leq\delta=4\cdot n^{-40}
. By (
20
), and by
union bound over all points in
\mathcal{N}
we have that with probability at least
1-2\cdot n^{95\cdot k}\cdot\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{k}\geq 1-n^{-50\cdot k}
, we have for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
||z||_{2}=1
,

\frac{1}{2}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}\leq y_{z}^{T}Ky_{z}\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}+\xi dn

(21)

Note that

\displaystyle\left|z^{T}Kz-y_{z}^{T}Ky_{z}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(z-y_{z}+y_{z})^{T}K(z-y_{z}+y_{z})-y_{z}^{T}Ky_{z}\right|

\displaystyle\leq|(z-y_{z})^{T}K(z-y_{z})|+2\cdot|(z-y_{z})^{T}Ky_{z}|

By triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq 16\cdot n^{-80}\|K\|_{2}+8\cdot n^{-40}\|K\|_{2}

As
||z-y_{z}||_{2}\leq 4\cdot n^{-40}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-30}

By Lemma
6
,
\|K\|_{2}\leq\|K\|_{F}\leq 2\cdot n^{5}

(22)

Therefore, by (
21
) and(
4.2
) with probability at least
1-n^{-50\cdot k}
, for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
||z||_{2}=1
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}-\frac{n^{-30}}{2}\leq z^{T}Kz\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}+\xi dn+\frac{3\cdot n^{-30}}{\varphi^{2}}

(23)

Also note that we have

\displaystyle\left|z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z-y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(z-y_{z}+y_{z})^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}(z-y_{z}+y_{z})-y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}\right|

\displaystyle\leq|(z-y_{z})^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}(z-y_{z})|+2\cdot|(z-y_{z})^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}|

By triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq 16\cdot n^{-80}\|\mathcal{L}_{H}\|_{2}+8\cdot n^{-40}\|\mathcal{L}_{H}\|_{2}

As
||z-y_{z}||_{2}\leq 4\cdot n^{-40}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-30}

By Lemma
6
,
\|\mathcal{L}_{H}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathcal{L}_{H}\|_{\infty}\leq d\cdot n

(24)

Therfore, by (
4.2
) and (
4.2
) we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}-{n^{-30}}\leq z^{T}Kz\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot y_{z}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}y_{z}+\xi dn+\frac{6\cdot n^{-30}}{\varphi^{2}}

As
\xi\geq n^{-4}
and
\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\leq n^{2}
, thus with probability at least
1-n^{-50\cdot k}
, for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
||z||_{2}=1
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z-2\xi dn\leq z^{T}Kz\leq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+2\xi dn\text{.}

∎

Now we prove Theorem
10
.

Proof.

(Of Theorem
10
) Note that as per line (
(C5)
) of Algorithm
2
we set
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{8}
. Thus by Lemma
7
, with probability at least
1-n^{-50\cdot k}\geq 1-n^{-100}
, the
matrix
K
defined in Lemma
6
