satisfies the following

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-2\xi^{\prime}dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq K\preceq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+2\xi^{\prime}dn\cdot I_{k}\text{.}

Therefore, by choice of
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{8}
we have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-\frac{\xi}{4}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq K\preceq\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+\frac{\xi}{4}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\text{,}

(25)

which implies

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{3}K-\frac{\varphi^{2}\xi dn}{12}\cdot I_{k}\preceq\mathcal{L}_{H}\preceq 2K+\frac{\xi}{2}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\text{.}

(26)

Now consider the convex program used in
2
.
Generating the constraints for this program requires finding the matrix
K

which can be generated via a single call to the
QuadraticOracle
(Algorithm
1
).
Note that this program is feasible as
\mathcal{L}_{H}
is a feasible solution. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{H}
being
a Laplacian satisfies the constraints
(C2)
,
(C3)
,
(C4)
and
(C6)
.
In addition, it also satisfies the condition
(C5)
as well. Further, the set of feasible solutions
contains an open ball: in particular all Laplacian matrices
L^{\prime}
with
\|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}-\mathcal{L}\|_{F}^{2}\leq n^{-10}

satisfy all the constraints. Therefore, in additional time
k^{O(1)}\cdot\log n
, the
Ellipsoid Algorithm returns some feasible solution
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
.
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies all these
constraints as well. In particular, it satisfies condition
(C6)
. This means

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{3}K-\frac{\xi}{2}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq 2K+\frac{\xi}{2}\cdot dn\cdot I_{k}\text{.}

(27)

Therfore, By (
25
) and (
27
) we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{6}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}-\xi dn\cdot I_{k}\preceq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{H}+\xi dn\cdot I_{k}

Running time:

The overall running time is dominated by the call to
QuadraticOracle
which finds the matrix

K
and thus it is at most

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
3

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
, and let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the of contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
. We denote the optimal Dasgupta tree for
Q
as
T^{*}_{Q}
. With this setup,
we will now show the main result (Theorem
3
)
which is finally presented in Section
4.3.3
. Our estimation primitive
EstimatedCost
(Algorithm
6
below) uses a simple vertex-weighted extension of a result of
[
CC17
]

to find a
tree
\widetilde{T}
on
\widetilde{Q}
.
Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
(See Definition
12
and Corollary
1
). We denote the cost of
\widetilde{T}
as
\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})
, we present an estimator (
EST
)
for the Dasgupta cost of an optimal tree in
G
. In particular, we show

\textsc{EST}:=\frac{12}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{12\xi dn^{2}k^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{5}}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G}).

The proof proceeds in two steps: in the first step, we prove Lemma
8

which upper bounds
EST
in terms of
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
,
where
T^{*}_{Q}
is the optimum Dasgupta tree for
Q
.
Next, we use Lemma
12
to relate
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
with
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
. We finally present the
EstimatedCost
procedure.

Algorithm 5

InitializeCostOracle(
G
)

1:

\xi\leftarrow\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}

2:

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\textsc{InitializeOracle}(G,\xi)

# See Algorithm
4

3:

return
\mathcal{D}

Algorithm 6

\textsc{EstimatedCost}(G,\mathcal{D})

1:

\xi\leftarrow\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}

2:

\widetilde{H}\leftarrow\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})

# (Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
)

3:

\widetilde{Q}:=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}

#
\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm\delta)|C_{i}|
computed by Algorithm
4

\&
stored in
\mathcal{D}

4:

Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the vertex-weighted recursive sparsest cut algorithm

# See Definition
12

5:

\textsc{EST}\leftarrow\frac{12}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{12\xi dn^{2}k^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}

6:

return
EST

4.3.1
Bounding the cost of the contracted graph with the approximated contracted graph

The main result of this section is Lemma
8
. This lemma bounds
\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})
in terms of
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
where
T^{*}_{Q}
is the
optimum Dasgupta tree for
Q
. This is useful in relating
EST
with
\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\frac{1}{n^{4}}<\xi<1
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
) and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
be the graph obtained by

\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm
2
), and let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
. Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm. Then with probability at least
1-2\cdot n^{-100}
we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{12}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\sqrt{\log k}\right)

To prove Lemma
8
we first present Definition
13
and Lemma
9
which is a variation of Claim 2.1. from
[
CC17
]
.

Definition 13

(
Maximal clusters induced by a tree
)
.

Let
Q=(V,E,W_{Q},w_{Q})
be a vertex and edge-weighted graph. Let
T
be a tree with
|V|
leaves (corresponding to the vertices of
Q
). For any node
u
of the tree
T
, we define the weight of the node
u
as the sum of the weight of those vertices of
Q
that are leaves of the subtree
T[u]
:

w_{T}(u)=\sum_{x\in\textsc{leaves}(T[u])}w_{Q}(x)\text{.}

Let
T(s)
be the maximal nodes in
T
such that their weight is at most
s
:

T(s)=\{u\in T:w_{T}(u)\leq s\}\text{.}

We refer to these nodes as as maximal clusters of weight at most
s
. For convenience, we also define
T(s)=\textsc{leaves}(T)
for every
s<\max_{x\in V}w(x)
. Note that
T(s)
is a partition of
V
.

We denote by
E_{T}(s)
the edges that are cut in
T(s)
, i.e. edges with end points in different clusters in
T(s)
. For convenience, we also define
E_{T}(s)=E
for every
s<\max_{x\in V}w(x)
.
Also, we let
W_{T(s)}=\sum_{(x,y)\in E_{T}(s)}W(x,y)
denote the total weight of the cut edges in
T(s)
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
Q=(V,E,W_{Q},w_{Q})
be a vertex and edge-weighted graph. Let
\ell=\sum_{x\in V}w_{Q}(x)
be the total vertex weight of graph
Q
. Let
T
be a tree with
|V|
leaves (corresponding to the vertices of
Q
). Then we have

\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T)=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}W_{T}(s)\text{.}

Proof.

The proof is identical to the proof given in
[
CC17
]
. Consider any edge
(x,y)\in E
. Let
r=w_{T}(\textsc{LCA}(x,y))
be the weight of the lowest common ancestor of
x,y
. Then, by Definition
10
the contribution of the edge
(x,y)
to the LHS
is
r\cdot W(x,y)
. Also, note that
(x,y)\in E_{T}(s)
for all
0\leq s\leq r-1
. Hence, the contribution of the edge
(x,y)
to RHS is also
r\cdot W(x,y)
.
∎

Next, we present Lemma
10
which shows that the Dasgupta’s cost of two graphs is close if the weight of every cut is similar in both graphs.

Lemma 10
.

Let
Q=(V,E,W,w)
and
Q^{\prime}=(V,E^{\prime},W^{\prime},w)
be vertex and edge-weighted graphs. Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. Suppose that for every set
S\subseteq V
we have

W^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)\leq\beta\cdot W(S,V\setminus S)+\alpha
. Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
denote the optimum Dasgupta tree of
Q
and

Q^{\prime}
respectively. Then we have

\text{WCOST}_{Q^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\leq\beta\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T)+\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot|V|\cdot||w||_{1}\text{.}

Proof.

The main idea in the proof is to use Lemma
9
to relate the cost of the tree to the cost of cuts.

Let
\ell=||w||_{1}=\sum_{x\in V}w(x)
. For every
0\leq s\leq\ell
, let
B_{1},B_{2},\cdots,B_{t_{s}}
denote the partition of maximal clusters of weight at most
s

induced by
T
in graph
Q
(Definition
13
). By Lemma
9
we have

\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T)=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}W_{T(s)}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\sum_{i\in[t_{s}]}W(B_{i},V\setminus\overline{B_{i}})\text{.}

(28)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\sum_{i\in[t_{s}]}W(B_{i},V\setminus\overline{B_{i}})

By (
28
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\sum_{i\in[t_{s}]}\frac{W^{\prime}(B_{i},V\setminus\overline{B_{i}})-\alpha}{\beta}

As for every
S\subseteq V
,
W^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)\leq\beta\cdot W(S,V\setminus S)+\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}W^{\prime}_{T(s)}-\frac{\alpha}{2\cdot\beta}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}t_{s}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}W^{\prime}_{T(s)}-\frac{\alpha}{2\cdot\beta}\cdot\ell\cdot|V|

As
t_{s}\leq|\textsc{leaves}(T^{\prime})|=|V|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q^{\prime}}(T)-\frac{\alpha}{2\cdot\beta}\cdot\ell\cdot|V|

By Lemma
9

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})-\frac{\alpha}{2\cdot\beta}\cdot\ell\cdot|V|

By optimality of
T^{\prime}
on
Q^{\prime}

Therefore, we get

\text{WCOST}_{Q^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\leq\beta\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T)+\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot|V|\cdot||w||_{1}\text{.}

∎

The proof of Lemma
8
use the result of
[
CC17
]
(Theorem
11
).

Theorem 11

(Theorem 2.3 from
[
CC17
]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. Suppose the RSC algorithm uses an
\alpha
approximation
algorithm for uniform sparsest cut. Then the algorithm RSC achieves an
O(\alpha)

approximation for the Dasgupta cost of
G
.

The following corollary from
[
CC17
]
, follows using the
O(\sqrt{\log|V|})
approximation algorithm for the uniform sparsest cut.

Corollary 2
.

[
CC17
]

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. Then algorithm RSC achieves an
O(\sqrt{\log|V|})
approximation
for the Dasgupta cost of
G
.

Next, we prove the following lemma which is an important intermediate step towards Lemma
8
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
) and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
be an approximation of
Q
such that the following hold:

•

for all
i\in[k]
,
\frac{w(i)}{2}\leq\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)
, and

•

for all
S\subseteq[k]
,
a\cdot W(S,\overline{S})-b\leq\widetilde{W}(S,\overline{S})\leq a^{\prime}\cdot W(S,\overline{S})+b^{\prime}
.

Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm. Then

\frac{a}{2}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-b\cdot n\cdot k\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+b^{\prime}n\cdot k\cdot\sqrt{\log k}\right)

Proof.

Let
T^{*}_{\widetilde{Q}}
be the optimum Dasgupta tree of
\widetilde{Q}
. By Corollary
1

the tree
\widetilde{T}
returned by the vertex-weighted recursive sparsest cut procedure satisfies

\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widetilde{Q}})\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O(\sqrt{\log k})\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widetilde{Q}})

(29)

Recall
Q=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},W,w\right)
is the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
,
i.e.,
Q=G/\mathcal{C}=
(Definition
4
).
Recall that
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)

where
\widetilde{Q}
satisfies items 1 and 2 in the premise. Now we define

\widehat{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},W,\widetilde{w}\right)
,
where
W
is the matrix of edge weights in
Q
, and
\widetilde{w}
is the vector of vertex weights in
\widetilde{Q}
. Let
T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}}
be the optimum Dasgupta tree of
\widehat{Q}
.
Note that the trees
T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}}
and
\widetilde{T}
satisfy the pre-requisites of
Lemma
10
. Therefore, we have

a\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widehat{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}})-\frac{b}{2}\cdot k\cdot||\widetilde{w}||_{1}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widetilde{Q}})\leq a^{\prime}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widehat{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}})+\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\cdot k\cdot||\widetilde{w}||_{1}

(30)

Note that the vertex weight function
w
of
Q
satisfies
w_{i}=|C_{i}|

for all
i\in[k]
. Also, recall from the premise, we also have

\frac{1}{2}\cdot w(i)\leq\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)
. Therefore, by
Definition
10
we have

\displaystyle\text{WCOST}_{\widehat{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}})

\displaystyle\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widehat{Q}}(T^{*}_{Q})

By optimality of
T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}}
on
\widehat{Q}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})

As
\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)

(31)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\text{WCOST}_{\widehat{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}})

As
\widetilde{w}(i)\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot w(i)
on
\widehat{Q}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})

By optimality of
T^{*}_{\widehat{Q}}

(32)

Therefore, by (
30
), (
4.3.1
), (
4.3.1
), and as

||\widetilde{w}||_{2}\leq 2||w||_{2}=2n
we have

\frac{a}{2}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-b\cdot n\cdot k\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(T^{*}_{\widetilde{Q}})\leq 2\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+b^{\prime}\cdot n\cdot k

(33)

Finally, by (
29
) and (
33
) we get

\frac{a}{2}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-b\cdot n\cdot k\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+b^{\prime}\cdot n\cdot k\sqrt{\log k}\right)

∎

Finally, we prove Lemma
8
.
See
8

Proof.

Recall that
\widetilde{H}=\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})
and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}

be the Laplacian of
\widetilde{H}
. Therefore, by Theorem
10
with
probability at least
1-1/n^{100}
, for all vectors
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{6}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{Q}z-\xi dn\cdot\|z\|_{2}^{2}\leq z^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}z\leq\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{Q}z+\xi dn\cdot\|z\|_{2}^{2}

(34)

Note that for every
S\subseteq[k]
, we have
\mathds{1}_{S}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{Q}\mathds{1}_{S}=W(S,\overline{S})
,
and
\mathds{1}_{S}^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\mathds{1}_{S}=\widetilde{W}(S,\overline{S})
. Also, note that

||\mathds{1}_{S}||_{2}^{2}\leq k
. Therefore, by (
34
) with probability at least

1-n^{-100}
for every
S\in[k]
we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{6}\cdot W(S,V\setminus S)-\xi dnk\leq\widetilde{W}(S,V\setminus S)\leq\frac{6}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot W(S,V\setminus S)+\xi dnk

(35)

Moreover, note that by Lemma
27
with probability at
least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have
\frac{1}{2}\cdot w(i)\leq\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)
.
Now, apply Lemma
11
. With probability at least

1-n^{-100\cdot k}-n^{-100}\geq 1-2\cdot n^{-100}
, this gives

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{12}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\sqrt{\log k}\right)

∎

4.3.2
Bounding the optimum cost of the graph with the contracted graph

The main result of this section is Lemma
12
, that relates the cost of the optimum tree of the contracted graph with the cost of the optimum of
G
. This allows us to bound the estimator proposed in Algorithm
3
with the Dasgupta’s cost of the optimum tree of
G
.

Lemma 12
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
). Let
T^{*}_{Q}
and
T^{*}_{G}
be optimum weighted Dasgupta trees for
Q
and
G
respectively. Then we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

To prove Lemma
12
, we show that there exists a tree on the contracted graph whose cost is not more than
O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)
times the optimum cost of the graph (see Lemma
15
). To show this, we exploit the structure of
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs and prove that in these graphs some
cluster respecting cut
has conductance comparable to the conductance of the sparsest cut (see Lemma
13
).

Definition 14

(
Cluster respecting cuts
)
.

Let
G
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering

C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
. We say that the the cut
(B,\overline{B})
is a cluster respecting cut with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
if both
B
and
\overline{B}

are disjoint unions of the clusters. That is, there exists a subset
I\subseteq[k]
such that

B=\cup_{i\in I}C_{i}
, and
\overline{B}=\cup_{i\not\in I}C_{i}
.

Definition 15

(
Cluster respecting tree
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
. We say that tree
T
for
G
(with
|V|
leaves) is cluster respecting with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
if there exists a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
(rooted at the root of
T
) with
k
leaves such that for every
i\in[k]
, there exists a unique leaf
\ell_{i}
(of
T^{\prime}
) such that the leaves in
T
which are descendants of
\ell_{i}
are exactly the set
C_{i}
. We call the tree
T^{\prime}
the contracted subtree of
T
.

Lemma 13

(Some cluster respecting cut has conductance comparable to the sparsest cut)
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller
than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with maximum degree
d

that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
(S,V\setminus S)

denote the sparsest cut of
G
. Then there exists a cluster respecting cut
(Definition
14
)
(B,V\setminus B)
such that

\max(\phi_{\text{out}}^{G}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}^{G}(\overline{B}))\leq\frac{4\cdot\phi_{\text{out}}^{G}(S)}{\varphi}\text{.}

Proof.

Let
(S,\overline{S})
denote the cut with the smallest conductance in
G
,
where
|S|\leq|V\setminus S|
. If
(S,\overline{S})
is cluster respecting,
we are already done. So, let us suppose it is not cluster respecting and let

\beta=\phi_{\text{in}}(G)=\phi_{\text{out}}(S)
.
For each
i\in[k]
, define
X_{i}=S\cap C_{i}
to be the vertices of
S
that belong to
C_{i}
. Similarly, for each
i\in[k]
, define
Y_{i}=\overline{S}\cap C_{i}
.
Also, we use
M_{i}
to denote for each
i\in[k]
, the smaller set between
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
.
That is,
|M_{i}|=\argmin(|X_{i}|,|Y_{i}|)
. Define
M=\cup_{i\in[k]}M_{i}
and let

B=(S\setminus M)\cup(\overline{S}\cap M)\ and\ \overline{B}=V\setminus B.

This makes
(B,\overline{B})
a
cluster respecting cut
. This is because
we move vertices of
M
from
\overline{S}
to
S
to get
B
and the other way around
to get
\overline{B}
. We will show that the sets
B
and
\overline{B}
are both non empty
and we will upperbound

\max(\phi_{\text{out}}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}(\overline{B}))=\displaystyle\frac{|E(B,\overline{B})|}{d\cdot\min(|B|,|\overline{B}|)}
.
By definition of
B
,

|E(B,\overline{B})|\leq|E(S,\overline{S})|+d|M|=\beta d|S|+d|M|.

Also
\cup_{i\in[k]}E(X_{i},Y_{i})\subseteq E(S,\overline{S})
. Moreover, recalling that
each cluster has
\phi_{\text{in}}(C_{i})\geq\varphi
, it follows that

|E(S,\overline{S})|\geq\sum_{i\in[k]}|E(X_{i},Y_{i})|\geq\varphi\cdot d|M|.

Therefore,
\varphi\cdot d|M|\leq|E(S,\overline{S})|=\beta\cdot d|S|
. This means

|E(B,\overline{B})|\leq\beta\cdot d|S|+\frac{\beta\cdot d|S|}{\varphi}\leq\frac{2\beta\cdot d|S|}{\varphi}

(36)

We define an index set

I_{\text{small}}=\{i\in[k]:|X_{i}|\leq|Y_{i}|\}
which indexes clusters where
S
contains fewer vertices
of
C_{i}
than
\overline{C_{i}}
. We show that if
\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}|X_{i}|<|S|/10
,
then
\min(|B|,|\overline{B}|)\geq 0.5|S|
. Moreover, we also show that the
other case, with
\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}|X_{i}|<|S|/10
cannot occur. In all, this
means
\max(\phi_{\text{out}}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}(\overline{B}))\leq\frac{4\beta}{\varphi}\cdot\phi_{\text{out}}(S)
.
Let us consider the first situation above.

Case 1:
Suppose
\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}|X_{i}|<|S|/10
.
Note that for any
i\not\in I_{\text{small}}
,
B\supseteq X_{i}
. Thus, we have

|B|\geq 0.9|S|\geq 0.5|S|
(and thus
B
is non empty). Also, note that in this case,

|\overline{B}|\geq|\overline{S}|-|M|\geq|S|-|M|\geq|S|-\frac{\beta}{\varphi}|S|

which is at least
0.5|S|
as well (and in particular,
\overline{B}
is also non empty).
Thus,
\min(|B|,|\overline{B}|)\geq 0.5|S|
.
This gives
\max(\phi_{\text{out}}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}(\overline{B})\leq\frac{4\beta}{\varphi}
.
In either case, we note that the cut
(B,\overline{B})
is cluster respecting and satisfies that

\max(\phi_{\text{out}}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}(\overline{B}))\leq\frac{4\beta}{\varphi}
.

Case 2:
Now, we rule out the case
\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}|X_{i}|\geq|S|/10
.
Note that all
X_{i}
’s are disjoint (as they are contained in different
clusters). Observe

|E(S,\overline{S})|\geq\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}|E(X_{i},Y_{i})|\geq\varphi\sum_{i\in I_{\text{small}}}d|X_{i}|\geq\varphi\cdot\frac{d|S|}{10}

The first step follows because for any
i\in I_{\text{small}}
,
E(X_{i},Y_{i})\subseteq E(S,\overline{S})
.
Moreover,
(X_{i},Y_{i})
is a cut of the cluster
C_{i}
with
\phi_{\text{in}}(C_{i})\geq\varphi
.
However, this means that
\phi_{\text{out}}(S)\geq\varphi/10
. But
G
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable
and
S
is the sparset cut in
G
. Thus, it better hold that
\phi_{\text{out}}(S)\leq\epsilon<\varphi/10

which leads to a contradiction.
∎

The following claim is an aside which proves the tightness of Lemma
13
.
This claim shows that the
O(1/\varphi)
loss in approximation to conductance is inherent when we
take a cluster respecting cut as opposed to the sparsest cut. The proof can be found in
Appendix
A.5
.

Claim 1
.

[Tightness of Lemma
13
]
Let
\epsilon>0
and
\varphi\in(0,1)
where
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
is smaller than
a sufficiently small positive constant. Let
d>3
be a constant.
Then, there exist a
(2,\varphi,\epsilon)
clusterable,
d
-regular graph
G
such that

\min\left(\phi_{\text{out}}(B),\phi_{\text{out}}(V\setminus B)\right)\geq\phi_{\text{in}}(G)\text{,}

where,
(B,\overline{B})
is the unique cluster respecting cut of
G
.

The following observation shows that in a cluster respecting cut
(B,V\setminus B)
, the sets
B,\overline{B}
induce
(k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs themselves. This is later used to prove Lemma

15
.

Observation 2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
(B,V\setminus B)
be a cluster
respecting cut in
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
14
). Suppose that
B
contains
k^{\prime}<k
clusters in
G
. For every
S\subseteq V
, let
G[S]
be a graph obtained by adding
d-d^{S}_{x}
self loops to every vertex
x\in S
, where
d^{S}_{x}
is the degree of vertex
x
in
S
. Then, we have
G[S]
is
(k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable and
G[V\setminus B]
is
(k-k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.

Proof.

We will show this observation for
B
. A similar argument holds for
V\setminus B
.
For every cluster
C_{i}\subseteq B
, we have

\phi^{G[B]}_{\text{out}}(C_{i})=\frac{|E(C_{i},B\setminus C_{i})|}{d|C_{i}|}\leq\frac{\epsilon d|C_{i}|}{d|C_{i}|}=\epsilon\text{.}

Also, not that for every cluster
C_{i}\subseteq B
and every
S\subseteq C_{i}
with
|S|\leq|C_{i}|/2
we have

\phi^{G[B]}_{\text{in}}(S)=\frac{|E(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|}{d|S|}\geq\varphi\text{.}

Therefore, for every cluster
C_{i}\subseteq B
we have
\phi^{G[B]}_{\text{out}}(C_{i})\leq\epsilon
and
\phi^{G[B]}_{\text{in}}(C_{i})\geq\varphi
. Thus,
B
is
(k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.
∎

We will need the following fact from
[
LT80
]
.

Fact 1
.

(Balanced edge separator theorem on trees)
Let
T
denote a binary tree with
n
nodes in total. Then there exists an
edge
e=(u,v)\in E(T)
such that both subtrees in
T\setminus e
contain
at most
2n/3
vertices.

We need one more ingredient before we move on to prove Theorem
3
. It is the following
observation.

Lemma 14
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be an expander with maximum degree
d
where
\phi_{\text{in}}(G)\geq\varphi
.
Let
T^{*}_{G}
be a tree with optimum Dasgupta cost for
G
. Then

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\frac{\varphi d|V|^{2}}{20}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{B}
denote the set of all binary trees with
|V|
leaves.
We will show that all trees
T\in\mathcal{T}_{B}
have
\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\varphi d|V|^{2}/20
.
In particular, this also means that

\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{B}}\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\varphi d|V|^{2}/20.

However, as noted in Section 2.2 of
[
Das16
]
, we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})=\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{B}}\text{COST}_{G}(T)
. Thus, it suffices to lower bound the Dasgupta cost
of any binary tree for
G
. To this end, let us setup some notation first. Take any tree
T\in\mathcal{T}_{B}
.
Consider any edge
(u,v)\in E(T)
and delete it from the tree. Denote the component containing
the vertex
u
by
T_{u}
and the component containing the vertex
v
by
T_{v}
. By Fact

1
, there exists an edge
(u,v)
in the tree such that on deleting it,
both
T_{u}
and
T_{v}
have at most
2/3\cdot|V(T)|
vertices. Since
T
is a binary tree with
|V|
leaves,
note that
|T|=2|V|-1
. Wlog, suppose
T_{u}
is the side with fewer vertices. Thus,

|T_{u}|\geq(1/3)|T|\geq 1/3\cdot(2|V|-1)\geq 0.6|V|.

Now, let
L
denote the set of leaves in the tree
T_{u}
. Note that
T_{u}
is again a binary tree and
with at least
0.6|V|
nodes. Thus, the number of leaves in
T_{u}
is at least
|L|\geq 0.3|V|
. Recall
the leaves of
T
are exactly the vertices of
G
.

And since
G
is a
\varphi
-expander, we have
|E(L,\overline{L})|\geq\varphi d|L|
. Now take any
edge
(x,y)\in E(L,\overline{L})
, and let
w
denote the LCA of
x
and
y
in the tree. Note that
the number of leaves which descend from
w
is at least
|L|\geq 0.3|V|
. Thus, the contribution to
Dasgupta cost from any edge crossing the cut
(L,\overline{L})
is at least
|L|
. Which means the
total Dasgupta cost for
T
is at least

|E(L,\overline{L})|\cdot|T_{u}|\geq\varphi d|L|\cdot|L|\geq\varphi\cdot 9d|V|^{2}/100\geq\varphi d|V|^{2}/20

as desired.
∎

Corollary 3
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph
that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
. Let
T^{*}_{G}
denote the tree
with optimum Dasgupta cost for
G
. Then

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\frac{\varphi}{20}\cdot d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
denote the graph obtained from
G
by throwing away all the edges
between different clusters of
G
. That is,
G^{\prime}
is simply the disjoint union
of
G[C_{i}]
for all
i\in[k]
. Take any tree
T
with
n
leaves. Note that
the Dasgupta cost of
T
on
G^{\prime}
is at most the Dasgupta cost of
T
on

G
. Note that since for any
i\in[k]
,
\phi_{\text{in}}^{G}(C_{i})\geq\varphi

it also holds that
\phi_{\text{in}}(G[C_{i}])\geq\varphi
.
Next, we claim that a tree with smallest Dasgupta cost on
G^{\prime}
is simply
obtained by merging optimum Dasgupta trees of all the clusters in a new tree. That is,
letting
T^{*}_{G^{\prime}}
denote an optimum Dasgupta tree for
G^{\prime}
, one way to obtain
T^{*}_{G^{\prime}}
is
takes optimum Dasgupta trees
T_{i}
for
G[C_{i}]
with root
R_{i}
and makes all
R_{i}
’s
immediate children of a new root
R
. Assuming this claim, and noting that for all
i\in[k]
,

\phi_{\text{in}}(G[C_{i}])\geq\varphi
an application of Lemma
14
gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\text{COST}_{G^{\prime}}(T^{*}_{G^{\prime}})\geq\sum_{i\in[k]}\text{COST}_{G[C_{i}]}(T_{i})\geq\frac{\varphi}{20}\sum_{i\in[k]}d|C_{i}|^{2}.

Finally, we prove the claim above which says that the root cut in
T^{*}_{G^{\prime}}
separates out all the
clusters
C_{i}
’s which follows from Lemma 2 of
[
Das16
]
.
∎

Next, we present Lemma
15
which is a corollary of Lemma
13
and shows that there exists a cluster respecting tree whose cost is not more than
O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)
times the optimum cost of the graph.

Lemma 15
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller
than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph
that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
. Let
T^{*}_{G}

be a tree with the optimum Dasgupta’s cost on
G
. Then, there exists a cluster respecting tree

T
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
on
G
(Definition
15
) such that

\text{COST}_{G}(T)\leq O\left(\frac{\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})}{\varphi}\right)\text{.}

Moreover the contracted tree of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k})

(Definition
15
) is a binary tree.

Proof.

Let
H=G/\mathcal{C}
denote the contraction of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{T}_{H}

denote the set of trees supported on
k
leaves (which correspond to the
k
clusters).
We will construct a cluster respecting tree
T
for
G
with the claimed properties.
This is done in two phases. In the first phase, we show it is possible to
obtain a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
by repeatedly applying Lemma
13
.
In phase two, we extend this tree further by refining each of the
k
leaves in
T^{\prime}
further
to obtain a tree
T
for
G
. Assuming the
T^{\prime}
after phase one indeed belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{H}
, we obtain
T

in the following way: For
i\in[k]
, take the leaf
C_{i}
(in
T^{\prime}
) and make all
|C_{i}|
vertices its direct children.

Phase 1: Producing
T^{\prime}
.
We show how to generate
T^{\prime}
level by level.
It will be convenient to attach to each node in
T^{\prime}
a set
I\subseteq[k]
of indices.
We begin by attaching
[k]
to the root. An application of Lemma
13
produces
a cut
(B,\overline{B})
with conductance within a factor
O(1/\varphi)
of the
sparsest cut in
G
which additionally is cluster respecting (see Definition

14
). Make
B
and
\overline{B}
the two children of the root
and attach to
B
the index set
I_{B}\subseteq[k]
which denotes the clusters contained in

B
(similarly define
I_{\overline{B}}\subseteq[k]
). Recurse and apply Lemma

13
to both
G[B]
and
G[\overline{B}]
.
Note that we can do this because both
G[B]
(resp.
G[\overline{B}]
) are

(k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable (resp
(k-k^{\prime},\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable) for some

1\leq k^{\prime}<k
(Observation
2
).
Thus, in all, phase 1 returns a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
.

Phase 2: Extending
T^{\prime}
On termination, as seen earlier, phase 1 produces
a tree
T^{\prime}
whose leaves correspond to the clusters
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k}
in some order.
Note that

\text{COST}_{G}(T)=\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})+d\sum|C_{i}|^{2}

(37)

We will show this cost is at most
O(1/\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
. To this end, let us consider another tree
T^{\text{RSC}}
that is obtained by taking the tree
T^{\prime}
and
extending it by repeated applications of
exact
recursive sparses cut procedure (with
approximation factor being
1
). Thus, the tree
T^{\text{RSC}}
obtained this way
is within a factor
O(1/\varphi)
for the sparsest cut at each step.
By Theorem
11
, we get

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{\text{RSC}})\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})

(38)

Let us rewrite this approximation in more detail. For
i\in[k]
, letting
T^{\text{RSC}}(i)

denote the tree obtained by applying recursive sparsest cut on each
C_{i}
. Thus, by (
38
) we get

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\text{COST}_{G}(T^{\text{RSC}})=\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})+\sum_{i\in[k]}\text{COST}_{G[C_{i}]}(T^{\text{RSC}}(i))

(39)

Now two cases arise. First, consider the case where
\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})\leq 2d\cdot\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}
.
In this case, by (
37
) we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T)\leq 3d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}
. Also, by Corollary
3
we have
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\frac{\varphi}{20}\cdot d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}
. Thus, we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T)\leq\frac{60}{\varphi}\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

Now consider the other case where
\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})>2d\cdot\sum|C_{i}|^{2}
.
Recall from (
37
) that

\text{COST}_{G}(T)=\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})+d\sum|C_{i}|^{2}\leq 2\cdot\text{WCOST}_{H}(T^{\prime})\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{,}

where, the last inequality holds by (
39
). Moreover, by construction, the tree
T^{\prime}

is the a binary tree (and is the contracted tree of
T
) as desired.

∎

Finally, we prove the main result of this subsection as follows:
See
12

Proof.

By Lemma
15
, we know there is a cluster respecting tree
T
(Definition
15
) on
n
leaves such that

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T)

(40)

Let
T^{\prime}
be the corresponding contracted tree obtained from
T
(Definition
15
). Note that by construction
T
in Lemma
15
we have
T^{\prime}
has
k
leaves such that for every
i\in[k]
all the vertices of
C_{i}
are direct children of the leaf corresponding to
C_{i}
. Thus, we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T)=\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{\prime})+d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}

(41)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T)

By (
40
)

\displaystyle=\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\left(\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{\prime})+d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}\right)

By (
41
)

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\left(\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}\right)

By optimality of
T^{*}_{Q}
on
Q

(42)

Note that
T^{*}_{Q}
has
k
leaves (corresponding to clusters
C_{1}\ldots C_{k}
). Let
T_{\text{ext}}
be the tree obtained by attaching every vertex
x\in C_{i}
to the leaf corresponding to the cluster
C_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
. Note that
T_{\text{ext}}
has
n
leaves and is a tree on
G
. Thus, we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T_{\text{ext}})=\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}

(43)

Also, by optimality of
T^{*}_{G}
on
G
we have
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\text{COST}_{G}(T_{\text{ext}})
. Therefore,

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}

(44)

Thus, by (
4.3.2
) and (
44
) we get that

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum|C_{i}|^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

∎

4.3.3
Proof of Theorem
3

Finally, to prove Theorem
3
we prove an intermediate Lemma
16
.

Lemma 16
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
) and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
0<a<1<a^{\prime}
and
b\leq\frac{a}{k\sqrt{\log k}}
. Let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
be an approximation of
Q
such that the following hold:

•

For all
i\in[k]
,
\frac{w(i)}{2}\leq\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)
, and

•

a\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-b\cdot dn^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+b\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}\right)
,

where,
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm.
We set
\textsc{EST}=\frac{1}{a}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{b}{a}dn^{2}+d\sum\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}
.
Then we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\textsc{EST}\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a\cdot\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)

denote the graph defined in the premise, where for
every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
. Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{RSC}(\widetilde{Q})

denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive
sparsest cut algorithm. We have that

a\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-b\cdot dn^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(a^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+b\cdot dn^{2}\sqrt{\log k}\right)

(45)

Note that

\textsc{EST}=\frac{1}{a}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{b}{a}dn^{2}+d\sum\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\text{.}

(46)

Therefore, by (
45
) and (
46
) we have

\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\leq\textsc{EST}\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}{a}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\frac{b\cdot dn^{2}}{a}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}\right)+d\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}

(47)

Let
T^{*}_{G}
denote a Dasgupta tree with optimum cost for
G
. Therefore, by Lemma
12
we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+d\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})

(48)

By the first part of both (
47
) and (
48
) we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\textsc{EST}

(49)

Also, we have

EST

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}{a\cdot\varphi}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\frac{b\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}{a}\right)+d\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}

By (
47
)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a}\right)\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})+O\left(\frac{b\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}{a}\right)+4\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|^{2}

By (
48
), and as
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq 2|C_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{a\sqrt{\log k}}{a^{\prime}\cdot\varphi}\right)\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})+O\left(\frac{dn^{2}}{k}\right)+4\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|^{2}

As
b\leq\frac{a}{k\sqrt{\log k}}

\displaystyle\leq O\left(a^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{a\varphi}\right)\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})+O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}d\cdot|C_{i}|^{2}\right)

As
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|^{2}\geq n^{2}/k

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a\varphi}\right)\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})+O\left(\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})

By Corollary
3

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a\cdot\varphi}\right)\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})

(50)

Therefore, by (
49
) and (
4.3.3
) we have

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\textsc{EST}\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a\cdot\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

∎

See
3

Proof.

Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
4
) and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
be the graph obtained by

\textsc{ApproxContractedGraph}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm
2
), and let
\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
. Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{RSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm. Therefore, by Lemma
8
with probability at least
1-2\cdot n^{-100}
we have

\frac{\varphi^{2}}{12}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\xi dn^{2}k^{2}\sqrt{\log k}\right)

(51)

Note that as per line (
5
) of Algorithm
3
we estimate the Dasgupta cost of
G
by

\textsc{EST}=\frac{12}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{12\xi dn^{2}k^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\text{.}

(52)

Set
a=\varphi^{2}/12,a^{\prime}=1/\varphi^{2},b=\xi\cdot k^{2}
, where
\xi=\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\sqrt{\log k}}
as per line (
1
) of Algorithm
3
.
Thus,
b=\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k\sqrt{\log k}}\leq\frac{a}{k\sqrt{\log k}}
. So, we can apply Lemma
16
, which gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\textsc{EST}\leq O\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\sqrt{\log k}}{a\cdot\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi^{5}}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

Running Time:
Now, we prove the running time bound. First let us
consider the
InitializeCostOracle
procedure. Note that this process calls

InitializeOracle
which in turn invokes
EstimateClusterSize
procedure
with
\delta=\frac{\xi\cdot\eta}{512\cdot k^{2}\cdot n^{40\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}

where
\xi=\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}
.
By Lemma
27
, this can be done in time

O\left(\frac{\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot k^{12}\cdot\log n\cdot\log k}{\varphi^{4}\cdot\eta^{3}}\right).

After the preprocessing in
InitializeCostOracle
is done, the

EstimatedCost
procedure calls the
ApproxContractedGraph

procedure that takes
n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
time.
Substitute
\xi=\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}
and note that
the procedure
WRSC
runs in time
\text{poly}(k,1/\eta)
. Therefore, the overall running time
of
EstimatedCost
procedure is

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
.
Finally, we bound the number of seed queries issued. To do this, note that according to
line
1
of Algorithm
1
, we take an initial sample which
has
s=\frac{10^{9}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{6}\cdot n^{80\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\eta^{3}\cdot\xi^{2}}

seeds. As noted earlier,
\xi=\frac{\varphi^{2}}{k^{3}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}
. Substituing this in,
we note that the number of seed queries issued is at most
n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\varphi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}
as claimed.
∎

.

5
An Almost
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
Query Lower Bound on Algorithms for Dasgupta Cost

In this section, we prove a lower bound on the number of queries needed for obtaining
a
\Omega(\frac{k}{\log k})
approximation to Dasgupta cost on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable instances.
In particular, we show the following theorem.

See
4

To do this, we will show the following lemma from which the above theorem follows directly.

Lemma 17
.

Fix
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
\varphi=\varphi(d)
such that for all

\epsilon\leq\varphi^{2}
and two distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}

which satisfy

•

With high probability,
G\sim D_{1}
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
clusterable
and has

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq O\left(\max\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\log k}{k},\frac{dn^{2}}{k}\right)\right)
.

•

With high probability,
G\sim D_{2}
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
clusterable
and has
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\Omega\left(\min(\epsilon dn^{2},\frac{dn^{2}}{k})\right)
.

such that any
Q
-query algorithm
A
which distinguishes between graphs drawn from
D_{1}

and
D_{2}
with probability at least
2/3
requires at least
\Omega(\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{k}})

queries the
RandomSampleModel
.

This lemma is shown in Section
5.1
. First, we deduce Theorem
4

using this lemma.

Proof.

(Of Theorem
4
)
Fix
d
and let
\varphi=\varphi(d)
be as obtained from Lemma
17
.
Let
k\geq 2^{\Omega(1/\varphi^{2})}
. Now, suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which
approximates Dasgupta Cost of all

(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs
G
to within a
o(k/\log k)
factor with probability
at least
2/3
and makes
o(\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{k}})
queries in the
RandomSampleModel
.
Now consider the distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
from
Lemma
17
and sample a graph
G\sim D_{1}
. Since the algorithm

\mathcal{A}
produces a tree
T
with Dasgupta cost which is within a factor
o(k/\log k)

of
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
, note that for
1/100\log k\leq\epsilon\leq\varphi^{2}
, the Dasgupta
cost of
T
on
G
is at most
o(\epsilon dn^{2})
. Thus, in this regime, using
\mathcal{A}
it is possible
to distinguish between graphs sampled from
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with probability at least
2/3
.
Contradiction.
∎

5.1
Proof of Lemma
17

We present a distribution on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable instances with small Dasgupta
cost and another distribution on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable instances with large
Dasgupta Cost. We show that any algorithm which makes very few queries is unlikely to
distinguish reliably between the graphs sampled from these distributions. Towards defining
these distributions, we begin with some setup.

Definition 16
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
an even
d\geq 4
and
0<\delta<1/100d
. The configuration model parameterized by

n,\delta,d
generates a random
d
regular graph
G=(V,E)
with
n
vertices
as follows. It first generates
d
half edges on each vertex. These edges are generated
in two rounds.

1.

In round 1, we generate
d-1
half edges on each vertex. We connect all these half
edges by a random perfect matching and this finishes round 1.

2.

In round 2, we generate the last half edge on each vertex. Each half edge in this
round is marked red with probability
\delta d
. Again, we connect all half
edges generated in this round by a random perfect matching.

An edge formed in round 2 by pairing one red half edge with any other edge is called a red edge.
Let
G
denote the final graph obtained.
G
might have self loops and parallel edges
and is not necessarily simple. Note that our definition of the configuration model departs
from the standard definition in the literature because of these special red edges.

Remark 3
.

Note that our config model allows self loops and parallel edges. Despite this,
one can verify that the guarantees of our algorithm in the
RandomSampleModel

(from Theorem
3
) continue to hold.

We state now the following fact which claims that a random
d
-regular graph is likely to be
an expander.

Fact 2
.

For
d\geq 3
, let
\varphi=\varphi(d)\in(0,1)
be such that

(2\varphi)\log_{2}(2\varphi)+(2-2\varphi)\log_{2}(2-2\varphi)>4/d
.
Then with probability at least
1-\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
, a random
d
-regular graph generated
according to the configuration model has expansion at least
\varphi
.

We now define our distributions over low and high Dasgupta cost instances (which we denote
as
D_{1}=D_{1}(n,d,\delta,k)
and
D_{2}(n,d,\delta,k)
respectively). Let
H_{1}=([k],E_{1})

be a copy of the cycle on
k
vertices and
H_{2}=([k],E_{2})
denote a

4
-regular expander with
\phi(H_{2})\geq\Omega(1)
. For
v\in[k]
, let
N_{b}(v)
denote the
neighborhood of
v
in
H_{b}
(for
b\in\{1,2\}
). We now describe in words how we generate
samples from the distribution
D_{1}
(or
D_{2}
). Fix
b\in{1,2}
. We will generate a

(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable
d
-regular graph
G\sim D_{b}
as follows. First, we generate
the
k
clusters each with size
n/k
by sampling each cluster from the config model. Then, we
ensure that the contraction of a graph sampled from
D_{b}
with respect to the
k
-clustering

\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k})
as follows. Note that
H_{b}
is a
2b
-regular graph. To enforce
that the contractions of
G
are indeed consistent with
H_{b}
, for
(i,j)\in E(H_{b})
, we add

\Theta(\epsilon dn/k)
edges between
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
while preserving
d
-regularity. This is done
by taking a red edge
e=(a,b)
with both endpoints in
C_{i}
and another red edge

f=(c,d)
in
C_{j}
, deleting them both and adding the edges
e^{\prime}=(a,c)
and
f^{\prime}=(b,d)

instead. An edge in
C_{i}
is red with probability
\Theta(\delta)
and so the number of red edges
in
\Theta(\delta nd/k)
with good probability. Our algorithm ensures there are
\Theta(\delta nd/2bk)

edges between
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
(whenever
(i,j)\in E(H_{b})
). Now, we present our algorithms which
produce a random instance from distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

Algorithm 7

\textsc{SampleFromD1}(n,d,\delta,k)

1:

b\leftarrow 1

2:

deg\leftarrow 2

3:

G\leftarrow\textsc{GenerateSample}(n,d,\delta,k,b,deg)

4:

return

G
.

Algorithm 8

\textsc{SampleFromD2}(n,d,\delta,k)

1:

b\leftarrow 2

2:

deg\leftarrow 4

3:

G\leftarrow\textsc{GenerateSample}(n,d,\delta,k,b,deg)

4:

return
G
.

Algorithm 9

\textsc{GenerateSample}(n,d,\delta,k,b,deg)

# (
b=1
indicates
D_{1}
,
b=2
indicates
D_{2}
)

1:

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k}\sim\textsc{ConfigModel}(n/k,d,6\delta)
.

2:

if

any
C_{i}
contains fewer than
4\delta dn
red edges

then

3:

return
\bot
.

4:

end

if

5:

for

i=1\text{ to }k

do

6:

repeat

\frac{\delta dn}{deg\cdot k}
times

7:

# (Under
D_{1}
,
deg=2
with
\delta dn/2k
edges b/w adjacent clusters)

8:

# (Under
D_{2}
,
deg=4
with
\delta dn/4k
edges b/w adjancent clusters)

9:

for

j\in N_{b}(i),j>i

do

10:

Pick a random red edge in
e\in C_{i}
and a random red edge
f\in C_{j}
.

11:

Uncolor
e,f

12:

\textsc{CrossEdges}(e,f)
.

13:

end

for

14:

end

for

15:

Uncolor all red edges.

16:

Pick
\sigma\in S_{n}
and randomly permute
V
.

17:

return
G
.

Algorithm 10

\textsc{CrossEdges}(e,f)

1:

Let
e=(a,b),f=(c,d)
.

2:

Replace
e,f
with
e^{\prime}=(a,c)
and
f^{\prime}=(b,d)
.

Note that the graphs sampled from
D_{1}
(resp
D_{2}
) consist of
k
clusters sampled from the configuration model.
Graphs sampled from
D_{b}
(for
b\in\{1,2\}
) are obtained in the following two steps.
First, line
1
of Algorithm
9
replaces
i\in V(H_{b})
by the cluster
C_{i}
.
Next, for
(i,j)\in E(H_{b})
we have
\Theta(\delta dn/k)
edges running between
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
according
to line
5
in Algorihtm
9
(with high probability). In particular, this
means that in graphs sampled from
D_{1}
, the
k
clusters sampled from the configuration model are arranged
in a cycle of size
k
where every pair of
successive clusters have
\Theta(\delta dn/k)
edges running between them. On the other hand, in graphs
sampled from
D_{2}
these clusters are arranged according to the graph
H_{2}
and corresponding to two
adjacent vertices in
H_{2}
, we have two connected clusters with
\Theta(\delta dn/k)
edges between them
with high probability.

Having defined the distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, we now state the main lemma we prove in this section.
Once properties of the distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are developed, Lemma
17

follows as an immediate corollary from this lemma.
We consider deterministic algorithms which make a total of
Q
queries among which some
s\leq Q

initial queries are cluster queries (and ask for the identity of the cluster to which a vertex sampled
uniformly at random belongs).
These
s
vertices, whose clusters are revealed by the clustering oracle, are called
tagged
.

Lemma 18
.

Fix
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
\varphi=\varphi(d)
such that for all

\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
such that any deterministic

Q
-query algorithm
A
which distinguishes between graphs drawn from
D_{1}(n,d,\epsilon,k)

and
D_{2}(n,d,\epsilon,k)
with probability at least
2/3
requires at
least
\Omega(\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{k}})

queries in
RandomSampleModel
.

We first show that the procedures
SampleFromD1
and
SampleFromD2

produce graphs which are
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable with good probability:

Claim 2
.

Let
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi(d),\varphi=\varphi(d-1)

such that for all
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
such that the clusters
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k}

obtained in Line
1
of
Algorithm
9
on calling
\textsc{SampleFromD1}(n,d,\epsilon,k)

(resp
\textsc{SampleFromD2}(n,d,\epsilon,k)
) satisfy the following with probability at least

1-3k\cdot\left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^{d/3}
:

•

Each
C_{i}
induces a
\varphi^{\prime}
expander.

•

For every
i\in[k]
, the number of red half edges in each
C_{i}
is between

4\epsilon dn/k
and
8\epsilon dn/k
(and thus
G\neq\bot
).

Moreover, conditioned on this event,
G
is
(k,\varphi/2,\epsilon)
-clusterable.

Proof.

Let
\varphi=\varphi(d-1),\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi^{\prime}(d)
be the lower bounds on inner
conductance of a graph sampled according to the configuration model (as stated in
Fact
2
). Now, fix any
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
.
By Fact
2
, for all
i\in[k]
,
C_{i}\sim\textsc{ConfigModel}(n/k,d,6\epsilon)
,
it follows that each
C_{i}
induces a graph with

\phi(C_{i})\geq\varphi^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
.
By a union bound over all the
k
clusters, all
C_{i}
’s induce a graph with
\phi(C_{i})\geq\varphi^{\prime}

with probability at least
1-k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
.
Next, note that Algorithm
9
returns
\bot
only when some cluster
C_{i}
contains very
few red half edges. Fix some
i\in[k]
. Thus, in expectation each
C_{i}
contains
6\epsilon dn/k

red half edges. Call a cluster
C_{i}
bad if it contains fewer than
4\epsilon dn/k

or more than
8\epsilon dn/k
red half edges. By a Chernoff bound, for a fixed
i\in[k]
,
C_{i}
is bad
with probability at most
2\exp(-\epsilon dn/3k)
. By a union bound over
all the
k
clusters, the probability that any cluster is bad is at most

2k\cdot\exp(-\epsilon dn/3k)
. Thus, both the above events occur with probability at least

1-k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}-2k\cdot\exp(-\epsilon dn/3k)\geq 1-3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}.

Note that conditioned on this event, for each
i\in[k]
, Algorithm
9
each

C_{i}
has
\epsilon dn/k
edges leaving it. This means
for every
i\in[k]
,
\phi^{G}_{\text{out}}(C_{i})\leq\epsilon
. We now lower bound
\phi^{G}_{\text{in}}(C_{i})
.
To this end, consider the graph
G_{2}
obtained as follows: for every
i\in[k]
, replace all the
half edges that arrived in the second round of the
ConfigModel
(Definition
16
)
by self loops. Observe that

\forall i\in[k],\phi^{G_{2}}_{\text{in}}(C_{i})\leq\phi^{G}_{\text{in}}(C_{i}).

Thus, it suffices to lower bound
\phi^{G_{2}}_{\text{in}}(C_{i})
. To this end, we first consider
the graph
G_{1}
which is formed after round 1.
In
G_{1}
each
C_{i}
is distributed according to
\textsc{ConfigModel}(n/k,d-1,0)
.
This means, in
G_{1}
each
C_{i}
induces a graph with expansion at least
\varphi^{\prime}
. For any
i\in[k]
,
and for any
S\subseteq C_{i}
, note that

\phi^{G_{2}}_{C_{i}}(S)\geq\frac{E_{1}(S,\overline{(}S))}{d|S|}\geq\frac{\varphi\cdot(d-1)|S|}{d|S|}.

This is at least
\varphi/2
for
d\geq 8
. Thus, for any
i\in[k]
, we indeed have

\phi^{G_{2}}_{\text{in}}(C_{i})\geq\varphi/2
.
This means the final graph
G
obtained in Line
17
of Algorithm

9
is
(k,\varphi/2,\epsilon)
-clusterable as desired.
∎

We first show that, with high probability, graphs sampled from
D_{1}
have small Dasgupta Cost.

Lemma 19
.

Fix
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
\varphi=\varphi(d)
such that for all

\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}

such that a graph
G=(V,E)
obtained by calling

\textsc{GenerateSampleD1}(\epsilon)
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable and

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq O\left(\max\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\log k}{k},\frac{dn^{2}}{k}\right)\right)

with probability at least
1-3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
.

Proof.

Use Fact
2
and set
\varphi=\varphi(d)
accordingly. Take any
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
.
With probability at least
1-3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
, the graph
G
obtained by

\textsc{SampleFromD1}(n,d,\epsilon,k)
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.
Recall that the contraction of
G
with respect to

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k}
is the graph
H_{1}
which is a copy of cycle on
k
vertices.
Thus, for all
i\in[k]
, there are
\epsilon dn/k
edges between
C_{i}
and
C_{i+1}
where
indices are taken modulo
k
.
Let
T^{*}
denote a tree with optimal Dasgupta cost on
G
. In what follows, we now
describe a cluster respecting tree (Definition
15
)
T
on
G
.
We denote the corresponding contracted subtree (Definition
15
)
of
T
as
T^{\prime}
. We note

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\leq\text{WCOST}_{G}(T^{\prime})+\sum d|C_{i}|^{2}=\text{WCOST}_{G}(T^{\prime})+d\cdot(n/k)^{2}\cdot k,

(53)

where the second term is a trivial upper bound on the sum of Dasgupta costs of the clusters, and
\text{WCOST}_{G}(T^{\prime})

refers to the weighted Dasgupta Cost of
T^{\prime}
on
G
(Definition
10
).
We now describe how to generate the tree
T^{\prime}
.
The root cut separates
C_{1}\cup C_{2}\cup\ldots C_{k/2}
from the remaining vertices, which is achieved
by deleting
2\epsilon dn/k
edges and thus this cut contributes a total of
2\epsilon\frac{dn^{2}}{k}

to
\text{WCOST}_{G}(T^{\prime})
. Subsequent levels in the tree are obtained by recursively
splitting these nodes into two balanced sets. In particular, there are
2^{i}
nodes in the tree at
the
i
-th level where each node
j\in[2^{i}]
contains clusters

C_{(j-1)\cdot 2^{i}+1},\cdots,C_{j\cdot 2^{i}}
. Thus, the contribution to Dasgupta Cost from this
level is
\epsilon\frac{dn}{k}\cdot\frac{n}{2^{i}}\cdot 2^{i}=\epsilon\frac{dn^{2}}{k}
. This means

\text{WCOST}_{G}(T^{\prime})\leq 2\frac{\epsilon dn^{2}}{k}+\frac{\epsilon dn^{2}}{k}\log k\leq\frac{2\epsilon dn^{2}\log k}{k}.

Together with Equation (
53
), this gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\leq\text{COST}_{G}(T)\leq O\left(\max\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn^{2}\cdot\log k}{k},\frac{dn^{2}}{k}\right)\right)
.
∎

Next, we show that, with high probability, graphs sampled from
D_{2}
have a large Dasgupta Cost.

Lemma 20
.

Fix
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists
\varphi=\varphi(d)
such that for all

\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}

such that a graph
G=(V,E)
obtained by calling

\textsc{GenerateSampleD2}(\epsilon)
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable
and
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\geq\Omega\left(\min(\epsilon\cdot dn^{2},dn^{2}/k)\right)
with probability at least

1-3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
.

Proof.

Use Fact
2
and set
\varphi=\varphi(d)
accordingly. Take any
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
.
With probability at least
1-3k\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
, the graph
G
obtained by

\textsc{SampleFromD2}(n,d,\epsilon,k)
is
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.
Recall that the contraction of
G
with respect to

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots C_{k}
is the graph
H_{2}
which is a copy of some
4
-regular
expander with
\phi(H_{2})=\alpha\geq\Omega(1)
. Recall that according to Corollary

3
, any Dasgupta tree for
G
(in particular a optimum
Dasgupta tree) has cost at least

\frac{\varphi}{20}\cdot d\sum_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|^{2}=\frac{\varphi}{20}\cdot dn^{2}/k
.
Now, we show that the cost of an optimum Dasgupta tree for
G
is also at least
\Omega(\epsilon dn^{2})
.
Let
T
denote a cluster respecting tree for
G
which satisfies

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T)

which we know exists as per Lemma
15
. Also note that according
to this lemma, the contraction
T^{\prime}
of this tree with respect to
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k})

is a binary tree with
k
leaves (which correspond to
V(H_{2})
). We show

\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\Omega(\alpha\epsilon dn^{2})
which in turn lower bounds
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
.

We do this by lower bounding
\text{COST}_{G}(T)
by just the contribution to this cost from some single cut induced
by
T
in
G
.
Take some internal node
x
in the contraction
T^{\prime}
of
T
which corresponds to a set
B_{x}\subseteq[k]
.
Suppose the children of
x
in
T^{\prime}
are
y
and
z
. Then since
T
is cluster respecting, it holds
that
B_{y}\cup B_{z}=B_{x}
where
B_{x}
contains the set of vertices of
H_{2}
that are leaves in
H_{2}

descending from
x
(and
B_{y}
denotes the analogous set of vertices in
H_{2}
descending from
y
).
Take a cut
(B,\overline{B})
induced by the tree
T^{\prime}
in the graph

H_{2}
. We denote the edges crossing this cut as
E_{2}(B,\overline{B})
.
We can lower bound
\text{COST}_{G}(T)
as

\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq|E_{2}(B,\overline{B})|\cdot\frac{\epsilon dn}{4k}\cdot\min(|B|,|\overline{B}|)\cdot\frac{n}{k}.

(54)

We now produce a good choice for
B
which leads to the desired lower bound.
Since the contraction
T^{\prime}
of
T
is a binary tree,
by Fact
1
, there exists an edge
(u,v)\in T^{\prime}
which satisfies that on deleting
the edge
(u,v)
, the number of edges in the component of
T^{\prime}
containing
u
and the component
containing
v
is at most
2|V(T^{\prime})|/3
. Denote these components as
T^{\prime}_{u}
and
T^{\prime}_{v}
and suppose

|V(T^{\prime})|/3\leq|T^{\prime}_{u}|\leq|T^{\prime}_{v}|
. Thus,

|T^{\prime}_{u}|\geq\frac{|V(T^{\prime})|}{3}\geq\frac{1}{3}\cdot\left(2|V(H_{2})|-1\right)\geq 0.6k.

We let
B=T^{\prime}_{u}
. Since
T^{\prime}
is a binary tree, we have
|B|\geq 0.3k
.
Now, take any edge
(x,y)\in E_{2}(B,\overline{B})
and denote the LCA of
(x,y)
in
T^{\prime}
by
w
.
Note that the number of leaves in
T^{\prime}
which descend from
w
is at least
|B|\geq 0.3k
.
Now recall, the leaves of
T^{\prime}
are exactly the vertices of
H_{2}
. And since
H_{2}
is an
\alpha
-expander,
we have

|E_{2}(B,\overline{B})|\geq\alpha\cdot 4|B|.

Substituting this in Equation (
54
), together with
|B|\geq 0.3k
,
we get
\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\frac{9}{25}\alpha\epsilon dn^{2}
as desired.
As noted earlier, this gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\Omega(\varphi\alpha\epsilon dn^{2}).

By Lemma
15
, it holds that

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\geq\Omega(\varphi\cdot\alpha\cdot\epsilon dn^{2})
. The claim follows by observing that
As noted earlier, this gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\geq\Omega(\varphi)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T)\geq\Omega(\varphi\alpha\epsilon dn^{2}).

By Lemma
15
, it holds that

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*})\geq\Omega(\varphi\cdot\alpha\cdot\epsilon dn^{2})
. The claim follows by observing that

\alpha\geq\Omega(1)
and
\varphi\geq\Omega_{d}(1)
(as noted in Fact
2
).
∎

We now turn to the proof of Lemma
18
. First, it will be convenient to establish
our lower bounds for the following augmented query model.

Definition 17
.

(Augmented Query Model)
When an algorithm issues an edge query
(v_{q},i_{q})
requesting the
i_{q}
-th edge
incident on
v_{q}
it gets at unit cost access to all the
d
neighbors of
v_{q}
.

Take any
Q
-query determenistic algorithm
A
. Suppose it gets
s\leq Q
tagged vertices
at the beginning of its execution. One can express its behavior as follows.
At any stage, it has explored the adjacency of
q\leq Q
vertices,
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots v_{q}
.
It can choose to further explore the adjacency list of some of the revealed (but unqueried)
vertex
v^{\prime}
. Note that
v^{\prime}
is an arbitrary (but fixed) function of all the vertices and edges
seen so far. Thus, the output returned by
A
(whether
G\sim D_{1}
or
G\sim D_{2}
) depends
only on the adjacencies of
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots v_{Q}
. We refer to the subgraph of
G
explored
by a
Q
-query algorithm
A
as the
transcript
of
A
on
G
and this is denoted as

\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{Q}(A,G)
. We define the following two distributions.

Definition 18
.

(Distribution over transcripts under
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
)
Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be distributions on
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs
as defined earlier. Let
A
be any
Q
-query deterministic algorithm.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{Q}
denote the set of all graphs with maximum degree at most
d
on
Q
vertices.
We define the distribution
P_{1}^{A}
as follows.

\forall H\in\mathcal{G}_{Q},P_{1}^{A}(H)=Pr_{G\sim D_{1}}(A\text{ on input $G$ obtains $H$ as transcript}).

Similarly, define the distribution
P_{2}
as:

\forall H\in\mathcal{G}_{Q},P_{2}^{A}(H)=Pr_{G\sim D_{2}}(A\text{ on input $G$ obtains $H$ as transcript}).

We now prove the following claim which shows that the transcript obtained by a low query algorithm

A
on input
G
drawn from either
D_{1}
or
D_{2}
is likely to be cycle free. We build up on this
and argue that conditioned on the transcript being cycle free, the distributions over the transcripts

P_{1}^{A},P_{2}^{A}
obtained by a low query algorithm are statistically close and cannot be told apart.

Claim 3
.

Let
d\geq 8,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist
\varphi=\varphi(d)
such that for all
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}

such that the transcript obtained by any determenistic
Q
-query algorithm
A

(where
Q\leq\frac{1}{100d}\sqrt{n/k}
) on input
a graph
G
drawn from the following distribution

D=\begin{cases}D_{1}(n,d,\epsilon,k)\text{with prob $1/2$}\\
D_{2}(n,d,\epsilon,k)\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

satisfies the following with probability
1-O\left(\frac{Q^{2}d^{2}}{n/k}\right)
:
all connected components of
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{Q}(A,G)
are acyclic and contain at most one tagged vertex.

Proof.

Use Fact
2
and set
\varphi=\varphi(d)
accordingly. Take any
\epsilon\ll\varphi^{2}
.
By Claim
2
with probability at most
p\leq 3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
,
either
G=\bot
or
G
fails to be
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.
We condition on avoiding these bad events. We show this result under the augmented query model.
Suppose the
Q
-query algorithm
A
gets
s\leq Q
tagged vertices at
the start of execution. We will prove the claimed properties of the transcript for
G\sim D_{1}

by induction on
Q-s
which is the number of queries still available to
A
.
(The argument for
G\sim D_{2}
is similar and we skip it). First, we setup our induction hypothesis.
To this end, for
s\leq q\leq Q
, define the event

\mathcal{E}_{q}=\text{ACYC}_{q}\land\text{TAG}_{q}.

Here
\text{ACYC}_{q}
is the event that the subgraph
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q}(A,G)
is cycle free and
\text{TAG}_{q}

is the event that all the components in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q}(A,G)
contain at most one tagged vertex.
Suppose for some
s\leq q\leq Q
, all previous events
\mathcal{E}_{s},\mathcal{E}_{s+1},\ldots\mathcal{E}_{q-1}

have occured. We write

\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1}=\mathcal{E}_{s}\land\mathcal{E}_{s+1}\land\ldots\land\mathcal{E}_{q-1}.

We will show by induction on
s\leq q\leq Q
that

\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{q}|\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1)}\geq 1-\frac{20qd^{2}}{n/k}.

For the base case, consider
q=s
. Note that
\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{s}|\mathcal{E}_{\leq s-1})=1

holds with probability
1
because the transcript so far consists only of the
s
vertices
and does not have any edges. Now consider the case where
s<q\leq Q
. Two cases arise.

Case 1: The
q
-th query is a vertex query:
In this case, by randomness of
\sigma
in
Line
16
,
A
obtains a uniformly random vertex
u\in V\setminus\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
.
Thus, in this case,
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q}(A,G)
continues to satisfy the induction hypothesis.

Case 2: The
q
-th query is an adjacency query:
In this case,
A
asks for all
the neighbors of some vertex
v_{q}\in\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
. Note that the number of
vertices and edges in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
is at most
qd
. We use principle of deferred
decisions while revealing the neighborhood of
v_{q}
. Let
i\in[k]
be such that

v_{q}\in C_{i}
. Let
H
denote the set of vertices in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
which
belong to the cluster
C_{i}
(the same cluster which contains
v_{q}
). Note that the total number
of half edges incident to vertices in
C_{i}
is
dn/k
. Letting
E_{q-1}
denote the set of edges in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}
,
we see that the number of half edges paired so far is at most
2\cdot|E_{q-1}|=2qd
.
Fix a vertex
u\in\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
. Note that the number of unpaired half edges incident on both

v_{q},u
is at most
d
. Now, we bound the probability that over the randomness of the instance,
the vertex
v_{q}
(which is also random) forms an edge with vertex
u
. By deferred decisions, this
is seen to be

Pr\left((v_{q},u)\in E(G)|\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1}\right)\leq\frac{d^{2}}{dn/k-2qd}\leq\frac{dq^{2}}{dn/2k}\leq\frac{2kd}{n}.

Here the second last step follows because
q\leq Q\leq\sqrt{n}
.
By a union bound over all the
|H|\leq qd
vertices in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)\cap C_{i}
, we get

Pr\left(\exists u\in H\colon(v_{q},u)\in E(G)\right)\leq\frac{2kd|H|}{n}\leq\frac{2kq\cdot d^{2}}{n}.

Now consider the set
R
of remaining vertices in the transcript (which do not belong to
C_{i}
). Fix
any vertex
u\in R
. An edge is formed between
v_{q}
and
u
only if

•

v_{q}
and
u
both receive a red half edge (either of these events occur with probability
6\epsilon d
).

•

Conditioned on the above event, these red edges get randomly paired to be crossed in line
12

of Algorithm
9
.

Note that the number of red edges remaining in any cluster before the crossing step is at
least
4\epsilon dn-\epsilon/2dn\geq 3\epsilon dn
. If either
v_{q}
or
u
do not have any red edge
incident on it,
v_{q}
does not form an edge with
u
with probability
1
. Now, suppose both

v_{q}
and
u
have a red edge. By principle of deferred decisions,
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1}
, in any given iteration in line
5
of Algorithm

9
, this happens with probability at most

6\epsilon d\cdot 6\epsilon d\cdot\frac{1}{3\epsilon dn/k\cdot 3\epsilon dn/k}\leq\frac{4k^{2}}{n^{2}}
. Across all the
at most
3\epsilon dn/k
iterations, by a union bound, we have

Pr\left((v_{q},u)\in E(G)|\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1}\right)\leq\frac{4k^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot\frac{3\epsilon dn}{k}=\frac{12\epsilon d}{n/k}.

By a union bound, the probability
that there is an edge between
v_{q}
and some vertex in
R
is at most

\frac{12\epsilon d|R|}{n/k}\leq\frac{12\epsilon qd^{2}}{n/k}.

In all, this means conditioned on
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
being acyclic and having at most one
tagged vertex in each component, the probability that some neighbor of
v_{q}
ends up
in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
,

Pr(\overline{\mathcal{E}_{q}}|\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1})\leq\frac{14\epsilon qd^{2}}{n/k}\leq\frac{20\epsilon qd^{2}}{n/k}.

In particular, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{\leq q-1}
,
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q}(A,G)
satisfies the statement of the
induction hypothesis with probability at least
1-\frac{20\epsilon qd^{2}}{n/k}
.

Therefore, the final transcript fails to satisfy the statement of the induction hypothesis
with probability at most

p^{\prime}=\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\frac{20\epsilon q\cdot d^{2}}{n/k}\leq\frac{10Q^{2}\cdot d^{2}}{n/k}.

In particular, with probability at least

1-(p+p^{\prime})\geq 1-O\left(\frac{Q^{2}\cdot d^{2}}{n/k}\right)
all the components
of
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{Q}(A,G)
are acyclic and contain at most one tagged vertex.
∎

Now, we show our indistinguishability result using which Lemma
18
immediately follows.

Lemma 21
.

Let
A
be any
Q
-query deterministic algorithm where
Q\leq\frac{1}{100d}\sqrt{n/k}
.
Then, the total variation distance
\|P_{1}^{A}-P_{2}^{A}\|_{TV}\leq\frac{1}{10}
.

Proof.

Let
G\sim D
where
D
is either
D_{1}
or
D_{2}
with probability
1/2
each.
By Claim
2
, with probability
p_{1}\leq 3k\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d/3}
,

G
is the empty graph
\bot
or
G
fails to be
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable.
We condition on avoiding these bad events.
From Claim
3
, we know that with probability

p_{2}\leq O\left(\frac{Q^{2}d^{2}}{n/k}\right)
,
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{Q}(A,G)
either contains cycles
or has a connected component with more than one tagged vertex. Suppose none of these
bad events occur. Since the
q
-th query of
A
only depends on
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
, whenever
A
issues
a vertex query (as say the
q
-th query), by the randomness of
\sigma
in Line

16
of Algorithm
9
,
A
obtains a uniformly random vertex

u\in V\setminus\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
. It follows that for
q\leq Q
, conditioned on the
v_{q}

(the vertex in the
q
-th edge query issued by
A
) having no neighbors
in
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{q-1}(A,G)
, the transcript is distributed identically
regardless of whether
G
is sampled from
D_{1}
or
D_{2}
. Thus, it holds that

\|P_{1}^{A}-P_{2}^{A}\|_{TV}\leq p_{1}+p_{2}\leq O\left(\frac{Q^{2}d^{2}}{n/k}\right).

For
Q\leq 1/d\cdot O(\sqrt{n/k})
, this is at most
1/10
as desired.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Lemma
18
.

Proof.

(Of Lemma
18
)
We note that
A
is a deterministic
Q
-query algorithm. It is given as input
a graph
G\sim D
where
D=D_{1}
or
D=D_{2}
with probability
1/2
each. It is
required to correctly output whether
D=D_{1}
or
D=D_{2}
. By Lemma
21

we note that the distribution
P_{1}^{A}
on the transcripts obtained by
A
on
G\sim D_{1}

is statistically
1/10
close to the distribution
P_{2}^{A}
.
A
being deterministic, the
output of
A
(which declares whether
D=D_{1}
or
D=D_{2}
) is an arbitrary but fixed
function of
\text{TRANSCRIPT}_{Q}(A,G)
. Let
p_{1}=Pr_{G\sim D_{1}}(A\text{ outputs }D_{1})
and

p_{2}=Pr_{G\sim D_{2}}(A\text{ outputs }D_{1})
. By closeness of
P_{1}^{A},P_{2}^{A}
, we have

|p_{1}-p_{2}|\leq 1/10
. Now, the probability that
A
gives an incorrect response
is

\displaystyle Pr_{G\sim D}(A\text{ is wrong})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}Pr_{G\sim D_{1}}(A\text{ outputs }D_{2})+\frac{1}{2}Pr_{G\sim D_{2}}(A\text{ outputs }D_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1-p_{1})+\frac{1}{2}p_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(p_{2}-p_{1})

\displaystyle\geq 9/20

\displaystyle>1/3

Thus, the algorithm
A
is likely to make an error in distinguishing between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with
probability strictly larger than
1/3
. Contradiction.
∎

And finally, we prove Lemma
17
.

Proof.

(Of Lemma
17
)
Use
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
as the distributions proposed in Lemma
18
.
Any
Q
-query determenistic algorithm in the
RandomSampleModel

is a
Q
-query deterministic algorithm for which receives
s\leq Q
tagged samples.
By Lemma
18
, any such algorithm cannot distinguish between

D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with probability at least
2/3
. The result follows.
∎

6
Approximating Dasgupta Cost in the Oracle Model With Cluster Queries

In this section, we present algorithms to approximate Dasgupta Cost of a

(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph where we are allowed to query the
cluster a vertex belongs to. In particular, we prove the following theorem.

See
5

The preceding theorem asserts a tighter approximation to Dasgupta Cost of
G
and bypasses the

\widetilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{n})
lower bound asserted in Theorem
4
thanks to the stronger
query model. The key to this speed up is a different (and faster) approximation to
the contracted graph
H=G/\mathcal{C}
. After this step, we use recursive sparsest cut on
H
as before
to obtain an approximation to
\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})
. We now present our new algorithm which approximates
the weighted adjacency matrix of the contracted graph
H
.

Algorithm 11

\textsc{ApproxContractedGraphOM}(G,\mathcal{D})

# Suppose
\widehat{c_{1}}\leq\widehat{c_{2}}\leq\ldots\leq\widehat{c_{k}}

1:

for

1\leq i<j\leq k

do

2:

s\leftarrow\frac{10^{11}k^{9}\log n\cdot\log k}{\eta\epsilon^{2}}
.

3:

Pick
s
random vertices from
V
and call the set
S
.

4:

Find
S_{i}=S\cap C_{i}
using the clustering oracle.

5:

Set
X_{ij}=0
.

6:

for

u\in S_{i}

do

7:

Pick random
v\in N(u)
.

8:

if

v\in C_{j}

then

9:

X_{ij}=X_{ij}+1

10:

end

if

11:

end

for

12:

Set
\widetilde{W}(i,j)=\frac{X_{ij}}{|S_{i}|}\cdot d\cdot\widehat{c_{i}}
.

13:

end

for

14:

\widetilde{H}\leftarrow\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)
.

15:

return
\widetilde{H}

Now, we present the procedure
EstimatedCostOM
.

Algorithm 12

\textsc{EstimatedCostOM}(G,\mathcal{D})

1:

\widetilde{H}\leftarrow\textsc{ApproxContractedGraphOM}(G,\mathcal{D})

# Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)

2:

\widetilde{Q}:=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}

3:

Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm subject to balanced constraints (introduced by
[
CC17
]
).

4:

\textsc{EST}_{\text{OM}}\leftarrow 3\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{3\epsilon dn^{2}}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}

5:

return
\textsc{EST}_{\text{OM}}

Finally, we present the procedure
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
below. Notice that this procedure
calls
\textsc{EstimateClusterSize}(G,i,\delta)
(Algorithm
16
). In
RandomSampleModel
,
we used the same procedure to estimate set sizes. We note that this procedure can be used with

OracleModel
as well.

Algorithm 13

InitializeOracleOM(
G
)

1:

\delta\leftarrow\frac{1}{100\cdot k}

2:

\widehat{c_{i}}\leftarrow\textsc{EstimateClusterSize}(G,i,\delta)
, for every
i\in[k]

# Algorithm
16

3:

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\{\widehat{c_{i}}\}_{i\in[k]}

4:

return
\mathcal{D}

The following claim follows as an immediate corollary of Lemma
27
.

Claim 4
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller
than a positive sufficiently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph
that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let

\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
\delta=\frac{1}{100k}
.
Then the procedure
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
(Algorithm
13
)
runs in time
O\left(\frac{\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}\right)
and with
probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for every
i\in[k]
returns a value

\widehat{c_{i}}
such that
\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm\delta)\cdot|C_{i}|
.

Next, we prove in Claim
5
that for all pairs of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
, the preceding
routine estimates
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
to within good accuracy as long as

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{100k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)
. We first make
a definition.

Definition 19
.

For a fixed
i\in[k]
, we let
|E_{out}(C_{i})|=\sum_{j\neq i}|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

denote the total number of edges that leave
C_{i}
. For a pair of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
,
if
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{100k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)

we refer to as the pair
C_{i},C_{j}
being
visible
. A pair
C_{i},C_{j}

which has fewer edges is called
invisible
.

Claim 5
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}

be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that
admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
. Condition on

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
(Algorithm
13
) succeeding (which happens
with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
). Then with probability at least
1-2/n^{8}
, for all
visible pairs
C_{i},C_{j}
of clusters, the following
holds: The
\textsc{ApproxContractedGraphOM}(G,\mathcal{D})
procedure
(Algorithm
11
) obtains an estimate
\widetilde{W}(i,j)
to
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

where
\left|\widetilde{W}(i,j)-|E(C_{i},C_{j})|\right|\leq 0.01|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

Proof.

Fix a pair of visible clusters
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
with
|C_{i}|\leq|C_{j}|
.
By definition of a visible pair, we have

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|=\epsilon_{ij}\cdot d|C_{i}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{100k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{i}|
.
Consider the following two cases.

Case 1:

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
.

Case 2:

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{j}}>\widehat{c_{i}}
.
Since we condition on
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
succesfully estimating size
of each cluster to good accuracy, this means
|C_{i}|>0.9|C_{j}|
. Thus, it holds that

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{100k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot 0.9\cdot d|C_{j}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{200k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{j}|
.

We consider the case where
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
.
(The other case, where
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes the other way round is completely
analogous and so we skip this case.)
From the preceding discussion
C_{i},C_{j}
