, we know

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{200k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}d|C_{i}|.

We now show how Algorithm
11
obtains a good estimate to
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

for this pair. Since,
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
, when the procedure

ApproxContractedGraphOM
processes the pair
C_{i},C_{j}
, it finds the samples which come
from
C_{i}
in Line
4
. Write

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|=\epsilon_{ij}\cdot d|C_{i}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{200k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{i}|

Conditioned on
InitializeOracleOM
being successful, we have

\widehat{c_{i}}\geq 0.99|C_{i}|\geq 0.99\cdot\eta\max_{\ell\in[k]}|C_{\ell}|\geq\frac{\eta}{2k}n.

(55)

Thus, on choosing
s=\frac{10^{11}k^{9}\log n\cdot\log k}{\eta\epsilon^{2}}
uniform samples from
V
,
by a Chernoff Bound, with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
, we have

|S_{i}|=s_{i}\geq\frac{10^{10}k^{8}\log n\cdot\log k}{\epsilon^{2}}.

Note that all vertices in the set

S_{i}
obtained in Line
4
are distributed uniformly inside
C_{i}
. Further,
since
G
is
d
-regular, the edge
(u,v)
obtained on taking a random
v\sim N(u)

is also distributed as a uniformly random edge in
G
. Therefore, the probability that
v\sim N(u)

ends up in
C_{j}
is exactly
\epsilon_{ij}
. The random variable
X_{ij}
in Line
9

tracks the number of seen edges which cross the cut
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
.
Let
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}=\frac{X_{ij}}{s_{i}}
and
\gamma=\frac{\epsilon}{20000k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}
. Note that
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}]=\epsilon_{ij}
.
By an additive Chernoff Bound,

Pr(\left|\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}]\right|\geq\gamma)\leq\exp(-\gamma^{2}s_{i})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\cdot 10^{8}k^{8}\cdot\log k}\cdot\frac{10^{10}k^{8}\log n\cdot\log k}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\leq n^{-10}.

Since,
\epsilon_{ij}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{200k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}
, it holds that
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}\in(0.999\epsilon_{ij},1.001\epsilon_{ij})
.
Finally, Line
12
sets
\widetilde{W}(i,j)=\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}\cdot d\cdot\widehat{c_{i}}
. Recalling,

\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm 1/100k)|C_{i}|
, it follows that

\widetilde{W}(i,j)\in(0.99,1.01)\cdot|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

holds with probability at least

1-n^{-10}-n^{-100}\geq 1-2n^{-10}
for any pair of visible clusters. Therefore, by
a union bound over all pairs of
visible clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
(of which there is at most
k^{2}
), with probability at least

1-2/n^{8}
,

\widetilde{W}(i,j)\in(0.99,1.01)\cdot|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

holds.
∎

We show another claim which shows that for any invisible pair
C_{i},C_{j}
of clusters,
\widetilde{W}(i,j)

is not too large with high probability.

Claim 6
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}

be smaller than a positive suffciently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that
admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
. Condition on

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
(Algorithm
4
) succeeding (which happens
with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
). Then with probability at least
1-2/n^{8}
, for all
invisible pairs
C_{i},C_{j}
of clusters, the following
holds: The
\textsc{ApproxContractedGraphOM}(G,\mathcal{D})
procedure
(Algorithm
11
) obtains an estimate
\widetilde{W}(i,j)
to
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|

where
\widetilde{W}(i,j)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{50k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)
.

Proof.

Fix a pair of invisible clusters
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
with
|C_{i}|\leq|C_{j}|
.
By definition of an invisible pair, we have

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|=\epsilon_{ij}\cdot d|C_{i}|<\frac{\epsilon}{100k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{i}|
.
Two cases arise.

Case 1:

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
.

Case 2:

\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{j}}>\widehat{c_{i}}
.
In this case, recall
|C_{i}|>0.9|C_{j}|
.
Therefore, it holds that
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|<\frac{\epsilon}{90k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{i}|
.

We consider case 1 first where
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
.
(The other case, where
\textsc{InitializeOracleOM}(G)
concludes the other way is analogous). Write

|E(C_{i},C_{j})|=\epsilon_{ij}\cdot d|C_{i}|<\frac{\epsilon}{90k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d|C_{i}|

The rest of the argument is very similar to the preceding proof. Like before,
choose
s=\frac{10^{11}k^{9}\log n\cdot\log k}{\eta\epsilon^{2}}
uniform samples from
V
,
by a Chernoff Bound, with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
, we have

|S_{i}|=s_{i}\geq\frac{10^{10}k^{8}\log n\cdot\log k}{\epsilon^{2}}.

Again just like before note that all vertices
in the set
S_{i}
obtained in Line
4
are distributed uniformly inside
C_{i}
. Further,
since
G
is
d
-regular, the edge
(u,v)
obtained on taking a random
v\sim N(u)

is also distributed as a uniformly random edge in
G
. Therefore, the probability that
v\sim N(u)

ends up in
C_{j}
is exactly
\epsilon_{ij}
. Line
9
tracks, using
the random variable
X_{ij}
, the number of seen edges which cross the cut
|E(C_{i},C_{j})|
. Let

\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}=\frac{X_{ij}}{s_{i}}
and
\gamma=\frac{\epsilon}{20000k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}
. Note that

\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}]=\epsilon_{ij}
.

If
\epsilon_{ij}=\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon}{90k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}
, a Chernoff Bound gives

Pr(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{80k^{4}})\leq n^{-10}
. For
\epsilon_{ij}<\epsilon_{0}
, a probability bound
of at most
n^{-10}
follows by a coupling argument (Proposition
1
).
Finally, Line
12
sets

\widetilde{W}(i,j)=\widetilde{\epsilon}_{ij}\cdot d\cdot\widehat{c_{i}}
. Recalling,
\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm 1/100k)|C_{i}|
,
it follows that

\widetilde{W}(i,j)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{80k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\left(|C_{i}|+\frac{|C_{i}|}{100k}\right)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{50k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot|C_{i}|

holds with probability at least

1-n^{-10}-n^{-100}\geq 1-2n^{-10}
for any pair of invisible clusters.
Therefore, by a union bound over all
pairs of invisible clusters, it holds with probability at least
1-2n^{-8}
that

\widetilde{W}_{ij}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{50k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)
.

∎

We now recall some setup we used earlier to obtain good approximations to Dasgupta
Cost of an optimum tree on
G
. Let
Q=([k],\binom{[k]}{2},W,w)
denote the vertex weighted contraction of
G

with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}
and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted
Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],\binom{[k]}{2},\widetilde{W}\right)

denote the weighted graph returned by Algorithm
11
and let

\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],\binom{[k]}{2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
where

\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
. Let
\widetilde{T}
denote the tree constructed on
\widetilde{Q}

by using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm. As before, we estimate Dasgupta cost of

G
as

\textsc{EST}_{\text{OM}}=3\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{3\epsilon dn^{2}}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}.

It is convenient to have the following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

Let
Q,\widetilde{Q}
be as defined above. Condition on
InitializeOracleOM
being succesful
in estimating all the set sizes (which happes with probability at least
1-n^{-100}
).
Let
\widetilde{T}=\textsc{WRSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree
constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm. Then with
probability at least
1-1/n
we have

\frac{1}{3}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\frac{\epsilon}{100k\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}dn^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\frac{\epsilon}{k}dn^{2}\right).

Proof.

We first show that with probability at least
1-4/n^{8}\geq 1-1/n
, simultaneously for
all
S\subseteq[k]
, it holds that

0.99\cdot W(S,\overline{S})-\frac{\epsilon dn^{2}}{100k^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\leq 1.01\cdot W(S,\overline{S})+\frac{\epsilon dn^{2}}{50k^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}.

First, note that by Claim
5
, with probability at least
1-2/n^{8}
, simultaneuosly,
for all visible pairs
of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
, we have

\widetilde{W}(i,j)\in(0.99,1.01)\cdot|E(C_{i},C_{j})|.

Also, by Claim
6
, with probability at least
1-2/n^{8}
, simultaneously, for
all invisible pairs
C_{i},C_{j}
of clusters we have

\widetilde{W}(i,j)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{50k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot d\cdot\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)
.

From now on, we condition on these events. Now take any
S\subseteq[k]
and consider the
cut
(S,\overline{S})
. Let
VIS
and
INVIS
denote the set of visible and invisible
pairs of respectievely. Now, note

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}(S,\overline{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{VIS}}\widetilde{W}(i,j)+\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{INVIS}}\widetilde{W}(i,j)

\displaystyle\leq 1.01\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{VIS}}W(i,j)+\frac{\epsilon d}{50k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{INVIS}}\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)

By Claims
5
and
6

\displaystyle\leq 1.01W(S,\overline{S})+\frac{\epsilon dn}{50k^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}

(56)

Also note,

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}(S,\overline{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{VIS}}\widetilde{W}(i,j)+\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{INVIS}}\widetilde{W}(i,j)

\displaystyle\geq 0.99\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{VIS}}W(i,j)

By Claim
5

\displaystyle\geq 0.99W(S,\overline{S})-0.99\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{INVIS}}W(i,j)

\displaystyle\geq 0.99W(S,\overline{S})-0.99\frac{\epsilon\cdot d}{100k^{4}\cdot\sqrt{\log k}}\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in\text{INVIS}}\min(|C_{i}|,|C_{j}|)

By Claim
6

\displaystyle\geq 0.99W(S,\overline{S})-\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn}{100k^{2}\sqrt{\log k}}

(57)

Set
a=0.99,a^{\prime}=1.01,b=\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn}{50k^{2}\sqrt{\log k}},b^{\prime}=2b
.
Moreover, note that by Lemma
27
with probability at
least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have
\frac{1}{2}\cdot w(i)\leq\widetilde{w}(i)\leq 2\cdot w(i)
.
Now, apply Lemma
11
with these parameters. With probability at least

1-4/n^{8}\geq 1-1/n
, this gives

\frac{1}{3}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn^{2}}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\frac{\epsilon\cdot dn^{2}}{k}\right).

∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem
5
.

Proof.

(Of Theorem
5
)

Let
Q=G/\mathcal{C}
be the contraction of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{C}

(Definition
4
) and let
T^{*}_{Q}
denote an optimum weighted
Dasgupta tree for
Q
. Let
\widetilde{H}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W}\right)

be the graph obtained by
\textsc{ApproxContractedGraphOM}(G,\xi,\mathcal{D})

(Algorithm
11
), and let

\widetilde{Q}=\left([k],{[k]\choose 2},\widetilde{W},\widetilde{w}\right)
,
where for every
i\in[k]
,
\widetilde{w}(i)=\widehat{c_{i}}
. Let

\widetilde{T}=\textsc{RSC}(\widetilde{Q})
denote a hierarchical clustering tree
constructed on the graph
\widetilde{Q}
using the recursive sparsest cut algorithm.
Therefore, by Lemma
22
with probability at least
1-1/n
, we have

\frac{1}{3}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})-\frac{\epsilon}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}dn^{2}\leq\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})\leq O\left(\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\text{WCOST}_{Q}(T^{*}_{Q})+\frac{\epsilon}{k}dn^{2}\right)

(58)

Note that as per line (
4
) of Algorithm
12
we estimate the Dasgupta cost of
G
by

\textsc{EST}_{\text{OM}}=3\cdot\text{WCOST}_{\widetilde{Q}}(\widetilde{T})+\frac{3\epsilon dn^{2}}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}+d\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{c_{i}}^{2}\text{.}

(59)

Set
a=1/3,a^{\prime}=1
and set
b=\frac{\epsilon}{100k\sqrt{\log k}}\leq\frac{a}{k\sqrt{\log k}}
.
An application of Lemma
16
gives

\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\leq\textsc{EST}_{\text{OM}}\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log k}}{\varphi}\right)\cdot\text{COST}_{G}(T^{*}_{G})\text{.}

Running Time analysis:
First, we consider the running time of
InitializeOracleOM
.
This procedure just estimates
|C_{i}|
for each
i\in[k]
. Choosing
\delta=1/100k
, we
just call
EstimateClusterSize
procedure size each call to which takes time

t=\frac{400\cdot\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\delta^{2}}
. Thus the overall time taken by

InitializeOracleOM
procedure is

T_{\text{init}}=k\cdot t=O\left(\frac{k^{5}\cdot\log n}{\eta}\right)
.
Condition on
InitializeOracleOM
being successful (which is a high probability event).
Now, we upperbound the running time of
EstimatedCostOM
procedure. The major contributors
to running time for this process are calls to
ApproxContractedGraphOM
(
k^{2}
of those)
and the call to recursive sparsest cut procedure. Consider a pair of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
.
Take a pair of clusters
C_{i},C_{j}
considered in line
1
of the

ApproxContractedGraphOM
procedure (where suppose
\widehat{c_{i}}\leq\widehat{c_{j}}
).
This iteration of the loop takes

L_{ij}=O\left(\frac{k^{9}\cdot\log n\cdot\log k}{\eta\cdot\epsilon^{2}}\right)
samples
in all and finds the samples which come from
C_{i}
. Thereafter, it just returns the empirical
number of crossing
E(C_{i},C_{j})
edges seen scaled appropriately. Thus, the total
time taken by
ApproxContractedGraphOM
procedure in all is at most
k^{2}\cdot L_{ij}

which is at most
\frac{k^{11}\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\epsilon^{2}}
. The time taken by recursive sparsest
cut is
k^{O(1)}
which means the total time taken by
EstimatedCostOM
routine is
at most
\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\epsilon}\right)^{O(1)}
.
Finally, we bound the number of seed queries/oracle queries issued by this algorithm.
Note that according to line
2
of Algorithm
11
,
we have
s=\frac{10^{11}k^{9}\log n\cdot\log k}{\eta\epsilon^{2}}
. These are all the vertices
for which we ever issue oracle queries which means number of oracle queries issued is
at most
\left(\frac{k\log n}{\eta\cdot\epsilon}\right)^{O(1)}
as desired.
∎

For completeness, we prove Proposition
1
.

Proposition 1
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2}\ldots X_{s}\sim Ber(p)
denote
s
independent and identially distiributed
Bernoulli variables with parameter
p
. Let
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots Y_{s}\sim Ber(q)
denote
another
s
Bernoulli variables with rate
0\leq q<p\leq 1
. Then for any

t\leq s
, it holds that
Pr(\sum Y_{i}>t)\leq Pr(\sum X_{i}>t)
.

Proof.

The proof is a coupling argument. We consider a different experiment which
produces random variables
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots Z_{s}
where for any
i\in[s]

Z_{i}
has
the same distribution as
Z_{i}
and
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots Z_{s}
is a collection of independent
random variables. For
i\in[s]
,

•

If
Y_{i}=1
, set
Z_{i}=1
.

•

If
Y_{i}=0
, set
Z_{i}=1
with probability
\frac{p-q}{1-q}
.

Thus, each
Z_{i}\sim Ber(p)
as desired. Moreover, by construction, for any
t\leq s
,
we have
Pr(\sum X_{i}>t)=Pr(\sum Z_{i}>t)\geq Pr(\sum Y_{i}>t)
as desired.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
8

The main idea in proving Theorem
3

lies in a procedure which provides query access to (an approximate version) of the Laplacian
of the
contracted graph
(see Definition
4
). This is achieved by the
procedure
ApproxContractedGraph
(Algorithm
2
) which is presented
in Section
4.2
. We do
this in sublinear time by implementing a
QuadraticOracle
procedure which on input a
vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
returns an approximation
\alpha_{z}
to the quadratic form
z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z
where

\alpha_{z}\in\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z-\xi dn,\frac{3}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot z^{T}\mathcal{L}_{H}z+\xi dn\right)
.
In turn, this is achieved by implementing a weighted dot product oracle which we present in the next section.

A.1
Weighted Dot Product Oracle

The main result of this section is Theorem
8
.

See
8

Remark 4
.

This result is similar to Theorem 2 in
[
GKL+21
]
. The difference being Theorem 2 in

[
GKL+21
]
approximates dot product between the embedding vectors
\left\langle f_{x},f_{y}\right\rangle
.
Here, we instead want to approximate the weighted dot product
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle
.
Further, in the setup considered in
[
GKL+21
]
, all the clusters
C_{i}
have sizes
within a constant factor of each other; whereas in our setup, their sizes could differ
up to a
1/\eta
factor. These differences lead to a running time which is

\text{poly}(1/\eta)
factor larger than the running times obtained in Theorem 2 of
[
GKL+21
]
.

We also need to set up some notation which is used in this section. Let
m\leq n
be integers.
For any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
with singular value decomposition (SVD)

A=Y\Gamma Z^{T}
we assume
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
are orthogonal
matrices and
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a diagonal matrix of singular values. Since

Y
and
Z
are orthogonal matrices, their columns form an orthonormal basis. For any
integer
q\in[m]
we denote
Y_{[q]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
as the first
q
columns of
Y

and
Y_{-[q]}
to denote the matrix of the remaining columns of
Y
. We also denote

Z^{T}_{[q]}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n}
as the first
q
rows of
Z^{T}
and
Z^{T}_{-[q]}
to denote
the matrix of the remaining rows of
Z
. Finally we denote
\Gamma^{T}_{[q]}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}

as the first
q
rows and columns of
\Gamma
and we use
\Gamma_{-[q]}
as the last
n-q

rows and columns of
\Gamma
. So for any
q\in[m]
the span of
Y_{-[q]}
is the orthogonal
complement of the span of
Y_{[q]}
, also the span of
Z_{-[q]}
is the orthogonal
complement of the span of
Z_{[q]}
. Thus we can write

A=Y_{[q]}\Gamma_{[q]}Z^{T}_{[q]}+Y_{-[q]}\Gamma_{-[q]}Z^{T}_{-[q]}
.

Algorithm 14

InitializeWeightedDotProductOracle(
G,\xi
)

1:

t\leftarrow\frac{20\cdot\log n}{\varphi^{2}}

2:

R_{\text{init}}\leftarrow n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}

3:

s\leftarrow n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}

4:

I_{S}\leftarrow

s
indices chosen independently and uniformly at random with replacement from
\{1,\ldots,n\}

5:

{\widehat{Q}}\leftarrow\textsc{EstimateTransitionMatrix}(G,I_{S},R_{\text{init}},t)

#
\widehat{Q}
has at most
R_{\text{init}}\cdot s
non-zeros

6:

\mathcal{G}\leftarrow
EstimateCollisionProbabilities
(G,I_{S},R_{\text{init}},t)

7:

Compute eigendecomposition
\frac{n}{s}\cdot\mathcal{G}:=\widehat{W}\widehat{\Sigma}\widehat{W}^{T}
of
\frac{n}{s}\cdot\mathcal{G}

#
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}

8:

\Psi\leftarrow\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widehat{W}_{[k]}\widehat{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widehat{W}_{[k]}^{T}

#
\Psi\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}

9:

return
\mathcal{D}_{w}:=\{\Psi,\widehat{Q}\}

Algorithm 15

\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi,\mathcal{D})

#
\mathcal{D}_{w}:=\{\Psi,\widehat{Q}\}

1:

R_{\text{query}}\leftarrow n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\xi\cdot\eta}\right)^{O(1)}

2:

{\widehat{m}_{x}\leftarrow\textsc{RunRandomWalks}(G,R_{\text{query}},t+1,x)}

# unlike in
[
GKL+21
]
, walk length
=t+1

3:

{\widehat{m}_{y}\leftarrow\textsc{RunRandomWalks}(G,R_{\text{query}},t,y)}

4:

return
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}:=(\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\widehat{Q})\Psi(\widehat{Q}^{T}\widehat{m}_{y})

We build up on a collection of tools from
[
GKL+21
]
. First, we use Lemma
23

which shows that
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graphs, the outer products of the columns of
the
t
-step random walk transition matrix small spectral norm. This holds because
the matrix power dominate by the first
k
eigenvectors and each of them have bounded infinity norm.

Lemma 23
.

[
GKL+21
]

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular
graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
.
Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
.
Let
M
be the random walk transition matrix of
G
. Let
1>\xi>1/n^{5}
,

t\geq\frac{20\log n}{\varphi^{2}}
. Let
c>1
be a large enough constant and let
s\geq c\cdot k^{4}\cdot n^{(400\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\log n/(\eta^{2}\cdot\xi^{2})
. Let
I_{S}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
be a multiset of
s
indices chosen independently and uniformly at random from

\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
S
be the
n\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column equals
\mathds{1}_{i_{j}}
. Suppose that
M^{t}=U\Sigma^{t}U^{T}
is the eigendecomposition of
M^{t}
and
\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}
is the SVD of
\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S
.
If
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{10^{5}}
then with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

\left|\left|U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}-\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}\right|\right|_{2}<\xi

The following lemma from
[
GKL+21
]
is instrumental in analyzing collision probabilities
of random walks from every vertex
x\in V
in a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clusterable graph. In our setup
however, the collision probabilities increase by a factor of
1/\eta
because of uneven size
of clusters.

Lemma 24
.

[
GKL+21
]

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a

d
-regular and that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let

\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
.
Let
M
be the random walk transition matrix of
G
. For any
t\geq\frac{20\log n}{\varphi^{2}}

and any
x\in V
we have

\|M^{t}\mathds{1}_{x}\|_{2}\leq O\left(\frac{k}{\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot n^{-1/2+20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\right)\text{.}

To prove the correctness of weighted dot product of spectral embedding of vertices, we use the similar proof strategy to
[
GKL+21
]
,
which, albeit, develops an
estimator for the
unweighted
dot product between spectral embeddings i.e.,
\left\langle f_{x},f_{y}\right\rangle
. In Lemma
25
, we show that the weighted dot product of
spectral embeddings i.e.,
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle
can be estimated by the appropriate linear transformation of the random walk transition matrix. Since we seek weighted dot products unlike
[
GKL+21
]
, we run a
t
-step random walk from
x
, and a
t+1
-step walk from
y
. The one-step longer walk helps us to inject the matrix of eigenvalues in between the dot product of spectral embedding of vertex
x
and
y
.

Lemma 25
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer,
\varphi\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular
graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
. Let

\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
M
be the random walk
transition matrix of
G
. Let
1/n^{5}<\xi<1
,
t\geq\frac{20\log n}{\varphi^{2}}
. Let
c>1
be a
large enough constant and let

s\geq c\cdot n^{480\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{8}/(\eta^{4}\cdot{\xi}^{2})
.
Let
I_{S}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
be a multiset of
s
indices chosen independently and uniformly at random from

\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
S
be the
n\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column equals
\mathds{1}_{i_{j}}
.
Let
M^{t}=U\Sigma^{t}U^{T}
be the eigendecomposition of
M^{t}
and

\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}
be the SVD of

\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S
. If
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{10^{5}}
then with probability
at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

\left|\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}{U}_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}-(M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x})^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y})\right|\leq\frac{\xi}{n}\text{.}

Proof.

Let
m_{x}=M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x}
and
m_{y}=M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y}
. Let
c^{\prime}>1
be a large enough constant we will set later. Let
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi\cdot\eta}{c^{\prime}\cdot k^{2}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}
. Let
c_{1}
be the constant in front of
s
in Lemma
23
. Thus for large enough
c
we have
s\geq c\cdot n^{480\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{8}/(\eta^{4}\cdot{\xi}^{2})\geq c_{1}\cdot n^{400\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{4}/(\eta^{2}\cdot{\xi^{\prime}}^{2})
, and therefore by Lemma
23
applied with

\xi^{\prime}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

\left|\left|U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}-\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}\right|\right|_{2}\leq\xi^{\prime}

By Cauchy-Schwarz and submultiplicativity of the spectral norm we have

\displaystyle\left|m_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}-m_{x}^{T}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}-\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}\right|\right|_{2}\|m_{x}\|_{2}\|m_{y}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\xi^{\prime}\|m_{x}\|_{2}\|m_{y}\|_{2}

(60)

In the rest of the proof we will show
m_{x}^{T}(U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T})m_{y}=\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}
(
Step 1
) and
m_{x}^{T}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-2}_{[k]}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}=m_{x}^{T}(M^{t}S)(\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]})(M^{t}S)^{T}m_{y}
(
Step 2
), and finally obtain the result by combining these facts with (
60
) and the upper bound on
\|m_{x}\|_{2}
provided by Lemma
24
.

Step
1
:

Note that
M^{t}=U\Sigma^{t}U^{T}
. Therefore we get
M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x}=U\Sigma^{t+1}U^{T}\mathds{1}_{x}
, and
M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y}=U\Sigma^{t}U^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}
. Thus we have

m_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}=\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}\left(\left(U\Sigma^{t+1}U^{T}\right)\left(U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}\right)\left(U\Sigma^{t}U^{T}\right)\right)\mathds{1}_{y}

(61)

Note that
U^{T}U_{[k]}
is a
n\times k
matrix such that the top
k\times k
matrix is
I_{k\times k}
and the rest is zero. Also
U_{[k]}^{T}U
is a
k\times n
matrix such that the left
k\times k
matrix is
I_{k\times k}
and the rest is zero. Therefore we have

U{\Sigma}^{t+1}\left(U^{T}U_{[k]}\right){\Sigma}^{-2t}_{[k]}\left(U^{T}_{[k]}U\right){\Sigma}^{t}U^{T}=UHU^{T}\text{,}

where
H
is a
n\times n
matrix such that the top left
k\times k
matrix is
\Sigma_{k\times k}
and the rest is zero. Hence, we have

UHU^{T}=U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\text{.}

Thus we have

m_{x}^{T}(U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T})m_{y}=\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}

(62)

Step
2
:

We have
\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}
where
\widetilde{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\widetilde{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(m_{x})^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}(m_{y})

\displaystyle=m_{x}^{T}\left(\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\cdot\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}\right)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)\left(\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\cdot\widetilde{W}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{U}^{T}\right)m_{y}

\displaystyle=m_{x}^{T}\left(\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)\left(\widetilde{W}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{U}^{T}\right)m_{y}

(63)

Note that
\widetilde{W}^{T}\widetilde{W}_{[k]}
is a
n\times k
matrix such that the top
k\times k
matrix is
I_{k\times k}
and the rest is zero. Also
\widetilde{W}_{[k]}^{T}\widetilde{W}
is a
k\times n
matrix such that the left
k\times k
matrix is
I_{k\times k}
and the rest is zero. Therefore we have

\widetilde{\Sigma}\left(\widetilde{W}^{T}\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\right)\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\left(\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}\right)\widetilde{\Sigma}=\widetilde{H}\text{,}

where
\widetilde{H}
is a
n\times n
matrix such that the top left
k\times k
matrix is
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-2}_{[k]}
and the rest is zero. Hence, we have

(\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T})\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(\widetilde{W}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{U}^{T})=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{H}\widetilde{U}^{T}=\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-2}_{[k]}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}

(64)

Putting (
64
) and (
A.1
) together we get

m_{x}^{T}(M^{t}S)(\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]})(M^{t}S)^{T}m_{y}=m_{x}^{T}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-2}_{[k]}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}

(65)

Putting it together.

By (
60
), (
62
) and (
65
) we have

\displaystyle\left|m_{x}^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}m_{y}-\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}{U}_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|m_{x}^{T}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2}\widetilde{U}_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}-m_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}{\Sigma}_{[k]}^{-2t}U_{[k]}^{T}m_{y}\right|

By (
62
) and (
65
)

\displaystyle\leq\xi^{\prime}\cdot\|m_{x}\|_{2}\|m_{y}\|_{2}

By (
60
)

(66)

By Lemma
24
for any vertex
x\in V
we have

\|m_{x}\|^{2}_{2}=\|M^{t}\mathds{1}_{x}\|^{2}_{2}\leq O\left(\frac{k^{2}}{\eta}\cdot n^{-1+40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\right)\text{.}

(67)

Therefore by choice of
c^{\prime}
as a large enough constant and choosing
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi\cdot\eta}{c^{\prime}\cdot k^{2}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}
we have

\left|m_{x}^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}m_{y}-\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}{U}_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}\right|\leq O\left(\xi^{\prime}\cdot\frac{k^{2}}{\eta}\cdot n^{-1+40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{\xi}{n}\text{.}

(68)

∎

Fix
t\geq\frac{\log n}{\varphi^{2}}
. We will use the following result, which is a corollary
of a Kim-Vu like bound (Theorem
12
) we show in Section
B
.

Corollary 4
.

Let
k\geq 2
. Suppose
G
is a
d
-regular graph which admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering

C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
M
be the
random walk transition matrix of
G
. Let
0<\sigma_{err}\leq 1/n
. Let
a,b\in V
. Suppose that we
run
R
random walks of length
t
from
a
and
b
. For any
x\in V
, let

\widehat{m}_{a}(x)
(resp.
\widehat{m}_{b}(x)
) be a random variable which denotes
the fraction out of the
R
walks from
a
(resp.
b
) which end in
x
.
Then on choosing

R\geq\left(k^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{n^{-3/2+60(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}}{\eta^{3/2}\cdot\sigma_{err}^{2}}\right)\cdot(100\cdot\log n)^{4}

with probability at least
1-n^{-99k}
, it holds that

|\widehat{m}_{a}^{T}\widehat{m}_{b}-(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{a})^{T}(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{b})|\leq\sigma_{err}.

Finally, Lemma
26
bounds the absolute deviation between
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}

and our estimator. We put the two together using triangle inequality to prove Theorem

8
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
.
Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Let
0<\delta<1/2
, and

1/n^{5}<\xi<1
. Let
\mathcal{D}
denote the data structure constructed by Algorithm

InitializeOracle(
G,\delta,\xi
)
(Algorithm
14
).
Let
x,y\in V
. Let
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the value returned by
\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi,\mathcal{D})
(Algorithm
14
).
Let
t\geq\frac{20\log n}{\varphi^{2}}
. Let
c>1
be a large enough constant and let

s\geq\frac{c\cdot n^{240\cdot\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{4}}{\eta^{2}}
. Let

I_{S}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
be a multiset of
s
indices chosen independently and uniformly at random from

\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
S
be the
n\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column equals
\mathds{1}_{i_{j}}
.
Let
M
be the random walk transition matrix of
G
. Let

\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}
be the SVD of

\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S
. If
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{10^{5}}
, and Algorithm

14
succeeds, then with probability at least
1-n^{-99k}
we have

\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-(M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x})^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y})\right|<\frac{\xi}{n}\text{.}

Remark 5
.

The result in Gluch et al. above, obtains a success probability of at least
1-n^{-100}
. It
can be improved to
1-n^{-99k}
using Corollary
4
.

We now prove Theorem
8
.

Proof of Theorem
8
.

Correctness:

Let
s=\varTheta{(n^{480\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{8}/(\eta^{4}\cdot\xi^{2}))}
. Recall that
I_{S}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
is the multiset of
s
vertices each sampled uniformly at random (see line
4
of Algorithm
14
). Let
S
be the
n\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column equals
\mathds{1}_{i_{j}}
. Recall that
M
is the random walk transition matrix of
G
. Let
\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S=\widetilde{U}\widetilde{\Sigma}\widetilde{W}^{T}
be the eigendecomposition of
\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}\cdot M^{t}S
. We define

e_{1}=\left|(M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x})^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y})-\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}{U}_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}\right|

and

e_{2}=\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-(M^{t+1}\mathds{1}_{x})^{T}(M^{t}S)\left(\frac{n}{s}\cdot\widetilde{W}_{[k]}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-4}_{[k]}\widetilde{W}^{T}_{[k]}\right)(M^{t}S)^{T}(M^{t}\mathds{1}_{y})\right|

By triangle inequality we have

\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\rangle\right|=\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}{U}_{[k]}^{T}\mathds{1}_{y}\right|\leq e_{1}+e_{2}\text{.}

Let
\xi^{\prime}=\xi/2
. Let
c
be a constant in front of
s
in Lemma
25
and
c^{\prime}
be a
constant in front of
s
in Lemma
26
. Recall, line
3
of Algorithm

14
sets
s=\varTheta{(n^{480\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{8}/(\eta^{4}\cdot\xi^{2}))}
.
Since
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{10^{5}}
and
s\geq c\cdot n^{480\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{8}/(\eta^{4}\cdot\xi^{\prime 2})
, by Lemma
25
with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
we have
e_{1}\leq\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}=\frac{\xi}{2\cdot n}\text{.}
Since

s\geq\frac{c^{\prime}\cdot n^{240\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n\cdot k^{4}}{\eta^{2}}
, by
Lemma
26
, with probability at least
1-2\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}
we have

e_{2}\leq\frac{\xi}{2\cdot n}\text{.}
Thus with probability at least
1-3\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}
we
have

\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\rangle\right|\leq e_{1}+e_{2}\leq\frac{\xi}{2\cdot n}+\frac{\xi}{2\cdot n}\leq\frac{\xi}{n}\text{.}

Running time of
InitializeOracle
:
The algorithm first samples a
set
I_{S}
. Then, as per line
5
of Algorithm
14
, it
estimates the empirical probability distribution of
t
-step random walks starting from
any vertex
x\in I_{S}
. The
EstimateTransitionMatrix
procedure runs
R_{\text{init}}
random walks of length
t
from each vertex
x\in I_{S}
. So it takes
O(\log n\cdot s\cdot R_{\text{init}}\cdot t)
time
and requires
O(\log n\cdot s\cdot R_{\text{init}})
space to store endpoints of random walks. Then
as per line
6
of Algorithm
14
it estimates matrix
\mathcal{G}
such that
the entry corresponding to the
x^{\text{th}}
row and
y^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathcal{G}
is an
estimation of pairwise collision probability of random walks starting from
x,y\in I_{S}
. To
compute
\mathcal{G}
we call Algorithm
EstimateCollisionProbabilities
(
G,I_{S},R_{\text{init}},t
)
(from
[
GKL+21
]
) for
O(\log n)
times. This procedure takes

O(s\cdot R_{\text{init}}\cdot t\cdot\log n)
time and it requires
O(s^{2}\cdot\log n)
space to
store matrix
\mathcal{G}
. Computing the SVD of
\mathcal{G}
(done in line
7
of Algorithm
14
)
takes time
O(s^{3})
. Thus overall Algorithm
14
runs in
time
O\left(\log n\cdot s\cdot R_{\text{init}}\cdot t+s^{3}\right)
. Thus, by choice of

t=\varTheta\left(\frac{\log n}{\varphi^{2}}\right)
,

R_{\text{init}}=\varTheta{(n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\log^{O(1)}n\cdot k^{O(1)}/(\eta\cdot\xi)^{O(1)})}

and
s=\varTheta(n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot(\log n)^{O(1)}\cdot k^{O(1)}/{\xi}^{O(1)})
as in
Algorithm
14
we get that Algorithm
14
runs in time

\log n\cdot s\cdot R_{\text{init}}\cdot t+s^{3}=(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\xi\cdot\varphi})^{O(1)}\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}

and returns a data structure of size

O\left(s^{2}+\log n\cdot s\cdot R_{\text{init}}\right)=(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\xi})^{O(1)}\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\text{.}

Running time of
WeightedDotProductOracle
:

Algorithm
WeightedDotProductOracle
runs
R_{\text{query}}
random walks of length
t,t+1
from vertex
x
and vertex
y
, then it computes
(\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\widehat{Q})
and
(\widehat{Q}^{T}\widehat{m}_{y})
. Since
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}
has
s
columns and since
\widehat{m}_{x}
has at most
R_{\text{query}}
non-zero entries, thus one can compute
\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\cdot\widehat{Q}
in time
R_{\text{query}}\cdot s
. Finally Algorithm
15
returns value
(\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\widehat{Q})\Psi(\widehat{Q}^{T}\widehat{m}_{y})
. Since
(\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\widehat{Q}),(\widehat{Q}^{T}\widehat{m}_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
and
\Psi\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}
one can compute
(\widehat{m}_{x}^{T}\widehat{Q})\Psi(\widehat{Q}^{T}\widehat{m}_{y})
in time
O(s^{2})
. Thus overall Algorithm
15
takes
O\left(t\cdot R_{\text{query}}+s\cdot R_{\text{query}}+s^{2}\right)
time. Thus, by choice of
t=O\left(\frac{\log n}{\varphi^{2}}\right)
,
R_{\text{query}}=n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{k}{\eta\cdot\xi}\right)^{O(1)}
and
s=n^{O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}\cdot(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\xi})^{O(1)}
we get that the Algorithm
15
runs in time
(\frac{k\cdot\log n}{\eta\cdot\xi\cdot\varphi})^{O(1)}\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}
.
∎

A.2
Proof of Lemma
3
and Lemma
5

See
3

Proof.

Let
a=\mathds{1}_{A}^{T}\cdot(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2})
and

b=(\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}U_{[k]}^{T})\mathds{1}_{B}
. This allows us
to write the first term on LHS of Equation (
5
) as
\langle a,b\rangle
.
Analogously, define
a^{\prime}=\frac{|A|}{|S_{A}|}\cdot\mathds{1}_{S_{A}}^{T}\cdot(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2})

and
b^{\prime}=\frac{|B|}{|S_{B}|}\cdot(\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}U_{[k]}^{T})\mathds{1}_{S_{B}}
so that
the second term on LHS of Equation (
5
) can be written as
\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle
. With this setup, we have the following:

\displaystyle\left|\langle a,b\rangle-\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle\right|=\left|\langle a,b-b^{\prime}\rangle+\langle a-a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle\right|\leq\|a\|_{2}\|b-b^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|a-a^{\prime}\|_{2}\|b^{\prime}\|_{2}

(69)

where the last inequality follows by triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz. Now by expanding out
a
we get

a=\sum_{x\in A}\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}\left(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}\right)\text{.}

We show this quantity is estimated coordinate wise very well by the vector
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined as follows:

a^{\prime}=\frac{|A|}{|S_{A}|}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{A}}\mathds{1}_{x}^{T}\left(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}\right)\text{.}

Note that for all
i\in[k]
we have
\mathbb{E}[a^{\prime}(i)]=a(i)
. For any
i\in[k]
we first show that with high probability
|a(i)-a^{\prime}(i)|
is small, then by union bound we prove that
||a^{\prime}-a||
is small. Note that for every
i\in[k]
we have
a(i)=\sum_{x\in A}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\cdot u_{i}(x)
, where
u_{i}
is the
i
-th eigenvector of
M
and
\sigma_{i}
is the
i
-th eigenvalue of
M
. For every
x\in S_{A}
, let
Z_{x}
be a random variable defined as
Z_{x}=|A|\cdot\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\cdot u_{i}(x)
. Thus, we have
a^{\prime}(i)=\frac{1}{S_{A}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{A}}Z_{x}
, and
a(i)=\frac{1}{S_{A}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{A}}\mathbb{E}[Z_{x}]
. Therefore, by Hoeffding Bound
(Fact
3
), we have

\text{Pr}\left[|a^{\prime}(i)-a(i)|\geq t\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{-2|S_{A}|\cdot t^{2}}{\left(2\cdot\max_{x\in S_{A}}|Z_{x}|\right)^{2}}\right)

(70)

Next we need to bound
\max_{x\in S_{A}}|Z_{x}|
. Note that for every
i\in[k]
and every
x\in V
we have

\displaystyle|Z_{x}|

\displaystyle=|A|\cdot\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\cdot|u_{i}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|A|\cdot||u_{i}||_{\infty}

As
\sigma_{i}=1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\leq 1

\displaystyle=|A|\cdot\frac{n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\min_{i\in k}|C_{i}|}}

By Lemma
4

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|A|\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}}}

As
\min_{i\in k}|C_{i}|=\eta\cdot\max_{i\in k}|C_{i}|\geq\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}

(71)

Let
w_{A}=\frac{|A|\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}}}
and
\beta=\frac{\xi\cdot\sqrt{\eta}}{2\cdot k\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}
. Therefore, by (
70
) and (
A.2
) we have

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[|a^{\prime}(i)-a(i)|\geq\beta\cdot w_{A}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{-2|S_{A}|\cdot(\beta\cdot w_{A})^{2}}{4\cdot w_{A}^{2}}\right)=\exp\left(-|S_{A}|\beta^{2}/2\right)\leq n^{-200k}\text{,}

where the last inequality holds by choice of
|S_{A}|\geq\frac{1600\cdot k^{3}\cdot n^{40\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\log n}{\xi^{2}\cdot\eta}\geq 400\cdot k\cdot\log n\cdot\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}
. Thus, by a union bound over all
i\in[k]
, with probability at least
1-k\cdot n^{-200k}
we have

||a-a^{\prime}||_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(a^{\prime}(i)-a(i))^{2}}\leq\sqrt{k}\cdot\beta\cdot w_{A}

(72)

A similar analysis shows that with probability at least
1-k\cdot n^{-200k}
we have

||b-b^{\prime}||_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}\cdot\beta\cdot w_{B}\text{,}

(73)

where,
w_{B}=\frac{|B|\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}}}
. Also note that

\displaystyle||a||_{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\mathds{1}_{A}^{T}\left(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\mathds{1}_{A}^{T}\right|\right|_{2}\cdot\left|\left|U_{[k]}\right|\right|_{2}\cdot\left|\left|\Sigma_{[k]}^{1/2}\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|A|}\cdot\max_{i\in[k]}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}

As
||U_{[k]}||_{2}=1
, and
\Sigma
is diagonal

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}

As
\sigma_{i}=1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\leq 1

(74)

Similarly we have

||b||_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}

(75)

Thus by (
69
) we get

\displaystyle\|a\|_{2}\|b-b^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|a-a^{\prime}\|_{2}\|b^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{k\cdot n}\cdot\beta\cdot(w_{A}+w_{B})

By (
72
), (
73
), (
A.2
), (
75
)

\displaystyle\leq\xi\cdot n

As
w_{A}+w_{B}\leq\frac{2\cdot n\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}}}
and
\beta=\frac{\xi\cdot\sqrt{\eta}}{2\cdot k\cdot n^{20\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}}

Therefore, with probability at least
1-2\cdot k\cdot n^{-200k}\geq 1-n^{-100k}
we have

\left|\mathds{1}_{A}^{T}\cdot(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})\ \mathds{1}_{B}\ -\frac{|A|\cdot|B|}{|S_{A}|\cdot|S_{B}|}\cdot\mathds{1}_{S_{A}}^{T}(U_{[k]}\Sigma_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{T})\mathds{1}_{S_{B}}\right|\leq\xi\cdot n

∎

Fact 3
.

(
Hoeffding Bounds
)
Let
Z_{1},Z_{2},\cdots Z_{n}
be iid random variables with
Z_{i}\in[a,b]
for all
i\in[n]

where
-\infty\leq a\leq b\leq\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum|(Z_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}])|\geq t\right]\leq\exp(-2nt^{2}/(b-a)^{2}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum|(Z_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}])|\leq t\right]\leq\exp(-2nt^{2}/(b-a)^{2})

Finally, we prove lemma
5
.
See
5

Proof.

Let
s=|S|
. For
x\in V
, and
r\in[s]
, let
Y_{x}^{r}
be a random variable which is
1
if the
r
-th sampled vertex is
v
, and
0
otherwise. Thus
\mathbb{E}[Y_{x}^{r}]=\frac{1}{n}
. Observe that
|S_{i}|=|S\cap C_{i}|
is a random variable defined as
\sum_{r=1}^{s}\sum_{x\in C_{i}}Y_{x}^{r}
where its expectation is given by

\mathbb{E}[|S\cap C_{i}|]=\sum_{r=1}^{s}\sum_{x\in C_{i}}Y_{x}^{r}\geq s\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\geq s\cdot\frac{\eta}{k}\text{,}

where, the last inequality holds since
\min_{j\in[k]}|C_{j}|=\eta\cdot\max_{j\in[k]}|C_{j}|\geq\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}
.
Notice that the random variables
Y_{x}^{r}
are negatively associated, since for each
r
,
\sum_{x\in V}Y_{x}^{r}=1
. Therefore, by Chernoff bound,

\text{Pr}\left[\left||S\cap C_{i}|-\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\right|>\delta\cdot s\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\right]\leq 2\cdot\text{exp}\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{3}\cdot\frac{s\cdot\eta}{k}\right)\leq n^{-120\cdot k}\text{,}

where, the last inequality holds by choice of
s\geq\frac{400\cdot\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}
. Therefore, by union bound,

\text{Pr}\left[\exists i:\left||S\cap C_{i}|-\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\right|>\delta\cdot s\cdot\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\right]\leq 2\cdot k\cdot n^{-120\cdot k}\leq n^{-100\cdot k}\text{.}

∎

A.3
Estimate Cluster Sizes

In this section, we present routines to estimate cluster sizes in
RandomSampleModel
and
OracleModel
.

Algorithm 16

\textsc{EstimateClusterSize}(G,i,\delta)

# (Assuming clustering oracle using
RandomSampleModel
or
OracleModel
)

1:

s\leftarrow\frac{400\cdot\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}

2:

S\leftarrow
(multi)set of
s
i.i.d random vertices together with their cluster label

3:

S_{i}\leftarrow S\cap C_{i}

# (Can find
S_{i}
using the clustering oracle)

4:

\widehat{c_{i}}\leftarrow\frac{|S_{i}|}{s}\cdot n

Lemma 27
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\varphi,\delta\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi^{2}}
be smaller than a positive sufficiently small constant. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\varphi,\epsilon)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
\eta=\frac{\min_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}{\max_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}|}
. Then in the
RandomSampleModel
or
OracleModel
procedure
\textsc{EstimateClusterSize}(G,i,\delta)
(Algorithm
16
) runs in time
O\left(\frac{\log n\cdot k^{2}}{\eta\cdot\delta^{2}}\right)
and with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for every
i\in[k]
returns a value
\widehat{c_{i}}
such that
\widehat{c_{i}}\in(1\pm\delta)\cdot|C_{i}|
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5
.
∎

A.4
Proof of Lemma
6

See
6

Proof.

Note that as per line (
5
), (
6
) and (
7
) of Algorithm
1
we have

\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})=d\cdot\left(\frac{\xi\cdot n}{2}+\frac{n}{s}\cdot\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i}}z_{i}^{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[k]}\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}z_{i}z_{j}\cdot\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\right)\text{,}

(76)

where
\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}=\textsc{WeightedDotProductOracle}(G,x,y,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
and
\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{8\cdot k^{2}}
as per line (
4
).
We define matrix
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
as follows:

\displaystyle K_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\frac{\xi\cdot d\cdot n}{2}+\frac{d\cdot n\cdot|S_{i}|}{s}-\frac{d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{x,y\in S_{i}}\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\text{ {} {} if }i=j\\
\frac{-d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\text{ {} {} otherwise.}\end{cases}

(77)

Note that by (
76
) and (
77
) for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we have

\textsc{QuadraticOracle}(G,z,\xi,\mathcal{D})=z^{T}Kz\text{,}

also, by (
77
) observe that
K
is symmetric. Finally, we show that probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
,
\|K^{\prime}\|_{F}^{2}\leq O(n^{4})
.
By Theorem
8
with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for all
x,y\in V
we have

|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}-\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle|\leq\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}

(78)

Therefore, with probability at least
1-n^{-100\cdot k}
for every
i,j\in[k]
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle apx}\right|

By triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot\sum_{x\in S_{i},y\in S_{j}}\left|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle\right|+\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{n}

By Theorem
8

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d\cdot n^{2}}{s^{2}}\cdot s^{2}\left(\max_{x,y\in V}|\left\langle f_{x},\Sigma_{[k]}f_{y}\right\rangle|+\frac{1}{n}\right)

As
\xi^{\prime}\leq\xi\leq 1
and
|S_{i}|,|S_{j}|\leq s

\displaystyle=d\cdot n^{2}\cdot\max_{x,y\in V}||f_{x}||_{2}\cdot||\Sigma_{[k]}||_{2}\cdot||f_{y}||_{2}+d\cdot n

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot n^{2}\cdot\max_{x\in V}||f_{x}||_{2}^{2}+d\cdot n

As
||\Sigma_{[k]}||_{2}=\max_{i\in[k]}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\right)\leq 1

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k+d\cdot n

As
||f_{x}||_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}^{2}(x)\leq k

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k

(79)

Thus by (
77
) and (
A.4
) for every
i,j\in[k]
we have

|K_{i,j}|\leq\frac{\xi\cdot d}{n}+\frac{d\cdot n\cdot|S_{i}|}{s}+2\cdot d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k\leq 3\cdot d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k\text{,}

(80)

where, the last inequality holds as
\xi\leq 1
,
|S_{i}|\leq s
. Therfore, with probability at least
1-3\cdot n^{-100\cdot k}\geq 1-n^{-99\cdot k}
we have

||K||_{F}\leq\sqrt{k^{2}\cdot\left(3\cdot d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k\right)^{2}}\leq 2\cdot d\cdot n^{2}\cdot k^{2}\leq 2\cdot n^{5}.

∎

A.5
Proof of Claim
1

See
1

Proof.

We present such an instance
G
explicitly. We pick a large enough integer
m
.
Let
X_{1}=\{1,2,\cdots,m-2\epsilon m\}
denote the set of first
m-2\epsilon m
integers
and use standard constructions to obtain an
d-1
regular
\varphi\cdot d
-expander on vertices
in
X_{1}
. Also, let
Y_{1}=\{m-2\epsilon m+1,m-2\epsilon m+2,\cdots m\}
denote
another set of
2\epsilon m
integers and obtain another
d-1
regular
\varphi\cdot d
expander on

Y_{1}
. Put a matching of size
\epsilon\varphi dm
between the sets
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
. Also, put
a matching on remaining degree
d-1
vertices in
X_{1}
. Notice that
|E(X_{1},Y_{1})|=\epsilon\varphi dm
.
Let
C_{1}=X_{1}\cup Y_{1}
. Now we describe another set of vertices.
This time we consider three sets:
X_{2}=\{1,2,\cdots,m-4\epsilon m\}
,

Y_{2}=\{m-4\epsilon m+1,\cdots m-2\epsilon m\}
and
Z_{2}=\{m-2\epsilon m+1,\cdots,m\}
.
We again obtain a
d-1
regular
\varphi\cdot d
expander on all of these sets. Next, add a matching
of size
\epsilon\varphi dm
between
X_{2}
and
Y_{2}
and between
X_{2}
and
Z_{2}
.
We add a matching between remaining degree
d-1
vertices in
X_{2}
and
another matching between remanining degree
d-1
vertices in
Z_{2}
.
Notice that
|E(X_{2},Y_{2})|=\epsilon\varphi dm=|E(X_{2},Z_{2})|
. Next, let

C_{2}=X_{2}\cup Y_{2}\cup Z_{2}
.

Finally, we add a matching between the remaining degree
d-1
vertices in
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
.
Overall this gives a
d
-regular graph on
2m
vertices. We let
B=C_{1}
and
thus
\overline{B}=C_{2}
. Notice that

\varphi_{\text{out}}(C_{1})=\varphi_{\text{out}}(C_{2})=\epsilon
. Also, by construction,
note that
\varphi_{\text{in}}(C_{1})=\varphi_{\text{in}}(C_{2})\geq\varphi
.
Now consider the following set
S=X_{1}\cup Z_{2}
. We see that

|E(S,\overline{S})|=|E(X_{1},Y_{1})|+|E(X_{2},Z_{2})|=2\epsilon\varphi dm.

Also
|S|=|X_{1}|+|Z_{2}|=m
. And therefore, it holds
that
\phi(G)\leq\phi(S)=2\epsilon\varphi
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Corollary
4
: A Kim-Vu bound

In this section, we prove a Kim-Vu (
[
KV00
]
) like bound that was used in Section
A.1
.
This is our key tool in proving Corollary
4
. We reproduce the statement
for convenience of the reader.
See
4

Thus, the end goal in Corollary
4
is to obtain concentration bounds on the
collision probability of random walks from
a
and
b
. We do this by showing concentration
bounds on the following degree
2
(squarefree)
collision-counting polynomial
:

Z=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[R]\\
w\in V\end{subarray}}\prod X_{i,w}Y_{j,w}.

Here
X_{i,w}
is an indicator random variable which takes the value
1
when the
i
th random
walk from
a
ends at
w
and
Y_{j,w}
is another indicator variable which is
1
when the

j
th random walk from
b
ends at
w
. Concentration bounds for such polynomials can be shown
using an approach given by Kim and Vu. This approach exploits a bijection between squarefree
polynomials
Z
in
N
indicator variables with maximum degree
h
and (weighted) hypergraphs
H_{Z}
of
maximum arity
h
on
N
vertices. The distribution on any vertex of
H_{Z}
is identical to the
distribution on the corresponding variable of the polynomial. This is setup so that concentration
bounds on the total weight of hyperedges induced by a random sample of vertices in
H_{Z}
implies
a concentation bound on the value taken by the polynomial
Z
. However, in the Kim-Vu setup, each
vertex
v
is included in the sample as a result of a Bernoulli trial
Ber(p_{v})
independent of other vertices.

The collision-counting polynomial
Z
defined above differs from this setup in one key respect.
We consider end point distributions of
t
-step random
walks from
a,b\in V
. Focus on one such walk from (say)
a
. Note that the number of possible end points
for this walk is
n
. In all we take
R
samples from the end point distribution of walks from
a
.
As opposed to the standard Kim-Vu setup, our end point distributions are not Bernoulli random
variables (and have support size
n
in particular). Because of this, we need to adapt the
Kim-Vu machinery towards proving Corollary
4
above.

We begin our adaptation with some setup. Let
V
denote a set of size
n
and take

R\in\mathbb{N}
. We consider the following weighted hypergraph
H=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},w)
. We index elements
of
\mathcal{V}
by a pair
(i,w)\in[R]\times V
.
In particular, the pair
(i,w)
refers to vertex
w\in V_{i}
(which is a copy of
V
).
The hypergraph has an edge set
\mathcal{E}
where for any
e\in\mathcal{E}
, it holds that
e
contains
at most one vertex from any
V_{i}
(for any
i\in[R]
). That is, if for some
i
, it holds
that
(i,z)\in e
then for any
z^{\prime}\neq z
we have
(i,z^{\prime})\not\in e
. Finally, for
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
w(e)

denotes the weight of the edge
e
. We also suppose that the
maximum arity of any hyperedge is at most some small constant
h
. That is,

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}|e|\leq h.

Now, consider the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{D})

where
\Omega=V^{R}
and
\mathcal{D}=D_{1}\times D_{2}\cdots\times D_{R}
is a product measure on
\Omega
.
Here the distribution
D_{i}
is supported on
V_{i}
(which we recall is a copy of
V
).
Now consider sampling
\omega\sim_{\mathcal{D}}\Omega
. We would like to show Kim-Vu style concentration
bounds on the total weight of hyperedges induced by the sample
\omega
. To state the
concentration bound we seek, we need the following definitions.

Definition 20
.

Let
H=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},w)
denote a hypergraph with maximum arity at most
h
as
defined above. We define a random variable
Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

as follows:

Y=Y_{H}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w(e)\prod_{(i,w)\in e}t_{i,w}

Here, for each
i\in[R]
, we have a distribution
D_{i}
is supported over
V
.
The events
(t_{i,w}=1)_{w\in V}
are mutually exclusive and the event
t_{i,w}=1

occurs with probability
D_{i}(w)
.
Note that
Y
is a polynomial in the indicator variables
(t_{i,w})_{(i,w)\in[R]\times V}

and that
deg(Y)\leq h
.

Next, following Kim-Vu, we define the notion of a truncated hypergraph.

Definition 21
.

(Truncated subhypergraph and Truncated polynomial)
For each
A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)
, we define the hypergraph truncated to
A
(denoted
H_{A}
) as follows:

–

\mathcal{V}(H_{A})=\mathcal{V}(H)\setminus A
.

–

\mathcal{E}(H_{A})=\{B\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H_{A}),B\cup A\in\mathcal{E}(H)\}
.

–

For
B\in\mathcal{E}(H_{A})
,
w(B)=w(B\cup A)
.

Also, we define the polynomial truncated to
A
as

Y_{H_{A}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}\text{~s.t.~}A\subseteq e}w(e)\prod_{(i,w)\in e\setminus A}t_{i,w}.

If
A
is the singleton
\{(i,z)\},(i,z)\in[R]\times V
, then we denote the hypergraph
truncated to
H
by
H_{(i,z)}
.

Definition 22
.

For
A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)
, write
E_{A}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A}}]
. Now define
E_{i}[H]=\max_{|A|=i}E_{A}
.
Note that with this notation, note
E_{0}[H]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{H}]
. We finally let
E_{H}=\max_{i\geq 0}E_{i}[H]

and let
E^{\prime}_{H}=\max_{i\geq 1}E_{i}[H]
. When
H
is clear from the context, for
i\geq 0
, we
will often use
E_{i}
(instead of
E_{i}[H]
by dropping
H
) to make our notation simpler.

Now, we state the analog of main theorem from Kim-Vu adapted to the above setup.

Theorem 12
.

Let
H=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},w)
denote a hypergraph with maximum arity at most
h
where

\mathcal{V}=[R]\times V
. Write
\Omega=V^{R}
and let
(\Omega,D_{1}\times D_{2}\times\cdots\times D_{R})

denote the associated probability space. Let
Y_{H}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\prod_{(i,w)\in e}t_{i,w}

denote a polynomial random variable defined with respect to
H
where for every
i\in[R]
,
we have a distribution
D_{i}
supported over
V
. The events
(t_{i,w}=1)_{w\in V}
are
mutually exclusive and the event
t_{i,w}=1
occurs with probability
D_{i}(w)
. Then, we have

Pr(|Y_{H}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{H}]|\geq a_{h}(E_{H}E^{\prime}_{H})^{1/2}\eta^{h})\leq O(\exp(-\eta+(h-1)\log(Rn)))

for any
\eta>1
and
a_{h}=8^{h}h!^{1/2}
.

Remark 6
.

Note that without loss of generality, we may assume that for any
i\in[R]
and for all
z,z^{\prime}\in V

it holds that no
e\in\mathcal{E}(H)
contains both
(i,z)
and
(i,z^{\prime})
. This is because the probability that
a random
\omega\sim\mathcal{D}
contains both
(i,z)
and
(i,z^{\prime})
is zero.

We now show how Kim-Vu bounds can be used to prove Corollary
4
.

B.0.1
Proof of Corollary
4

In this section, we show how to use the Kim-Vu like bound proved in Section
B

to establish Corollary
4
.

We will need some setup. Take a finite set
V

with
|V|=n
and take
R\in\mathbb{N}
. We consider a probability space
\Omega=V^{2R}
which
is equipped with the product measure
m_{a}^{R}\times m_{b}^{R}
. That is, we have
2R
disjoint copies
of
V
. The first
R
of these are equipped with
m_{a}
and the last
R
are equipped with
m_{b}
.
Here
m_{a}=M^{t}\mathds{1}_{a}
and
m_{b}=M^{t}\mathds{1}_{b}
are end point distributions of
t
-step
random walks from
a
and
b
respectively. We consider the following unweighted hypergraph

H=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
. Here
\mathcal{V}=[2R]\times V
is just the disjoint union of the
2R
copies
of
V
. For any
(i,w)\in[R]\times V
we connect it to
(j,w)
where
j\in\{R+1,R+2,\cdots,2R\}
.
Thus all the copies of
V
equipped with
m_{a}
are connected via perfect matchings to all copies
of
V
that are equipped with
m_{b}
.

For
i\leq R
, let
X_{(i,w)}
be an indicator random variable which is
1
iff the sample
from the
i
th copy of
V
is
w
(alternatively, in the
i^{th}
copy of
V
, a
t
-length walk
started at
a
ends at
w
).
Similarly, for
R<j\leq 2R
, define indicator variables
Y_{(j,w)}
which are associated with samples taken
from
m_{b}
. Let
S
denote the set of
2R
vertices which get picked in this process. That is,

S=\left\{X_{(i,w)}:X_{(i,w)}=1\text{ where }i\in[R],w\in V(G)\right\}\cup\left\{Y_{(j,w)}:Y_{(j,w)}=1\text{ where }R<j\leq 2R,w\in V(G)\right\}

We now define the following polynomial in the indicator variables
X_{i,w}
and
Y_{j,w}

Z_{H}=\sum_{e=\{(i,w),(j,w)\}\in\mathcal{E}(H)}\prod X_{i,w}Y_{j,w}.

Following the notation in
B
, we have the following claims.

Claim 7
.

Consider the preceding setup. The following all hold.

•

E_{0}\leq R^{2}\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2}^{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}^{2})
.

•

E_{1}\leq R\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2})
.

•

E_{2}=1
.

•

E_{r}=0
for all
r\geq 3
.

Proof.

We prove the first item.

\displaystyle E_{0}=\mathbb{E}[Z_{H}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R],j\in\{R+1,\cdots,2R\}\\
w\in V\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}(w)\cdot Y_{j}(w)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R],j\in\{R+1,\cdots,2R\}\\
w\in V\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}(w)]\cdot\mathbb{E}[Y_{j}(w)]

\displaystyle=R^{2}\langle m_{a},m_{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq R^{2}\cdot\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2}^{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}^{2}).

Now, we consider the second item above.
For ease of notation, write
A=[R]\times V
and
B=\{R+1,\cdots,2R\}\times V
.
Write
val_{1}=\max_{(j,w)\in B}\mathbb{E}[Z_{(j,w)}]
and
val_{2}=\max_{(i,w)\in A}\mathbb{E}[Z_{(i,w)}]
.
Note that
E_{1}=\max(val_{1},val_{2})
. We upperbound
val_{1}
first, the upperbound on
val_{2}
follows
similarly. Choose any
j,w\in B
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{(j,w)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[R]}\mathbb{E}[X_{(i,w)}]

\displaystyle=R\mathbb{E}[X_{(1,w)}]=Rm_{a}(w)\leq R\|m_{a}\|_{\infty}\leq R\|m_{a}\|_{2}

Similarly
val_{2}\leq R\|m_{b}\|_{2}
which means
E_{1}\leq R\max\left(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}\right)
.

For item 3, we pick
u,v\in A\cup B
. Note that

E_{\{u,v\}}=0
when
u,v\in A
or
u,v\in B
.
Moreover, for
(i,u)\in A
and
(j,v)\in B
, if
\{u,v\}\not\in\mathcal{E}(H)
then also we have that

E_{\{u,v\}}=0
. Finally when
(i,u)\in A
,
(j,v)\in B
and
\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}(H)
we get that

E_{\{u,v\}}=1
which also means
E_{2}=1
.

The last item is immediate as all edges have arity
2
.
∎

Claim 8
.

Choose
R\geq\sqrt{n}
. Then the following holds

E_{H}\leq R^{2}\cdot\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2}^{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}^{2})
(where
E_{H}
is as defined in
Definition
22
).

Proof.

This proof follows by using Claim
7
and the premise.
Recall that
E_{H}=\max_{i\geq 0}E_{i}=\max(E_{0},E_{1},E_{2})
. Note that
\|m_{a}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}

since
m_{a}
is a distribution supported on a set of size
n
(and the same holds for
m_{b}
).
Thus, on setting
R\geq\sqrt{n}
, it follows that both
R\cdot\|m_{a}\|_{2}\geq 1

and
R\cdot\|m_{b}\|_{2}\geq 1
. Since
E_{2}=1
, we see that both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are at most

\alpha=R\cdot\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}).

Note that
\alpha\geq 1
. Thus,
\alpha^{2}\geq\alpha
.
Moreover, from Claim
7
, it holds that
E_{0}\leq\alpha^{2}
. In all,
this means that
E_{H}=\max(E_{0},E_{1},E_{2})\leq\alpha^{2}\leq R^{2}\cdot\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2}^{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}^{2})
.
∎

Claim 9
.

Choose
R\geq\sqrt{n}
. Then the following holds

E^{\prime}_{H}\leq\max(R\|m_{a}\|_{2},R\|m_{b}\|_{2})
(where
E^{\prime}_{H}
is as defined in
Definition
22
).

Proof.

The proof is identical to the one used for Claim
8
.
∎

Let
p_{a,b}:=\mathds{1}_{a}^{T}M^{2t}\mathds{1}_{b}
for any
a,b\in V
denote the collision
probability of
t
-step random walks from
a
and
b
.
Now, we are ready to prove Corollary
4
.

Proof.

(Of Corollary
4
)
Note that
p_{a,b}=\mathbb{E}[Z_{H}]/R^{2}=\langle m_{a},m_{b}\rangle
. Write
\widehat{p}_{a,b}=Z_{H}/R^{2}
.
Apply Theorem
12
with
h=2
(and note that
8^{h}\cdot h!^{1/2}\leq 100
when
h=2
).
This gives

Pr(|Z_{H}-E_{H}|\geq(E_{H}E^{\prime}_{H})^{1/2}(100k\log n)^{2})\leq n^{-100k}\cdot 2Rn.

We note that on setting
\sigma_{err}\leq 1/n
, it holds that

R\geq\left(k^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{n^{-3/2+60(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}}{\eta^{3/2}\cdot\sigma_{err}^{2}}\right)\cdot(100k\log n)^{4}\geq\sqrt{n}.

Thus, we can apply Claim
8
and conclude that

E_{H}\leq R^{2}\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2}^{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}^{2})
from Claim
8
. This gives

Pr\left(|Z_{H}-E_{H}|>R\left(\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2})\right)\cdot\sqrt{E^{\prime}_{H}}\cdot(100k\log n)^{2}\right)\leq n^{-100k}\cdot 2Rn.

Together with
Z_{H}=R^{2}\widehat{m}_{a,b}
, this means

Pr\left(|p_{a,b}-\widehat{p}_{a,b}|>\frac{1}{R^{2}}\cdot\left(R\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2})\right)\cdot\sqrt{E^{\prime}_{H}}\cdot(100k\log n)^{2}\right)\leq n^{-100k}\cdot 2Rn.

Again, since
R\geq\sqrt{n}
, we apply Claim
9
and obtain

E^{\prime}_{H}\leq\max(R\|m_{a}\|_{2},R\|m_{b}\|_{2})
. Substituting this bound on
E^{\prime}_{H}
, we get

\displaystyle n^{-100k}\cdot 2Rn

\displaystyle\geq Pr\left(|p_{a,b}-\widehat{p}_{a,b}|>\frac{\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2})}{R}\cdot\sqrt{R\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2})}\cdot(100k\log n)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=Pr\left(|p_{a,b}-\widehat{p}_{a,b}|>\frac{(\max(\|m_{a}\|_{2},\|m_{b}\|_{2}))^{3/2}}{\sqrt{R}}\cdot(100k\log n)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq Pr\left(|p_{a,b}-\widehat{p}_{a,b}|>\frac{k^{3/2}\cdot n^{-3/4}\cdot n^{30\epsilon/\varphi^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\right)^{3/4}}{\sqrt{R}}\cdot(100k\log n)^{2}\right)

Here the last step follows using Lemma
24
.

Plugging in
R\geq k^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{n^{-3/2+60(\epsilon/\varphi^{2})}}{\eta^{3/2}\cdot\sigma_{err}^{2}}\cdot(100k\log n)^{4}
,
from the premise, it again follows

Pr\left(|p_{a,b}-\widehat{p}_{a,b}|\geq\sigma_{err}\right)\leq n^{-100k}\cdot 2Rn\leq n^{-99k}

∎

B.0.2
The Computational Model

Denote
Y=Y_{H}
and write

\mathbb{E}[Y]=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}Y(\omega)\mathcal{D}(\omega)=\sum_{\omega\in V^{R}}Y(\omega)\prod_{i\in[R]}D_{i}(\omega_{i}).

It is convenient to represent the outcome
\omega\in V^{R}
of the above random
process using the following tree
T
of depth
R
. The tree is defined level by level.
The tree helps us view the output
\omega
of the above sampling procedure one coordinate
at a time as it is revealed.
At the
i
th level of the tree, we sample a vertex
\omega_{i}\sim V
from the distribution
D_{i}
.
Thus, the set of leaves of
T
is precisely the set
\Omega
.
Let
\omega^{i}
denote the vector containing first
i
coordinates of
\omega
. The following notation
will be used later.

Definition 23
.

(Conditional expectation)
For a node
\omega^{i}
at level
i\in[R]
, we let

\mathbb{E}[\omega^{i}]:=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\omega}\sim\mathcal{D}}[Y|\widetilde{\omega}^{i}=\omega^{i}]=\sum_{\widetilde{\omega}:\widetilde{\omega}^{i}=\omega^{i}}Y(\widetilde{\omega})\prod_{j=i+1}^{R}D_{j}(\widetilde{\omega}_{j}).

Also for vectors
v,w
of length
i
and
j
, let
(v,w)
denote the vector of length
i+j
obtained by writing

v
followed by
w
. One key benefit of
using the tree
T
is that it can be used to track how the expected value fluctuates on taking
samples according to the distribution
D_{i}
at the
i
th level conditioned on the decisions so far.
This is explored in the next section.

B.0.3
Small Fluctuations result in good concentration

Let us introduce some definitions.

Definition 24
.

(Fluctuations and Absolute fluctuations)
Fix a leaf
\omega\in T
. For
i\in[R]
,
z\in V
, let

\mu_{i,z}(\omega):=\mathbb{E}[(\omega^{i-1},z)]-\mathbb{E}[\omega^{i-1}]=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\omega}\sim\mathcal{D}}[Y|\widetilde{\omega}^{i}=(\omega^{i-1},z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\omega}\sim\mathcal{D}}[Y|\widetilde{\omega}^{i-1}=\omega^{i-1}].

denote the fluctuation in expected value of
Y
having fixed the first
i-1

coordinates according to
\omega^{i-1}
and setting
\omega_{i}=z
.
Following Kim-Vu, we also define absolute fluctuations
c_{i,z}(\omega)=|\mu_{i,z}(\omega)|
.
For
i\in[R]
, and a leaf
\omega
of
T
, we also define

c_{i}(\omega)=\max_{z\in V}c_{i,z}(\omega)
.
Also, it is convenient to define
c(\omega)=\max_{i}\max_{z}|c_{i,z}(\omega)|
.
Let
c_{Y}=\max_{\omega\in\Omega}c(\omega)
.

For
\omega\in\Omega
, we define the following.

Definition 25
.

(Two norm of the fluctuation)
For a leaf
\omega\in\Omega
, for
i\in[R]
let us define

V_{i}(\omega)=\sum_{z\in V}D_{i}(z)\mu_{i,z}(\omega)^{2}.

We also define
V(\omega)=\sum_{i=1}^{R}V_{i}(\omega)
and

V_{Y}=\max_{\omega\in\Omega}V(\omega)
.

Now we adapt a key theorem from Kim-Vu which is a key tool in proving Theorem
12
.

Theorem 13
.

If
c^{\prime}\geq c_{Y}
and
V^{\prime}\geq V_{Y}
and
0<\eta<V^{\prime}/(c^{\prime})^{2}
, then

Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq\left(\eta V^{\prime}\right)^{1/2})<2\exp(-\eta/4)

Proof.

We first show that one can assume, w.log that
\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
. To see this, take any
constant
\alpha
and note that all the values
\{\mu_{i,z}\}_{i\in[R],z\in V}

for the polynomial
Y
are identical to those for the polynomial
Y+\alpha
.
Therefore, all the absolute fluctuations
\{c_{i,z}(\omega)\}_{i\in[R],z\in V}

and the two norm of fluctuations
\{V_{i}(\omega)\}_{i\in[R]}
are the
same as well. In particular, this holds for
\alpha=-\mathbb{E}[Y]
. So, we can indeed
assume that
\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
. Now following Kim-Vu, note that for any
x>0
,

\displaystyle Pr(Y\geq(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2})

\displaystyle=Pr(e^{xY}\geq e^{x(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{xY}]}{e^{x(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2}}}

Lemma
28
shows that for
x<1/c_{Y}
, we have
\mathbb{E}[e^{xY}]\leq e^{x^{2}V_{Y}}
.
We choose
x=\frac{1}{2}(\eta/V^{\prime})^{1/2}
whereupon it indeed holds that
x<1/c^{\prime}\leq 1/c_{Y}
.
For this choice of
x
, Lemma
28
gives

Pr(Y\geq(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2})\leq e^{x^{2}V_{Y}}\cdot e^{-x(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2}}\leq e^{x^{2}V^{\prime}-x(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2}}=e^{-\eta/4}.

By symmetry, we similarly obtain a bound for the lower tail which in all gives

Pr(|Y|\geq(\eta V^{\prime})^{1/2})\leq 2\exp(-\eta/4)
. This finishes
the proof.
∎

Now, we state and prove Lemma
28
used in the previous proof.

Lemma 28
.

If
0<x<1/c_{Y}
and
\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
, then
\mathbb{E}[e^{xY}]\leq e^{x^{2}V_{Y}}
.

Proof.

We adapt the proof of the same lemma given on page 5 of Kim-Vu
[
KV00
]
.
We use induction on
R
. The case
R=0
is trivial. Suppose
R\geq 1
.
Fix
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
. Now, fix any
z\in V
. Note that we
have

\displaystyle\mu_{1,z}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y|\omega_{1}=z]-\mathbb{E}[Y]=\mu_{1,z}(\omega^{\prime})

This means,
\mu_{1,z}
is a constant function over
\Omega
for any choice
of the first level vertex
z
. So, we drop
\omega
and write
\mu_{1,z}

in place of
\mu_{1,z}(\omega)
from now on. Armed with this notation, note

V_{1}(\omega)=\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\mu_{1,z}^{2}=V_{1}(\omega^{\prime})

(81)

also holds for all
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
. We denote this constant value as
V_{1}

(and again drop
\omega
from now on).

Consider the function
Y-\mu_{1,z}
assigned to the subtree rooted
at the child of root labeled by
z\in V
. Denote this tree as
T_{z}
.
This subtree has height
R-1
. Moreover,
note that

\mathbb{E}[Y-\mu_{1,z}|\omega_{1}=z]=\mathbb{E}[Y|\omega_{1}=z]-\mu_{1,z}=0.

(82)

Now, we use induction hypothesis on the tree
T_{z}
. For any
z\in V
, this gives

\mathbb{E}[e^{x(Y-\mu_{1,z})}]\leq e^{x^{2}\cdot V_{Y-\mu_{1,z}}}=e^{x^{2}(V_{Y}-V_{1})}.

Therefore, by conditioning on the value of
\omega_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{xY}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot e^{x\mu_{1,z}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[e^{x(Y-\mu_{1,z})}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot e^{x\mu_{1,z}}\cdot e^{x^{2}(V_{Y}-V_{1})}

It remains to show that
\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)e^{x\mu_{1,z}}\leq e^{x^{2}V_{1}}
.
Again following Kim-Vu, we take Taylor expansion of the LHS above. This gives

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)e^{x\mu_{1,z}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\left(1+x\mu_{1,z}+x^{2}\mu_{1,z}^{2}/2+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=1+x\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot\mu_{1,z}+\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot(x\mu_{1,z})^{i}

\displaystyle=1+x\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[Y|\omega_{1}=z]+x^{2}\cdot\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\mu_{1,z}^{2}\cdot(x\mu_{1,z})^{i-2}

(83)

We will bound the two terms in Equation (
83
) separately. Denote the two terms as
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
For the first term
T_{1}
, note that, since we assume without loss of generality that
\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
,

T_{1}=\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[Y|\omega_{1}=z]=\mathbb{E}[Y]=0.

(85)

We now consider the second term of Equation (
83
). It will be convenient to define a
distribution
\mathcal{P}
supported on
V
where
\mathcal{P}(z)=\frac{D_{1}(z)\mu_{1,z}^{2}}{V_{1}}
. Note that
this is a bona fide distribution by Equation (
81
) which defines
V_{1}
above.

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=x^{2}\cdot\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)\mu_{1,z}^{2}\cdot(x\mu_{1,z})^{i-2}

\displaystyle=V_{1}x^{2}\cdot\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\sum_{z\in V}\frac{D_{1}(z)\mu_{1,z}^{2}}{V_{1}}\cdot(x\mu_{1,z})^{i-2}

\displaystyle=V_{1}x^{2}\cdot\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{P}}[(x\mu_{1,z})^{i-2}]

Since
x<1/c_{Y}
, we note
|x\mu_{1,z}|\leq 1
for any
z\in V
. Therefore,

\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{1}{i!}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{P}}[(x\mu_{1,z})^{i-2}]\leq\sum_{i\geq 2}1/i!\sum_{z\in V}\mathcal{P}(z)=\sum_{i\geq 2}1/i!<1.

(86)

Putting Equations (
85
) and (
86
) together in Equation (
83
),
we upper bound
\sum_{z\in V}D_{1}(z)e^{x\mu_{1,z}}\leq 1+V_{1}x^{2}\leq e^{x^{2}V_{1}}

which finishes the proof.
∎

The following theorem is the key tool in the proof of Main Theorem. It appears as Theorem 2.2.2 in

[
KV00
]
. The proof is a direct adaptation and we reproduce the proof given in Kim-Vu for
completeness.

Theorem 14
.

(Theorem 2.2.2 in
[
KV00
]
)
Let
V
and
c
be positive numbers and let
B=\{\omega\in\Omega:c(\omega)>c\lor\ V(\omega)>V\}
.
If
0<\eta<V/c^{2}
then

Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|>(\eta V)^{1/2})\leq 2e^{-\eta/4}+Pr(B).

Proof.

Call
B
the set of bad leaves. Take
\omega\in B
and consider the path from root to
\omega
.
Let
i
denote the first level such that either
V_{i}(\omega)>V
or
c_{i}(\omega)>c
. If
i

is such a level, we call the node
\omega^{i-1}
exceptional. Note that all leaves
\omega^{\prime}

below an exceptional node are bad. Now, define a new polynomial
Y^{\prime}
as follows. For
a leaf
\omega^{\prime}\not\in B
, set
Y^{\prime}(\omega)=Y(\omega)
. For any leaf
\omega^{\prime}
below an exceptional
node
\omega^{i-1}
, we set
Y^{\prime}(\omega)=\mathbb{E}[Y|\omega^{\prime{i-1}}=\omega^{i-1}]
.
Since the exceptional nodes are incomparable, the function
Y^{\prime}
is well-defined. Thus,
in the new tree
V(\omega)\leq V
and
c(\omega)\leq c
for every leaf
\omega
. So,
Theorem
13
implies
Pr(|Y^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{\prime}]|\geq(\eta V)^{1/2})\leq 2\exp(-\eta/4)
.
Finally, note that
\mathbb{E}[Y^{\prime}]=\mathbb{E}[Y]
and recall
Y^{\prime}(\omega)=Y(\omega)
for all
\omega\not\in B
.
This gives

Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|>(\eta V)^{1/2})\leq 2e^{-\eta/4}+Pr(B)

as desired.
∎

B.0.4
Proof of Theorem
12

We again adapt the proof given in
[
KV00
]
. To do this, we first borrow a definition.

Definition 26
.

(Shadow hypergraphs)
Let
H=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},w)
denote a weighted hypergraph as defined in Definition

20
. The shadow
H^{*}
of
H
is a weighted
hypergraph on the same (ordered) set
\mathcal{V}
of vertices where the edge set of

H^{*}
is defined as follows: Take any edge
e=\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots a_{l}\}\in\mathcal{E}(H)
where

a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots a_{l}
and produce
l
new edges (which we call shadow edges)

e_{j}
where
e_{0}=\emptyset
and
for
1\leq j\leq l-1
, we set
e_{j}=\{a_{1},\ldots a_{j}\}
. Finally, in
H^{*}
,
we set the weight of edge
e_{j}
to be
w(e)\cdot\prod_{s=j+1}^{l}p_{a_{s}}

where
p_{w}
is the expectation of the indicator
t_{w}
.
If an edge in
H^{*}
is shadow
of multiple edges we keep one copy of the edge and add the weights.

Also, we will need the following claims.

Claim 10
.

For any
(i,z)\in\mathcal{V}(H)
, consider the truncated hypergraph
H_{(i,z)}
(Definition
21
).
We have, for any
j<h
,

E_{j}[H_{(i,z)}]\leq E_{j+1}[H].

(Recall from Definition
22
that
E_{j}[H]=\max_{|A|=j}\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A}}]
.)

Proof.

E_{j}[H_{(i,z)}]=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)\setminus(i,z)\\
|A|=j\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A\cup\{i,z\}}}]\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)\\
|A|=j+1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A}}]=E_{j+1}[H].

∎

Claim 11
.

For any
j<h
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{j}[H^{*}]\leq h\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j}[H]
.

Proof.

\mathbb{E}_{j}[H^{*}]=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)\\
|A|=j\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A}^{*}}]\ \leq h\cdot\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subseteq\mathcal{V}(H)\\
|A|=j\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{H_{A}}]=h\mathbb{E}_{j}[H].

Here, the first step follows from the definition of
E_{j}
and
H^{*}
. The second step
follows because the maximum number of shadow edges of any edge in
H
is at most
h

and by the property that all the indicators for all variables in any edge are independent
(see Remark
6
).
∎

Our adaptation of the proof is pretty direct. The only difference is that we have an
n
-ary tree
instead of a binary tree as considered in Kim-Vu. We just adapt our definitions of fluctuation and
absolute fluctuation (see Definition
24
). The proof is given only for completeness sake.

Proof.

(Of Theorem
12
)
For any
\eta>1
, a positive polynomial
Y
and its support
hypergraph
H
, we show by induction on the arity
h
of biggest
hyperedge of
H
the following.

Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq c_{h}(E_{H}E^{\prime}_{H})^{1/2}\eta^{h})<d_{h}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-1)\log{Rn}).

Here,
d_{h}
is recursively defined as follows:
d_{1}=2
and
d_{h}=(1+1/n)d_{h-1}+2/n^{h-1}

and
c_{h}=2^{h-1}(h!)^{1/2}
.

Base Case:
For base case, we take
h=1
. In this case, all edges have at most one vertex.
Thus,
Y=\sum_{(i,z)\in\mathcal{V}}w_{i,z}t_{i,z}+w_{0}
. Here,
w_{i,z}
is the
weight of
t_{i,z}
and
w_{0}
is the weight of the (possibly) empty edge. We want to
show

Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq\eta\max(E_{0},E_{1})\cdot E_{1})\leq 2\exp(-\eta/4)

(87)

Note
that since we seek large deviation bounds on
|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|
, we may
assume that
w_{0}=0
. In this case,
E_{1}=\max_{(i,z)\in\mathcal{V}}w_{i,z}
. Keeping
with Kim-Vu, set

V=\max(E_{0},E_{1})E_{1}\eta,\ \ c=E_{1}.

We prove the
deviation bounds we seek in this case using Theorem
14
.
We now check if conditions of Theorem
14
apply.
In this (
h=1
) case, note that for fixed
i\in[R]
and
z\in V
,

c_{i,z}(\omega)=w_{i,z}
holds for all
\omega\in\Omega
. We denote this value as
c_{i,z}
.
Next, note that

\sum_{i\in[R]}V_{i}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)c_{i,z}^{2}=\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)w_{i,z}^{2}\leq\max w_{i,z}\sum D_{i}(z)w_{i,z}=E_{1}E_{0}<V.

Moreover, by choice of
V
and
c
above, it follows
V/c^{2}=V/E_{1}^{2}\geq\eta
. Thus, conditions
of
14
indeed holds. Since,
\eta>1
, this gives

\displaystyle Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq\eta V^{1/2})\leq Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq(\eta V)^{1/2})\leq 2e^{-\eta/4}

which satisfies Equation (
87
) as desired.

Inductive Step:
Now, we suppose the induction hypothesis holds for
h-1
and we prove it for
a hypergraph
H
with
\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}(H)}|e|=h
.
Take a leaf
\omega
of
T
. For some
c
and
V
to be chosen later, we define
the following events:

–

\mathcal{E}_{1}(\omega)
: The event that
c(\omega)>c
.

–

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\omega)
: The event that
V(\omega)>V
.

Use a union bound and write
Pr(\mathcal{E}_{1}\lor\mathcal{E}_{2})\leq Pr(\mathcal{E}_{1})+Pr(\mathcal{E}_{2}|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}})
.
For the first term above, note

\displaystyle Pr(\mathcal{E}_{1})

\displaystyle=Pr(c(\omega)>c)=Pr(\exists i\in[R],z\in V\ s.t.\ c_{i,z}(\omega)>c)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}Pr(c_{i,z}(\omega)>c)

Next, for the second term, note

\displaystyle Pr(\mathcal{E}_{2}|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}})

\displaystyle=Pr(V(\omega)>V|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}})=Pr(\sum_{i\in[R]}V_{i}(\omega)>V|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}})

\displaystyle=Pr(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)c_{i,z}(\omega)^{2}>V|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq Pr(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)c_{i,z}(\omega)>V/c)

Here note that we dropped the conditioning in the last inequality. We can do this because
the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}|\overline{\mathcal{E}_{1}}
is a subevent of the event that
\omega\sim_{\mathcal{D}}\Omega

satisfies
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)c_{i,z}(\omega)>V/c
.

Putting together the above discussion we get

Pr(c(\omega)>c\lor V(\omega)>V)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}Pr(c_{i,z}(\omega)>c)+Pr(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)c_{i,z}(\omega)>V/c)

(88)

Now, we will upper bound both of the probabilities above using Theorem
14

and the induction hypothesis for hypergraphs with edges of maximum arity
h-1
.
Pick
\omega\sim_{\mathcal{D}}\Omega
. Consider the polynomial.

C_{i,z}=\sum_{(i,z)\in e\in\mathcal{E}(H)}w(e)\prod_{s\in e\setminus(i,z)}t_{s}^{(i,z)}.

where for
s=(i^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in[R]\times V
,
t_{s}^{(i,z)}=t_{s}
if
i^{\prime}<i
and
t_{s}^{(i,z)}=D_{i^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})

if
i^{\prime}>i
. Thus, this polynomial is defined with
t_{s}^{(i,z)}
as its variables where
s=(i^{\prime},z^{\prime})

with
i^{\prime}<i
. For the random
\omega
picked, let
\omega^{i-1}
denote the first
i-1
coordinates
in
\omega
. Now, set the variables in the polynomial
C_{i,z}
as follows.
For
i^{\prime}<i,s=(i^{\prime},z^{\prime})
let

t_{s}^{(i,z)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }\omega_{i^{\prime}}=z^{\prime}\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Then note that

C_{i,z}(\{t_{i^{\prime},z^{\prime}}^{(i,z)}\}_{i^{\prime}<i})\in\{c_{i,z}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}[\omega^{i-1}],c_{i,z}(\omega)+\mathbb{E}[\omega^{i-1}]\}

where the
\mathbb{E}[\omega^{i-1}]
is a constant on fixing
\omega^{i-1}
.

Also, note that the supporing hypergraph of
C_{i,z}
is the
(i,z)
-truncated
hypergraph of
H
(see Definition
21
). This corresponds to the
truncated graph
H_{(i,z)}
which has arity
h-1
. So, we apply the
induction hypothesis. This gives

Pr(|C_{i,z}-\mathbb{E}[C_{i,z}]|>c_{h-1}(\mathbb{E}[C_{i,z}]\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[C_{i,z}])^{1/2}\eta^{h-1})<\ d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log(Rn))

(89)

Note that
\mathbb{E}[C_{i,z}],\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[C_{i,z}]
are both at most
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H]
(which follows from
Claim
10
). This together with Equation (
89
) gives

Pr(C_{i,z}>2c_{h-1}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H]\eta^{h-1})<d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log{Rn})

(90)

For the second term in Equation (
88
), define the following polynomial

C=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}D_{i}(z)C_{i,z}=\sum_{(i,z)\in[R]\times V}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathcal{E}(H)\\
e\ni(i,z)\end{subarray}}\prod_{s\in e\setminus(i,z)}D_{i}(z)t_{s}^{(i,z)}.

Note that the support hypergraph of this polynomial is the precisely the shadow
H^{*}
of
H
.
Recall that all edges of
H^{*}
have arity at most
h-1
. Thus, we again use the induction
hypothesis and obtain

Pr(|C-\mathbb{E}[C]|>c_{h-1}\cdot(E_{H^{*}}E^{\prime}_{H^{*}})^{1/2}\eta^{h-1})<\ d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log(Rn))

(91)

Using Claim
11
, we get

Pr(C>2c_{h-1}\cdot h\cdot\mathbb{E}[H]\eta^{h-1})<d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log{Rn})

(92)

Finally, set
c=2c_{h-1}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H]\eta^{h-1}
and
V=4k\cdot c_{h-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}[H]\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H]\eta^{2k-1}
.
This gives
V/c^{2}\geq\eta
(as
\mathbb{E}[H]\geq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H]
). By Equation (
90
) and a
union bound over all
(i,z)\in[R]\times V
, we get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[R]\\
z\in V\end{subarray}}Pr(c_{i,z}(\omega)>c)<\ Rn\cdot d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log{Rn})=d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-1)\log Rn).

Next, note that
V/c=2c_{h-1}h\cdot\mathbb{E}[H]\cdot\eta^{h}
. So an application of Equation
92
gives

Pr(C>V/c)\leq d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-2)\log Rn)

Finally, we use Theorem
14
which gives

\displaystyle Pr(|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]|\geq(V\eta)^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(-\eta/4)+(1+1/Rn)\cdot d_{h-1}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-1)\log Rn)

\displaystyle\leq d_{h}\exp(-\eta/4+(h-1)\log Rn)

Finally, just like done in Kim-Vu, since
(V\eta)^{1/2}=2c_{h-1}h^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}[H]\mathbb{E}^{\prime}[H])^{1/2}\eta^{h}
,
we finish up by choosing
c_{h}=2h^{1/2}c_{h-1}
.
∎
